THE CAP IS A PLAIN SEMAPHORE, first come first served, that every `Hash`, `Verify` and `Decoy` waits in on the REQUEST'S CONTEXT, so a waiter whose request went away leaves and nothing is derived for nobody — it was one queue with no bound and no context, so derivations ran for clients that had hung up; and it was then per-source turns, one slot per address served in turn, which bought a timing equalisation nothing asks for any more at the price of a scheduler of its own. `Rehash`, the one derivation nobody waits on, takes a slot only if one is free NOW and never one a freed slot hands to a waiter. The PHC string is what makes a cost raise possible: the plaintext is not stored, so the only instant a stronger digest can be computed is the one where somebody presents their password — and STALE MEANS WEAKER (below this build's cost in memory, passes or digest length and above it in none), never merely different, because a cost moves only with a build and so during a rolling upgrade, where a node on the older build read every verifier the newer one wrote as stale and rewrote it DOWN, flapping each person's verifier for the whole rollout. TWELVE CHARACTERS AND NO COMPOSITION RULES, with a blocklist of HUNDREDS rather than a published ten-thousand corpus — those are ranked by frequency and cluster at six to ten characters, so almost none is reachable behind the floor, and what is reachable is a few families caught by FOLDING a candidate (`password1234`, `P@ssw0rd1234` and `p4$$w0rd1234` are one entry). THE ENUMERATION ORACLE is what the file is shaped by and it takes two mechanisms: a throttle keyed on the subject AS TYPED from its source, decided before anything is looked up (keyed on what the subject RESOLVED to, its delay IS the roster), and a DECOY for a subject that does not exist that VERIFIES what was presented against ONE DUMMY VERIFIER at this build's cost — drawn, never derived, once per hasher and held as bytes — so a miss is a hit's work: one derivation at the same cost under the same cap and the same constant-time compare. It does NOT go back through `Verify`, which answers an unreadable verifier with a decoy: it did, and a hasher whose dummy did not decode recursed until the stack overflowed and took the process with it — and `NewHasher` panics on a cost argon2 cannot derive at (zero passes, lanes or digest), which `decode` refuses in a stored verifier for the same reason, since argon2 panics there rather than refusing. THE RESIDUAL IS STATED, NOT PADDED: the directory read before the derivation, and a real verifier written at another cost than this build's. Every refusal was once padded to one deadline measured from admission, and holding it under load took a turn per address at the cap and a decoy whose hold was DRAWN from the node's last sixteen derivations — a scheduler and a sampler to hide one indexed read. THE THROTTLE IS A CURVE AND NEVER A LOCKOUT: each failure doubles its key's next wait from 1 s to 30 s, a wait up to 5 s is served inside the request and a longer one is `429` with a `Retry-After`, because a refusal after N is a lockout anybody who can type a login can cause. ONE KEY for a password, the (typed subject, source) PAIR (IPv6 by its /64) with no allowance, and a success clears ITS OWN pair and NOTHING ELSE: it flushed the whole source, so anybody holding an account signed in as themselves between guesses at somebody else's and wiped every one. NO ADDRESS IS EVER REFUSED ON ITS OWN: there was a SOURCE curve too, ten free and then the same wait, and a refusal decided on an address is one anybody sharing it holds shut for everybody else — one stranger's failure every twenty-five seconds, at any name, kept a whole office or everybody behind an untrusted proxy at 429, right passwords never compared, and since a pending attempt counted against it, fourteen honest sign-ins in flight met the same 429. One password across many names from one address therefore meets no curve — the derivation every name costs under the verify cap, the floor and the blocklist bound it and the per-minute failure count shows it — and an attempt that NAMES NOBODY (an invitation or password link, whose secret is 256 bits nobody outside the engine can compute — a MAC under the company's key, or a `crypto/rand` draw) is ADMITTED UNCOUNTED, a nil `Ticket`, since there is nothing a curve would slow. A SECOND FACTOR ALSO CLIMBS THE PERSON'S OWN CURVE (`AdmitSecondFactor`), keyed on the RESOLVED person under a digest domain of its own, on each node, lifted by the code that completes the sign-in: on the pair alone somebody holding the password spread their guesses at six digits over every address they owned, a /48 finding the code in about an hour — and keying on the resolved person is safe only here, past the password, where it tells nobody who exists; `Ticket.Fail` reports the TRANSITION into the ceiling — the failure that takes the key there, not every failure while it is there — which the sign-in surface announces as `iam_second_factor_throttled`, since it means the password is known, and the curve's own state is the dedupe: a run held at the ceiling announces once, and a curve that has aged back down announces its next climb. An admitted attempt counts as a failure against its pair until its `Ticket` resolves (once: fail, succeed or release), so a burst at one account is served along the curve rather than at once — counted APART from the bounded memory of failures, because filed there at admission a burst of fresh names nobody verified, abandoned before the cap, evicted a climbing pair for the price of the requests alone, where forgetting one is meant to cost `MaxPairs` failures; EACH NODE KEEPS ITS OWN CURVE, and the residual is DECIDED rather than overlooked: the pairs' failures are a bounded LRU (`MaxPairs`) timed on this process's monotonic clock under a digest key it generated and never wrote, so a run a load balancer spreads over N nodes meets N curves and is admitted up to N times as often — bounded instead by what bounds it on one node, thirty seconds per attempt at the ceiling, an argon2id verification under the verify cap, the twelve-character floor, the blocklist and, past the password, the second factor. Sharing the curve through the coordination store was a round trip on the sign-in path and a subsystem of its own, bought for a factor of N on a rate the curve already cuts sixty-fold at its first step, and for nothing at all on the single node most deployments are. TOTP is hand-written over `crypto/hmac`, `crypto/sha1` and `encoding/base32` and certified against the RFC's own vectors, because forty lines of arithmetic against a module with its own idea of tolerable drift is the dependency rule working; the LAST ACCEPTED STEP is stored, or the drift tolerance is a ninety-second window in which one shoulder-surfed code works repeatedly. Every secret comparison is constant-time and every candidate loop runs to the end, asserted BY AST because no behavioural test can see the difference. A MACHINE TOKEN is `cwl_pat_<id>_<position>_<secret>` and whether one is good is a TABLE over one snapshot's values (`CheckToken`), THREE-VALUED on the position it carries in the clear: a node below the mint answers UNKNOWN (503) and one that covers it and holds no row answers REFUSED (401) — the two are otherwise one observation. The verifier covers the prefix, the id and the secret and deliberately NOT the position, which is a hint about when to look and can only ever buy a forger a 503. What a valid token carries is its mint's grants cut, PER REQUEST, to what its owner STILL holds, so a demotion reaches every token they made; and it is minted at its owner's revocation epoch AND the company's session generation and refused once either moves, because a restore brings a revoked token back exactly as it brings a revoked session back. A token held by its NAME rather than its secret (a view ticket minted through it) is decided by the same table less the two facts only a bearer has (`CheckHeld`): no secret to verify, and an absent row is unknown rather than refused, since no mint position says whether this node has applied it |
| `iam/session` | THE SIGNED COOKIE a browser holds, and the two THREE-VALUED TABLES that resolve it to a person and a seat. The seat table's SEATLESS arm serves a row bound to no seat under its own login, and who lands on it is a SERVICE ACCOUNT or a Tier A token's unbound machine row — never a PERSON, who holds a human seat for as long as they are here (ADR-0032), which the directory's writer enforces since the table carries no kind; every binding the directory writes names a human seat, so the GONE arm is a revision that took one away. The seam's `Chart` answers a node running no company `ErrNoCompany`, EXPORTED HERE so the directory's writer can name its remedy, while `ResolveSeat` reads it as the unknown arm like any other error, a 503, since another node can serve the request. SIGNATURE-STATELESS AND ROW-STATEFUL, because neither half works alone: a stateless cookie cannot be revoked before it expires, so off-boarding would be impossible, and an opaque id into a table costs a read for every forged cookie and has nothing to say until the row it names has been applied on the node the request reached. THERE IS NO VALIDATION CACHE ANYWHERE, stated in the package doc — the lookup is a local read of a replicated row and a map lookup on the running org, so a cache would be a second idea of who is signed in, with its own staleness, in front of a read that is already local. The rule the whole package is shaped by is 503 AND NEVER 401 ON A NODE THAT IS BEHIND: a browser reads 401 as "sign in again" and discards the cookie, so one stalled applier answering 401 logs everybody on that node out and sends them all back to the sign-in form at once — and the grace exists only for the arm a lagging node can honestly serve (reads of a session it has not yet seen), ending at `statelog.StallGrace`, so a node serving stale identity is by definition one already alarmed. What makes those three answers REACHABLE is the START POSITION in the bearer: an absent row on a node that covers it is a session that ended, and on a node below it is one that has not arrived, and the two are otherwise one observation with two opposite answers. ONE COOKIE NAME AUTHENTICATES — `__Host-crewlet_session` on https, the bare name on plain http, read as issued (`Presented`) — because the bare name is one a sibling host can plant, and read too it signed a visitor who held no session in as whoever's session the sibling planted; a sign-out still reaches both (`Held`), so a browser holding the name an http deployment issued before it moved to https has that session closed. THE COOKIE IS SET THREE WAYS AND NO OTHER — `Begin` (a sign-in, which may follow somebody else in that browser), `Continue` (the SAME person's session carried on: a re-issue, or a step-up's, an enrolment's or a password change's replacement) and `End` (a sign-out, a session the guard refused) — because each also decides what the browser's HTTP cache keeps: an answer kept by object id is keyed on no cookie, since the cookie's bytes move at every re-issue and a key holding them missed everything kept five minutes later, so `Begin` and `End` send `Clear-Site-Data: "cache"` and `Continue` sends nothing; the cookie and its deletions are unexported, so no caller sets one without that decision. THE KEYRING IS REQUIRED — a per-process random key is REFUSED by name, here and in `runtoken` alike, because every ingress node would derive a different key and a browser would be signed in on whichever node its request reached — and the key TAG is `runtoken`'s own, so a rotation is add, restart, flip, restart, drop, and dropping a key early is what ends live sessions. A RE-ISSUE WRITES NOTHING — the idle deadline it moves is inside the signed bearer — and THERE IS NO ROTATION INDEX, because a cookie carries nothing that tells its owner's copy from a replayed one: the v2 bearer's index, the session's age in windows, served a LAGGING index as the idle session it is indistinguishable from, and its one arm that acted — an index AHEAD of the clock past a two-minute overlap — was inside the signed payload and so written only by a node holding the keyring, from its own clock, which made it a detector of clock skew that bumped the person's revocation epoch and signed them out of every session and machine token they held. So the bearer is v3 with no index and no `session.rotate_after`, and what bounds a captured cookie is the idle and absolute deadlines and the two counters. A bearer carries a SCOPE only when its session may do less than everything (`enrol`, one that may only enrol a second factor) — signed, re-issued with it, and read with the row's copy through `Validation.EnrolmentOnly` — and a whole session's bearer is byte for byte the nine fields every build reads, so a rolling upgrade keeps every ordinary session while a build that knows no scope refuses a scoped one as malformed rather than serving it whole. A session exchanged from a Tier A token carries NOTHING OF THE TOKEN: its person is the token's login, which the guard's token table answers by the entry's ID — a gone entry is a subject that may not act, on a node that has not applied the session's row too — so a new value under the same id leaves the sessions the old one opened working until their hour ends, and an operator who must cut them off at once rotates by giving the token a new id. A MAC over the value once rode in the cookie to end them on a rotation as well, and went: a second credential check on every request, a key derivation and an ending of its own, to shorten an hour an id change already shortens to nothing. A WRITE on a node below the bearer's start position WAITS for it rather than answering 503 — at most `auth.SessionCatchUp`, the publisher's own resolve budget, over the identity reader's `AwaitApplied` — because a sign-in answers before any node applies the session it opened (`NoWait`), so the request that reads the bearer next owes its position the wait the sign-in skipped: refused outright, every client that signs in and then acts met a 503 on every node, and `crewlet iam token -login` failed on every real run. A DEADLINE IS DECIDED BEFORE ANY ROW IS READ, so an expired cookie is refused even by a node that cannot read its estate — and nothing announces it: an expiry nobody authored is no row, where announcing it once per session took a remembered set the size of every session a node had noticed and a second read of the rows to tell it from an ending a record had already announced. An OPEN CONNECTION's bearer is decided with its idle deadline set aside (`Signer.ValidateOpen`), because a socket can never be re-issued a cookie and an open live view is activity — its rows and its absolute deadline still end it, and nothing is re-issued. `session.Idle` is a CONSTANT and not config, because it rides in every bearer and two nodes disagreeing about it would hand one browser two expiries. A session held by its NAME (a view ticket's lineage, on the artifact origin where no cookie travels) lands on the same rows through `StandingByName`, the session row's own epoch standing in for the bearer's; the generation and the absolute deadline are the caller's to have judged first |
| `iam/authevents` | WHERE THIS NODE'S AUTHENTICATION FACTS BECOME EVENTS — the one trail the sign-in surface, the request guard, the identity directory, the identity writer and every state-log applier hand what they saw to, built ONCE PER PROCESS by the engine because its per-minute count is the node's: a failed sign-in and a refused bearer are counted together only because both reach the same trail. TWO DOORS and they are the whole design: a fact a VERIFIED credential or the engine authored is published as it happens (`Emit`), while a FAILED ATTEMPT publishes NOTHING — it bumps `crewlet.auth.attempts.failed` (by method and outcome) and one on its SOURCE's count for the minute — the throttle's own unit (`credential.SourceKey`: an address, an IPv6 client's /64), folded by the trail rather than by any surface, because counted per raw address one host cycling addresses inside its /64 took every named place in a minute's first milliseconds and pushed every other caller into the overflow — and the engine's own loop publishes ONE `iam_login_failures` per node once the minute has CLOSED, carrying each source's count, because internal/events admits no type whose rate an unauthenticated caller authors and a row per attempt was 6.9 million a day from one host. A FAILURE CARRIES NOTHING ABOUT WHO — `Failure` is a source, a method and whether the throttle turned it away, and a test holds the shapes of it and of the row: the presented value is where a password typed into the login box lands, an unsalted hash of it reverses against the company's own roster in one pass, and the tally this replaced kept keyed digests to count distinct names and the ids of the people failures resolved to, which made a row a list of who was being guessed at. The one size an attacker would otherwise choose is `MaxSourcesPerMinute` (64, with its reason), past which every further source's failures fold into one overflow count, so the row grows with nothing an attacker's address pool decides. NO FACT IS COALESCED PER WINDOW: there was a third door, a once-per-window dedupe keeping a bounded set per class of fact, and each fact went the way that needs no remembered set — a Tier A token's use is on every record it writes, under its name; its overreach is a WARN log line in the guard; a session past its own deadline is an expiry nobody authored, and no row; and a person's second factor reaching its ceiling is announced by the failure the curve itself reports as taking it there. Publishes are DETACHED from the request and bounded, because the gesture already happened and a client hanging up must not erase its row; the loop's final flush on stop is what keeps the minute a restart interrupted |
| `usage` | What each node's seats, schedules and people did each company day — and who read the repositories a unit keeps to itself — replicated: `statelog`'s FIFTH domain and its second COMPACTED one (ADR-0020). Spend, turn and read history used to be a query over `crewlet_events`, the NODE estate's audit log, so every answer described whichever node served it, a ninety-day chart was drawn over the thirty days that log keeps, and a node that left took its share of the company's history with it. THE NODE IS PART OF THE SUBJECT: each data node derives only its own day from its own event log (`store.DB.UsageForDay`) and publishes one record per (node, day, seat), per (node, day, schedule), per (node, day, person) and per (node, day, unit repository read) — a PERSON being a human seat the auxiliary model spent for (a question answered on the operator surface, a background pass on a unit a person leads), filed under that seat's HANDLE as the principal's own seat names it (`iam.RecordOwner`) and never an identity-directory person id, a principal bound to no seat charging the company alone — WHOLE, every time it moves — so every object has exactly one writer, nothing arbitrates, nothing merges at write time, and a reader sums across nodes. A seat is its AGENT ID and a schedule its scope's identity, never a name, because a name is prose two seats may share; a seat's handle rides along only as a label for a seat the company no longer holds. An apply REPLACES under a monotone position guard, which is what makes a republished day harmless and a replayed older one a no-op. NOT A DUTY: every DATA node runs one publisher, for its own days, armed in the engine's CORE whatever its company keeps — a node without `data` keeps no event log, so its events reach a data node through CUSTODY and its spend is counted in that node's day — re-deriving today and yesterday every 15 s when a cheap fingerprint moves, because a turn that ended a second before midnight is written a moment after it. A DAY IS THE ROWS ITS NODE KEEPS, never a custody batch it has written and not settled (`store`'s kept-rows predicate, a seek of the in-flight identities' key from every row counted, on every usage count), because until the claim settles two data nodes can hold one batch and a reader summing the fleet would bill it twice. A ROW CHANGES ONLY THE DAY IT FALLS ON: a turn's figures — its reviews, send-backs and every segment's duration — are read off the ONE record that ends it, which states the whole turn (`types.TurnTally`, counted as the turn runs and carried across a park), because a stateless node's records reach the data nodes a custody batch at a time and a resumed turn leaves its first segment behind, so summed per node from what each held they were short on one node and counted on none — and a turn whose end states NO tally (ended by a build before it, or resumed from a park such a build made, whose earlier segments nobody measured) is read off its records by its id as it always was, never as zeros, which would publish every pre-upgrade turn as zero-length and unreviewed over the history the old build wrote. So a batch settled as KEPT moves the fingerprint of today or yesterday, and an older day through the mark the settle leaves in its own transaction (`usage_rederive`, a row per UTC day, cleared up to the newest seq the publisher read), and the publisher derives every such day again — NEVER one that starts before `store.EventHistory`, the event log's read floor, whose rows may be swept and whose short derivation would replace the whole day on every node with less, and a day older than the dispatch ledger's retention without its schedules. A day derived again keeps the instants it was FIRST CUT on (`usage_day_windows`), so a moved company clock never moves rows between days already published — a day cut for the first time abuts its adjacent labels' cuts and is clamped by any farther one it would reach into, so the day the clock moves on is longer or shorter instead, and an instant on a label whose old cut misses it belongs to the label beside it — and NO DAY, today and yesterday included, IS EVER PUBLISHED BELOW WHAT THE FLEET HOLDS: inside the floor a day only grows, so a smaller derivation proves the node's own log lost rows (a store restored beside a later usage log, one started empty, a parked turn's earliest records swept), and what goes out is the derivation JOINED with the fleet's record (`usage.Join`), every figure at the larger of the two — the derivation itself on an ordinary day, and on a short one the fleet's figure where it is larger while every other keeps moving, where holding the short derivation back whole froze the day — logged once (`usage_rederive_short`), and no day is derived until the node's usage applier has replayed the log to its end, so what it is joined with is the fleet's rows rather than a restore's. What the publisher itself still owes — the day before the newest it fingerprinted, or an older one a tick could not settle — is a WATERMARK in the node's store (`usage_watermark`), which the next start derives every day from again, so the tail of the day a node stopped in reaches the fleet when it comes back within the read floor; an ORDERLY stop also flushes today and yesterday once more, after every writer of a usage row has stopped (the custody keeper leaving its group among them), on a budget of the state log's own resolve budget, so a node stopped and never started again leaves its last day whole — a crash it never comes back from loses up to a tick. An unresolved publish is sent again rather than counted — while a refusal no retry changes (a record too large, a full log) is logged ONCE per object and skipped until that object next changes, never re-sent every tick. Turn durations are a mergeable 2^(1/6) HISTOGRAM (±6% quantiles) rather than a list or a stored percentile, because a week is seven days merged and a fleet three nodes merged. The 181-day HORIZON is the APPLIER's — a record for day D deletes rows older than D − 181 in its own transaction, a pure function of the record — because a sweep would be a second writer of the replicated estate. A person's record is written at the base version and published at once, like a seat's and a schedule's. A SEAT'S CODE DAY (`Record.Code`, record version 3 — 4 where it counts failed checks or ejections, `Record.Code.ChecksFailed` and `Record.Code.Ejections` — carried only where it counts something, so a seat's day with none stays the base format every build applies and one with them is RETAINED by an older build rather than applied without them): the changes the seat opened, the reviews it gave, the findings the reviews of its own changes raised, the checks that failed its changes' heads and its changes' ejections from their queues — the last three counted ON THE OWNER — off the `change_opened`, `review_submitted`, `code_check_finished` and `code_queue_ejected` events the node that landed each record announced, which name each seat by the agent id resolved when they were published (a person is counted on no seat), each change and review once by its id, each failed check by its attempt and each ejection by its entry, on the seat's head row (replicated 0093, 0097) and joined by max like every counter; the seat activity answer sums them. The FOURTH kind, `repo_read` (record version 2, which an older build RETAINS under the identity its writer stated rather than applying it without a home for its readers): one record per (node, day, `visibility: unit` repository) naming every reader — a credential and the human seat it acted as, or a seat, each by how it read — at most the 1 024 most-read (`ReadersPerRepoDay`) with the rest counted, published joined with the fleet's record like every kind, and read across every node a page at a time in reader order (`RepoReadsPage`: one principal kind per statement, each SEEKING the principal its keyset resumes at, since a statement over the whole repository read every reader before the page's first again on every page) beside the count the cap dropped (`RepoReadsElided`). |
| `code` | THE CODE DOMAIN: the company's git repositories as records on one ordered log, `CREWLET_CODE_LOG` — `statelog`'s EIGHTH domain, its sixth STRICT one and its sixth that claims identity. BESIDE ITS REPOSITORIES THE DOMAIN HOLDS THEIR CHANGES, WHAT IS SAID ABOUT THEM AND WHOM THEY WAKE, each told in full below and mapped here first: a change (`web!42`) is rows on its repository's subject, its lifecycle decided by the repository's committer beside the pushes that move its branches; its conversation — asks for review, reviews with the findings they raise, comments and threads — and what the repository's home git node reads of each revision (its facts, its mergeability and the readiness judged beside it) are records on the change's own subject, published only by the repository's committing data node through its `ChangeWriter`; and every record about a change carries the routing snapshot its decide computed (`routingFor`, one function for every writer), from which the log's own consumer (`FeedGroup`, `crewlet-code-feed`) wakes the seats it concerns and the applier writes each recipient a `code_notifications` row, which a person's inbox reads — a person is never woken. A repository IS ROWS — its settings, protection rules, path owners, refs and pack list, written only by the applier — and its BYTES are not: every push stores ONE object, its pack BUNDLE (the pack, then the `.idx` version 2 and the `.rev` version 1 the receiving `receive-pack` wrote, back to back), named by a `code_packs` row declared `Required` — one PUT a push and one GET a push on every git node, with no `index-pack` anywhere but the receiver's, because a cold sync from S3 is bound by how many GETs it makes and re-indexing what it downloaded would make it bound by CPU on every node. The format stays EXACT by a RULE: NO STORED PACK REACHES 2 GiB — a push is held to `MaxPackBytes` (five quarters of the 1 GiB push ceiling, pinned below `1<<31`) and whatever later rewrites stored packs caps them with `pack-objects --max-pack-size` — so an index never grows its 64-bit offset table and `CheckBundle` compares section sizes computed from the object count, the ONE rule the receiver and every cache hold a bundle to (`SealWriter` gathers the seal as the bytes stream past, keeping a few dozen of them), and its row-level half (`PackRef.Check`) is what a proposal and a cache's snapshot are held to before any byte moves, so a row the domain admitted is never one a git node refuses. A BUNDLE IS LAID OUT AND STORED IN ONE PLACE (`bundlestore.go`, ADR-0008): `LayBundle` makes the row's `PackRef` from the three files a git wrote (each section's size from its file, the object count from the index's fan-out through `OpenIdx`, the one reading of an index's header) and `StoreBundle` uploads them back to back through the `Putter` it is handed, under `UploadDeadline`, sealing the bytes on their way and answering a seal its rule refuses as `*BadBundle` — the one store every writer of a stored pack takes, the receiver's landing among them, so none lays a bundle out or seals it by a reading of its own. The id a compaction's output is laid out under is ONE derivation too (`DeriveOutputPackID`: an unnamed `statelog.DeriveOpID` over the compaction's id and the output's place, at the instant that id carries) — derived rather than minted, as a push's pack carries the push's own id, so the build that names an output and the check of the proposal carrying it agree with nothing to look up. THE SUBJECT IS THE ARBITRATION UNIT: everything that moves or governs a repository's refs or packs is a record on `repo.<id>`, ONE SUBJECT A REPOSITORY because every write updates its row anyway, so a per-ref subject would never narrow a write's contention; a NAME is claimed on `name.<token>` — a bounded digest the applier recomputes from the payload, refusing a record that arbitrated one name and claims another — so two creates of one name contend. Pushes are GROUP-COMMITTED: a `Committer` takes one repository's pushes in order on one goroutine and puts everything already queued behind a record in flight into the next — WITHOUT LINGER, up to `MaxPushesPerRecord` (64), `MaxRecordBytes` and `MaxRecordRows` — decides the batch in ONE snapshot with two reads for the whole of it (the push ids, the refs) and its protection rules PARSED and every command's protection VERDICT formed ONCE A BATCH and BEFORE ITS PUBLISH, on the committer goroutine from a read of the rules of its own — CPU alone, so a round holds its connection of the reader pool for the reads alone — kept across the publish's compare-and-set rounds for as long as each round's snapshot holds the same rules, compared whole, and formed again inside a round whose rules differ (`Protections`, which `ProtectionRefusal` asks, answering the rule a refusal names from its one walk — asked in gitref's one-off form, the round re-read every pattern for every command, 83–87 ms against 14–16 ms at three thousand commands and sixty-four rules; and at the costliest rules a repository can carry the formation is 1.8–2.0 s, sixteen of which were the whole `CommitBudget` when every round formed them inside its snapshot, and four such batches at once held every reader of the node's pool, where a round is 13–22 ms), each push against what the ones before it did, and answers an `ok` ONLY FROM THE PUSH ROW its record applied, never from the decision; a push id the history already holds is answered from its row, so a forwarded or failed-over push is one push. The batch runs under the committer's own lifetime, never a caller's, bounded by `CommitBudget` (30 s, half the router's write attempt); a queue holds `CommitterInbox` (1 024) pushes and `CommitterInboxBytes` (sixteen records) and past either answers `busy` at once, and so does the NODE past `CommitterNodeBytes` (sixty-four records) held across every repository from each push's queueing to its caller's answer — a node commits for every repository rendezvous ranks it first for, and the estate's proposal lane is as wide as one repository's whole queue, so per-repository bounds alone let a thousand of the largest pushes (up to `EntryRoom`, a mebibyte of entry, each) from as many repositories hold about a gibibyte — and it is bounded in TIME as well — by the CALLER'S OWN DEADLINE, which a routed proposal carries from the asker's attempt: a push starts a batch only while a `CommitBudget` and `ReplyMargin` (1 s, the answer's trip and the clocks' skew) are left before it, and is answered `busy` undecided the moment they are not, watched while it waits on the caller's own goroutine and judged again as its batch forms, a push being either TAKEN or WITHDRAWN and never both — never by a wait counted from its arrival, which forgot what the asker spent before the push arrived (the trip, the serving node's floor wait) and answered up to that much after the asker had gone; one asked in-process with no deadline may wait less than a `CommitBudget`, and one whose caller has gone is never decided, so every push a committer decides is answered inside the one attempt its asker gives it, never after the asker has handed the same push id to the runner-up; a batch is DECIDED AT OR PAST WHAT ITS PUSHES WERE CHECKED AGAINST — published with the highest `Proposal.Seen` (the receiving node's own sync position) as its session, a per-batch floor and never one the node keeps — because the committer is usually another node and may be behind the receiver, and decided from below it a push is told a false `stale` or `repo_deleted` that no compare-and-set catches (an empty decision is never put to the broker), while a committer that cannot reach it in the publisher's budget is refused `behind`, a refusal about this node; and a refusal about THIS node — made before anything was appended, or an eviction found at ANOTHER operation's record when a batch's answer was lost (`statelog.Unavailable.Dropped` false: the record there may be a healthy peer's, and the batch's own may never have landed) — is `ErrNotServing`, so the router asks the runner-up with the same push id, while a gate that dropped the batch's OWN record is `not_applied` naming it. Every BUSY refusal carries its REASON on `crewlet.code.commit.busy` beside its kind (busy.go): `queue`, `node` and `waited`, a BACKLOG — the one busy that says this committer turns proposals away, at its queue's count or bytes, at the node's bound, or at its queue's TIME bound (`waited`: a proposal that queued with more of its asker's attempt left than a batch and its answer and lost it waiting behind the repository's earlier ones, withdrawn as it waits or refused as its batch forms; a committer slower than its pushes holds fewer than `CommitterInbox` of them by the time the oldest runs out, so its count alone would never say so) — which the committer keeps by repository in fifteen one-minute buckets expired by their minute (`CommitBusyWindow`, which IS `statelog.TrimInterval`, borrowed under ADR-0015 rather than held equal by a test) and hands the engine as `Committer.AlarmReading` (a `BusyReading`: the count, the repository with the most and its count, how many met the node's bound and how many waited out their time), so `code_commit_busy` stands on the data node that decides the repository's pushes; `late` (a proposal that ARRIVED with no more of its asker's attempt than a batch and its answer, the attempt spent upstream by the nodes it crossed) and `contended` (every compare-and-set round lost, a second committer during a handoff) are counted and logged, never alarmed; and a repository's refusals of every reason are logged once a minute (`code_commit_busy`), once the minute has closed — at the committer's next refusal or the next reading — never once a refusal. The reconcile's ORDER BETWEEN ACTIVATIONS is the ACTIVATION FENCE: `Store.Activate` publishes an `Activated` record on the company subject FIRST, its apply raises the replicated `code_activation` row to that activation and never lowers it, and the applier refuses — applies as nothing, identically on every node — every config-managed create, settings record and lifecycle record below the fence at its position. Every decide of an activation reads a snapshot holding its own fence, so an older activation's record is either EARLIER in the log (and corrected from the rows) or LATER (and refused), and an activation that changes nothing writes ONE record rather than one per declared repository. A git node's bare repository is never the record, only a disposable cache of these rows. A repository's PATH OWNERS (`RepoSettings.PathOwners`, `code_path_owners`) are settings like the rest, carried whole by every create, settings and restore record and compared by the reconcile's `equal` — ONE ROW AN OWNER, its globs packed one a line, because a row a glob made a settings record replacing a repository's 2 048 of them touch 4 096 rows, past `MaxRecordRows`, where a row an owner is 64 and the final purge takes them beside the rules; and AT MOST `MaxPathOwnersBytes` (config's 256 KiB) IN ALL as a record encodes them (`pathOwnersBytes`, computed and held to `json.Marshal` by the row-size test), because every record this build forms is held to `MaxRecordBytes` and the counts alone let globs JSON spells long weigh 1.6 MB, a record of 2.2 MB beside the rest of the widest settings, where the bound leaves the widest create, settings change or restore at 894 KB, some 150 KB under it (`TestTheLargestSettingsFitTheirRecordAndTheDetail`, exactly); read beside the row in a lifecycle decide, and on the repository's detail (`RepoDetail.PathOwners`) only for a read that ASKS (`ReadQuery.PathOwners`, refused on every other kind: the API's question of one repository asks, and no resolution of a repository does — a seat tool's once a turn, a refs listing's first page, the status and readers questions — since none reads them and at their bound each would carry a quarter of a mebibyte to drop), NEVER on the `RepoRef` every read, push and fetch decides from, so a path owner, whom a change touching its paths asks for a review, grants nothing; a tool's settings change adds and removes globs one owner at a time (`PathOwnersChange`), resolved against the rows its decide reads, and an owner it gives globs to must be one the deciding chart holds. A branch rule's MERGE GATE (`Protection.Gate`, a `*MergeGate`: `merge_by`, `approvals`, `approvals_unless`, `human_paths` of `{glob, unless}`, `checks`, `landing` `{depth, batch}`, `owners_approve`, `delete_source`, `agents_edit_checks`) rides its rule WHOLE, one column of the record's own JSON (`code_protections.gate`), because nothing filters on a field and whatever judges a merge reads each gate whole; a record carries every field explicitly in the one order `CanonicalGate` forms, held by `RepoSettings.Validate` to its closed sets, to `MaxHumanPaths` and `MaxRequiredChecks` (32 each, config's), to `MaxGateBytes` of JSON (8 KiB, so 64 rules' gates are 512 KiB of the settings), to path classes alone in an `unless` (an approval already carries across `no_code_change`) and to no gate on a tag rule. RULES ARE COMPARED BY VALUE (`Protection.Equal`), never `slices.Equal`: a gate is a pointer, and by `==` every reconcile of an unchanged gated declaration would read two reads of one rule as two rules. NO ROW OF A REPOSITORY CARRIES A GATE: `RepoRef.Protections` are `PushRule`s — each rule as a push is judged by it, typed apart from `Protection` (which embeds one beside its gate) so no consumer takes a gate nobody read for none — read without the gate's column (`pushRulesIn`, `pushRulesOfSQL`) by every read of the row: the admission of every git request (`Resolve`), every page of a git node's sync (`Snapshot`, whose row the cache's state file keeps and rewrites at every push), a resolution of refs, a listing and the push decide's rounds, none of which reads a gate and each of which would otherwise decode, ship or write half a mebibyte of them at the bound (`TestNoRowOfARepositoryCarriesAMergeGate` holds the types); one repository's detail answers its rules' gates (`RepoDetail.Gates`, each a `RuleGate`) only to a read that asks (`ReadQuery.Gates`: a `repo` read or a refs listing's first page — the API's question of a repository and code_get_repo), and a decide that rewrites the settings reads the rules whole beside the row (`protectionsIn`), as it reads the path owners: the lifecycle decides, and the reconcile through `protectionsOfSQL`. A protect request's answer names its pattern's rule whole beside the row (`LifecycleResult.Rule`: read back by the table's key, `ruleSQL`, or as the decide held it where the request needed no record). WHO MAY DO WHAT TO A REPOSITORY IS DERIVED FROM THE CHART, NEVER STORED (ADR-0058): `GrantOf(principal, repo, roster)` is the one function every judge of it calls — a person, who always holds a human seat, maintains everywhere and a credential bound to no seat reads, both without reading the roster, and a seat holds the minimum of its tier's cap (`role.code.access`, `Tier.Cap`: unset reads) and its RELATION on the chart (the owning unit's effective lead maintains, its subtree writes, the lead chain above it reads, the owning seat maintains and its unit's leads read, any other seat reads a `visibility: company` repository and NOTHING of a `visibility: unit` one — `Visibility`, a setting like any other carried whole by every create, settings and restore record, and the last row of the relation and nowhere else, so it narrows SEATS ONLY and no person's standing reads it), every unit a durable REFERENCE the `Roster` resolves as `org.Index.UnitByRef` does, since the chart reaches this package only as that interface — the engine's `codeRoster`, built once an epoch at its first question — and EVERY READ A PRINCIPAL MAKES NAMES ITS ASKER (`ReadQuery.Asker`, refused absent; the code questions (`api/queries`) ask as the request's principal, bound to a seat or not, and `codetest.Reader` is the suite's), judged on the serving node by `ReaderOptions.Roster` read once a read: a listing leaves out what the asker may not read and still pages by the rows it SCANNED, so a page may be short or empty with a cursor, a detail or a history the asker may not read is `ErrNotPermitted` after the row is read while a name never declared is still `ErrRepoNotFound`, and a reader holding NO CHART (a node before its first epoch is installed) refuses a seat's read ABOUT ITSELF, `ErrNotServing` before any row is read, so the estate's walk asks a peer that holds one rather than taking a definite "not permitted" no chart decided; A SEAT'S READ MADE FOR A COLLEAGUE — inside a turn that colleague woke, the requester ceiling's case — NAMES THAT SEAT TOO (`ReadQuery.CeilingSeat`, and its siblings on `RefQuery` and `GitQuery`: beside a seat's asker only, refused beside any other and on the engine's own reads) and is judged with the asker by ONE function (`JudgeRead`, every reader's and the git node's), from the one chart and row: the asker first, refused `ErrNotPermitted` about itself, then the colleague, `ErrAboveCeiling` — never `ErrNotPermitted`, which would be false of an asker that reads the repository — so a listing leaves out what either may not read; a seat's WRITE carries the same requester as a `WriteCeiling` (a colleague seat, or NOBODY: a stranger or a person who may not act), judged by the node that decides it — an engine write's git node through `Grant.Under`, a lifecycle request's data node by asking the request again as the colleague (`ceilingLifecycleRefusal`), a change's proposal and conversation write's committing data node at the least ACCESS the write needs and never the relation beyond it (`WriteCeiling.changeRefusal`, `CeilingNeeds`: review for a review, read for every other), so an author may ask a seat to review its change — sparing the seat's OWN WORK as `authz.DecideWithin` spares a steerable verb on what an agent owns (`Grant.OwnWork`: its namespace and a repository its seat owns; `steeredOwnWork`: its scratch create and the settings and protections of a repository it owns that hand it to nobody; `ownChange`: a change it owns, and an open of its own namespace), never what discloses, hands on or retires one; an estate request's field and no record's, which a build that predates it would ignore, so it holds while none serving the code host does (no `main` build carries this package) — and `Grant.RefRule` judges one command against it before any protection (`not_permitted`, its sentence naming what the principal holds, what the command needs and a remedy the rule itself admits, and no ref, since a proposal's Detail is bounded) — and THE PUSH DECIDE HOLDS A SEAT'S PUSH TO IT (decide rule 2b, every command before anything about its ref is read, `not_permitted` RECORDED like every verdict on a command) from the DECIDING committer's chart and calendar (`CommitterOptions.SeatRules`, REQUIRED, read once a batch that carries a seat's push), beside three rules a person's push never meets: `direct_push: persons` takes a PERSON's push alone (`ProtectionRefusal(c, rules, by)`, so every protection rule refuses a seat's direct push `protected_direct`, its unit's lead included, and `none` refuses everybody); a seat OUTSIDE the owning unit (its relation below write, so it pushes only into its own namespace there) fills at most HALF of the repository's live refs and of `repo_max_bytes` (`OutsideSeatShare`), so its owners always keep half; and a seat lands at most `Limits.PushesPerSeatDay` pushes a company day, every repository together, COUNTED BY THE PUSH APPLY in `code_seat_days` (replicated 0063: a row a seat and day, read once a round by its key for every seat the batch names, never a count over the push history) on the day the committer stamps on every seat's entry (`PushEntry.Day`, base format under the condition its doc states), a seat's oldest day beyond its two newest forgotten as a newer one is counted — the day before the newest kept for a committer whose clock has not reached the company's midnight yet, judged from the rows rather than the calendar — so the count is a function of the log alike on every node — past it the WHOLE push is refused `push_quota` UNRECORDED, no entry and the batch's other entries going on, because a recorded refusal would cost the log the record a runaway's push the quota exists to bound; the overshoot across repositories, whose committers each count what their own node has applied, is accepted and stated (a seat's pushes in flight at one instant less one, never cumulative while no two committers' clocks stand a day apart). A committer that cannot judge a seat decides none of its pushes and says so about ITSELF (`ErrNotServing`, so the router asks the repository's runner-up under the same push id) — one holding no chart yet, and one CONFIRMED behind the company's configuration (`CommitterOptions.Current`, judged as a push is queued and again as its batch forms) — and decides a person's as ever. A full_access seat owns `SeatNamespace` — `refs/heads/seats/<handle>/`, NEVER the handle alone, because a handle is a role name's slug and people name branches under `docs/` and `release/`, so a namespace there handed a seat that only reads every person's such branch — and `refs/heads/seats` and `refs/heads/seats/<name>` as a branch are `reserved_ref` for every principal (`StatelessRefusal`) and no repository's default branch — gitref's `SeatsRoot` and `ReservedSeatBranch`, the one spelling the decide and the configuration read — since under `ref_conflict` a branch `seats` would block every seat's namespace and `seats/<x>` that seat's. `SeatBranch` is the branch a seat's coding run works on when its call names none, inside that namespace: the WORK ITEM's key spelled for a ref (never the turn's dedupe key, which differs for two turns on one item), so every run on an item continues one branch, a key the spelling changed taking a dash and 12 hex digits of its own digest so two keys that spell alike are two branches, or `run-` and the turn id's random tail for a turn on no item — always inside a ref's 255 bytes, since a handle is at most 64. NO REF IS CREATED BESIDE A DIRECTORY OF ITS OWN NAME (`ref_conflict`, decided in the batch's one snapshot: a create is refused where a live ref is a directory it would make or lies under its name, judged against the working state, so the push's own deletions free a name, an earlier create of the batch takes one and a tombstone is in nobody's way), since no clone can hold a file and a directory of one name and every existing clone's fetch failed once a repository took both; a batch that creates reads two statements more for it — a primary-key seek for every directory its creates would make (and the upper bound of a create under another), and one seek of the LIVE REFS' partial index (`code_refs_live_idx`, replicated 0054) for every gap the creates' ranges make once cut at their bounds and at every live name — so a round's reads follow what the batch spells, never what the repository holds: tombstones under a create cost it nothing (118 nested creates over `MaxTombstonesPerRepo` of them, 7–15 ms a round, where a probe over the key stepped over every one, once a create, 7.7–9.9 s), nesting adds a cut rather than a gap a create, and the worst a record carries is 2 573 creates of the deepest refs, some 303 000 key seeks, 1.2–1.4 s a round — while the index costs the push apply about 5 µs a live ref it writes (20 ms on the record of the most creations). The repository's row COUNTS what its refs hold — live refs, the branches and tags among them, and tombstones — moved by the applier as it moves the refs, so no read walks a namespace to count it. Four horizons are the APPLIER's, each chosen by the record's broker instant and its own repository's rows and never by a sweep: push history 181 days (`usage.History`), read oldest POSITION first and stopping at the first push inside the horizon, so a push stamped earlier than the one before it (a leader change) waits for that one and every node forgets the same rows, ref tombstones 48 h, or sooner, oldest first, past `MaxTombstonesPerRepo` (100 000) — and a record whose deletions its sweep cannot make room for removes their rows and raises the mark to its own position, which no record the committer forms needs, since its row bound counts every deletion three times (its ref, the old tombstone the sweep may take for it, and the object it left) — so a deletion never counts against the live refs' `MaxRefsPerRepo` and never refuses a push, not even against the creates of the push that makes it (a tombstone is how a delta sync learns of a deletion; past the swept mark a snapshot is full, and so is a delta that would carry more rows than the full answer, so a cache behind a clean-up larger than what the repository keeps reads what is left), swept by every push record into whatever its own rows leave of the framework's row budget, because one record can make over a thousand and a fixed share fell behind any repository that deletes refs in bulk, REF DROPS 14 days (`PruneGrace`, git's own `gc.pruneExpire`) — a `code_ref_drops` row (replicated 0059) for the object every forced update or deletion moved its ref off, which no ref names any more and which dropping a repository's unreachable objects must keep for the grace, keyed by its record's instant first (replicated 0061: the one order the expiry, a purge and its bounded count read, so the table has no index beside its key) and by the object as well since one record's entries may move one ref off several, written in the record's order and counted in the committer's row bound (`commandRows`: a forced update two rows, a deletion three, any other one, the one count `rowBound` and `withinRecord` take), and forgotten past the grace by every push record of its repository with whatever its rows leave once the tombstones have had theirs — after the sweep, whose room a deletion's tombstone needs, and no fixed share, which falls behind one push of a thousand forced updates; that keeps pace with one such push a record and with deletions whose tombstones are not yet due, falls behind a record the committer fills with forced updates or with deletions whose tombstones come due (some 1 900 drops a record at most), and leaves what it falls behind by to the repository's later records with room, up to `MaxRecordRows`-4 a record, so the drops past the grace grow while its records stay full and drain once they are not — and a purge in bounded parts whose last record leaves a deletion marker dropping every later record about the repository. A purge has to be STARTED, since nothing else writes to a deleted repository: `Retention.Pass` — the body of the `code-retention` duty, whose lease and tick are the engine's, and which the engine runs only on a node that SERVES THE REVISION THE FLEET'S ACTIVATION POINTER NAMES (`Engine.servedFleetRevision`, the one helper the mailbox roster reads too, nothing activated counting as no), declining and giving the duty back anywhere else — asked before each claim and each renewal, and logged `code_retention_duty_declined` with both epochs, at WARN only where the lag is CONFIRMED by the posture's own rule (`configplane.FleetView.LagConfirmed`: a failed apply, or behind past `LagGrace`) or the node cannot tell, and at DEBUG, asked again a reconcile interval later rather than a tick, on a node merely applying an activation or still starting (the duty's first turn runs before `crewlet run` has built the reconciler, read as one that has not ticked, as the posture reads it) — because the pass purges by its own node's `code.deleted_retention_days` and a holder that failed to apply a revision lengthening it would purge by the retention the company replaced, with no undo — steps every repository PURGING first, WHATEVER THE RETENTION (a purge once begun holds its name and has no restore, so a longer retention saves only a repository still merely deleted), then every one deleted past `code.deleted_retention_days`, oldest deletion first, each until its purge is done, publishing records and never touching a row, and reading the listing a page at a time — ids and names alone — over a partial index per status (replicated 0052). It PACES ITSELF, because every data node applies every step on its one write lane and the holder sees only its own: back to back a pass held the lane over 80% of the time and a node 1.2 times slower queued behind it, so a pass waits twice a step's duration after each (`RetentionLaneShare`, a third — a node up to three times slower keeps up with no queue) and takes steps for `RetentionPassBudget` (`statelog.StallGrace`, a minute — one up to six times slower ends a pass at most StallGrace behind), stopping there with `Remain` for the next tick — one budget and one pacer over its sweeps of retired packs, taken first, and its purges, which have the rest: a sweep step of a few rows is a seventeenth of a purge part on the whole path (`BenchmarkRetireSweepStep`), so the sweeps take under a twentieth of a day's steps at the code host's design point and the purges drain some 22 000 parts a day. A repository whose step fails is reported and passed over, one restored (or restored and deleted again) since the listing's stale read is skipped rather than failed, and a purged name goes to `OnPurged` ONLY ONCE THIS NODE'S ROWS HOLD THE PURGE — a last record that resolved pending is read again at its position — so the re-declaration it drives never meets the repository still purging; a name it cannot hand on yet (not applied in a read's budget, refused by `OnPurged`, or the context ended first) is HELD by the `Retention` value, reported `Unhanded`, and handed on by the next pass. A COMPACTION — the merge of some of a repository's packs into fewer by its HOME git node (`code/compact`) — is a record of its own on `repo.<id>` (`OpCompact`, base format under the condition its doc states, `Compaction`), proposed through `Committer.ProposeCompaction` and queued in the repository's one committer queue beside the pushes, under their bounds and busy past them (`crewlet.code.commit.busy`, by `kind`), but DECIDED ALONE, never batched: its op id is the compaction's own, so a failover is one compaction the ledger collapses, and its session its `Seen`, so it is decided from rows at least as new as its plan's; the repository not deleted or purging (`RepoGone`'s sentence; archived is decided), every input one live row of part `pack` and not full (`PackRow.Full`, the ONE predicate: a pack section at `FullPackBytes`, seven eighths of the 1 GiB cut `CompactPackBytes`, or a bundle at `FullBundleBytes`; `FullAt` the same rule at a suite's scaled cut, both bounds scaled by it) else `stale`, every output a bundle `PackRef.Check` admits else `pack_too_large` and its key minted within `RecordWithin` else `upload_expired` (`uploadExpired`, the push's own arm lifted into one helper), and no more objects out than the inputs' rows hold, else `ErrInvalidProposal` before any record (`code_compaction_invalid`, a builder's bug) — every rule of which its builder holds a compaction to BEFORE a byte of it is stored (`CompactionProposal.ValidateUnstored`, as `Proposal.ValidateUnstored` does a push's pack: a stand-in key and digest where its puts will fill them); at most `MaxCompactInputs` (1 024; `BenchmarkApplyACompaction` applies that many in 62 ms, and the widest PUBLICATION — those inputs beside a layer replacing 31 of 16 parts each, 2 609 rows, the op's widest record — in 68 ms, both under the third of `statelog.ApplyTxTimeBudget` they are held to) inputs and `MaxCompactOutputs` (16, every output uploaded at once within `RecordWithin`) outputs, `compactDecidedRows` (2 065) rows; answered applied only from the output rows its record applied, in `code_packs` or already retired. The apply moves ROWS, every row of an input it finds and every output `code_packs` does not hold, the counters by those rows and never by the record's lists, and a merge judges no reachability — every object of the inputs is in the outputs — so a push decided against the inputs never lands a ref a compaction cut (ADR-0060). A git node finds the repositories a compaction may be due in by the `compaction_due` read — the engine's own, refused with an asker, at the level it names (stale where none) — the active and archived repositories at `MinPacks` live packs or more, MOST FIRST, each with its row's version and the objects its packs hold, one backward walk of `code_repos_packs_idx` (replicated 0060) that stops at the first below the floor. THE PACK BOUND IS THE BACKPRESSURE: a push carrying a pack into a repository at `MaxPacksPerRepo` live packs (2 048, half the spool wait's cold sync at S3's slow first byte), the packs of its batch's earlier entries counted in, is refused `compaction_behind` WHOLE and undecided — its record carries nothing of it, the batch's later entries are judged without it, and a push carrying no pack (deletions, refs moved onto commits the repository holds) is decided as ever — in the one sentence the code host's precheck forms too (`BehindDetail`), naming what the repository holds beside the bound, since a later build's committer may have taken it past this build's. A push its receiver checked at another DROP EPOCH than the row's (`Proposal.DropEpoch`, its synced copy's, never recorded) is refused `expunging` WHOLE and undecided, once the status admits it and before any rule of its own (`ExpungingDetail`) — and so is a MERGE, a CANDIDATE and a LANDING built at another epoch (`MergeProposal.DropEpoch`, `QueueProposal.DropEpoch`, never recorded: `MergeRefusalExpunging`, `ExpungingBuiltDetail`), whose builder builds it again; a merge's or a landing's source deletion records its ref drop through the one push apply: an expunge's begin since has left dropping what that copy held live, which its commit retires; and a snapshot reads every page at one drop epoch (`StateCursor.DropEpoch`, `ErrSnapshotMoved` otherwise), so the epoch a receiver carries is its copy's. A CODING BOX'S push whose pack holds a commit committed as the engine that the repository does not already hold is refused `forged_committer` (`RefusalForgedCommitter`) by its RECEIVER alone, before any proposal — an answer about a push, never recorded and never carried by the router, as `store_full` is, so it is no field of a record and takes no `versionedFields` row. A compaction may carry a published LAYER (`LayerRef`, `Compaction.Layer`; base format under the same stated condition, with the `versionedFields` row it would take named at the field): a PUBLICATION, git's own incremental multi-pack-index layer with its bitmap and commit-graph layer, built once for the fleet, its rows the repository's chain in `code_layers` (root first, at most `MaxPublishedLayers`, 32) and its bytes `LayerPartBytes` (1 GiB) parts, at most `MaxLayerParts` (16), named by `code_layer_parts` — `LayerReferences`, the first `Derived` table (ADR-0051): kept while named, never audited, never carried by a backup, copied by a store move. Its rules (publication.go): no compaction retires a pack a layer names unless it replaces that layer, so every pack a layer row names stays live; the layers it replaces are the chain's top and its base the one below (else stale); the chain it leaves at most `MaxPublishedLayers` (else `ErrInvalidProposal`); every pack it names live; and it COVERS EVERY PACK LIVE AT ITS PLAN (`Seen`) THAT ITS BASE DOES NOT — the closure git's bitmap needs, since a layer over a cut's full packs alone is refused by git and writes no bitmap — a pack pushed after the plan staying outside the chain; its parts held to the push's `upload_expired` rule. The apply replaces the top — the replaced layers and their parts gone at once, the layer at its base's place plus one — and RECOUNTS `published_packs`/`published_objects` from the rows, with `published_at`, `publish_took_ms` and `layers_position` (replicated 0062); a publication that does not fit the chain it meets applies as nothing, whole (`code_layer_not_applied`), and one applied already finds its layer and changes nothing; it is answered applied only from its layer's row beside its outputs'. A snapshot serves the WHOLE chain on page 1, first (`RepoSnapshot.Layers`, `LayersServed`), on every full answer and on a delta whose floor is below `layers_position`, charged to that page; and the `publication_due` read — the engine's own, refused with an asker, naming nothing else, at the level it names (stale where none) — lists the active and archived repositories whose live packs are past those the last publication's recount found covered — every one holding a pack no layer covers, and one holding only a pack pushed since that is byte-identical to a published one, which has nothing to publish and a plan over its packs answers not due, because a push moves the live count alone (exact, it would read the chain on every push) — LEAST RECENTLY PUBLISHED FIRST, one walk of the partial `code_repos_unpublished_idx`. Only the final purge removes a chain, bounded apart from what a part counts. A LAYER'S FILES ARE READ WITHOUT GIT, BY ONE READING (`layer.go`, ADR-0008), because a git process trusts a layer's multi-pack-index and commit-graph layer by their names, which are their own trailers it never reads: `ParseMidx`, `ParseBitmap` and `ParseGraph` read git's three formats as git 2.56.0's readers do — as exact as git's writer about what every file it writes has (the chunks contiguous from the table of contents to the trailer, each at the size its count gives, the trailer recomputed) and as lenient as git's reader about what that reader ignores (a chunk or a bitmap option it does not know) — every count held to the file's own size before it sizes anything (the three `Fuzz…` targets, seeded with git's own files, hold that by the bytes each read allocates), and none holds a file: a header, a table of contents and the chunks a count is read from, the rest hashed as it streams. GIT'S HASHFILE TRAILER IS ONE RULE (`hashfile.go`, ADR-0008): `HashTrailer` over the piece loop `HashPieces` is what both a layer's check and the cache's check, scrub and Verify hold a file's trailer by — the same layer files reach both, fetched and then kept — and what `crewlet code export` holds the bundle it writes by, reading a file a piece at a time in a slot its caller's hold takes (`Pieces`: a git node's read lane, a piece of the cache's `CheckPieceBytes`) and checking its context before every read, so a check of the largest repository's root layer, some 2.6 GB, stops when its caller does and never holds a read slot for more than a piece; so every reader here, `CheckLayer` and `LayLayer` take a context and `Pieces`. `CheckLayer` holds a layer's three files to its row and the chain below it before any git process is handed them — each file exactly its section, the multi-pack-index's trailer the row's `LayerID` and its PNAM the row's `Packs`, the bitmap that index's with its tables fitting it, the commit-graph layer's BASE the GraphIDs of the rows below it and its filters version 2 — refusing `ErrBadLayer` naming the field, definite and never retried, while a failed read, a refused hold and an ended context are answered as they came; `LayLayer` states a row's byte fields from the files a build wrote by the same reading and holds them to each other and to the base the build was written over (its commit-graph layer's BASE exactly the base rows' GraphIDs, `CheckLayer`'s one comparison), so a build whose files no node would install is refused before a byte is stored, and the publisher has only its plan's packs left to compare. A layer's bytes, the three files back to back, are `LayerPartBytes` parts cut by ONE rule both ways (`layerPart`: a part's start in the layer and its runs in the files, by its index): `StoreLayer` uploads every part at once as `LayerContentType`, each under `UploadDeadline`, the first failure the answer and the parts stored the collector's, and `FetchLayerPart` lays one part read through the store's verified read (`LayerReader`, which `*objstore.Store` satisfies) back where it came from. An EXPUNGE — the one rewrite that drops objects — is two records of their own on `repo.<id>` (`OpExpungeBegin`, `OpExpungeCommit`, base format under the same stated condition, each op's doc naming the `versionedFields` row it would take), proposed through `Committer.ProposeExpunge` into the same queue and each decided alone under its own operation id (decide_expunge.go, apply_expunge.go): its BEGIN (`ExpungeBegin`) rewrites some live packs — a full one among them — into packs holding what a ref reached at its maintainer's scan, live (`PackExpunge`), and the rest, DROPPING (`PackCruft`, `PackDropping`: still a `code_packs` row naming its object, served, never counted live and never a rewrite's input), so it keeps every object and needs no fence; it moves the repository's DROP EPOCH (`drop_epoch`, `RepoRef.DropEpoch`) and names its open expunge (`expunge_id`), and drops the published layers from the lowest naming an input up, recounting the published columns and moving `layers_position`; its rules: no expunge open, the epoch the row's plus one, every input one live row of part `pack`, the layers it drops exactly the chain's top over its inputs (`LayersOver`, exported for the maintainer that plans the begin), each output `PackRef.Check`ed and minted within `RecordWithin`, and no more objects out than in (`ErrInvalidProposal`); at most `MaxExpungeInputs` (`MaxCompactInputs`, 1 024: `BenchmarkApplyACompaction`'s expunge/begin arm applies the widest begin, 2 625 rows with a chain of 32 layers dropped, in 75 ms, under the same third of `statelog.ApplyTxTimeBudget`, and its widest commit in 13.5 ms) inputs and `MaxExpungeOutputs` (32) outputs. Its COMMIT (`ExpungeCommit`, op id its own `CommitID`, never the begin's) retires exactly the repository's dropping packs into `code_packs_retired` and keeps a live RESCUE (`PackRescue`) of what a push landed between scan and begin revived; its ONE precondition is its expunge, open at its epoch — no ref, so nothing a push does starves it. A person's purge is a REQUEST (`OpExpungeRequest`, `ExpungeRequest`, a bound person's): it records `expunge_request` and its position and nothing else, a later one while it is open is recorded too and WIDENS it to its own id and position (it asks about more: a pack pushed between the two, what a ref dropped between them left), and it is SERVED — cleared — once no pack dates its CONTENT from before it (`requestServed`, one seek of `code_packs_content_idx`: at its own record for a repository with no pack, at a commit otherwise). Every pack row names the position its content dates from as a purge reads it (`content_position`, replicated 0067, served as `PackRow.Content`; carried into `code_packs_retired`): a push's pack its own position, a compaction's outputs, an expunge's that keeps a ref drop's grace and every rescue the oldest of the packs they replace (`rewritePlan.content`: moving content judges none of it), and a purge's kept and dropping packs its scan (`ExpungeBegin.Seen`, never past the begin) — so a rewrite that judges nothing neither serves a request nor strands one by adding its outputs after it, and a purge's cycle takes the live packs that date from before its request. Every pack row also names WHEN its newest content arrived (`arrived_at`, replicated 0100, served as `PackRow.Arrived`; carried into `code_packs_retired`): a push's and a queue merge's pack its record's broker instant, every rewrite's output the latest of the rows it retires (`rewritePlan.arrived`), the record's own instant where it retires none — so the maintainer's weekly expunge keeps every pack that arrived within `PruneGrace` whole, by a column rather than a position, which says nothing of time. While an expunge is open a second begin is stale, and so is a publication (rule 6g, read off the row first), which the `publication_due` listing holds to by naming no repository with an expunge open (`expunge_id = ''`, a residual predicate on its walk) until the commit lands; a plain compaction is decided as ever, and `livePacksSQL` reads live packs alone. Output ids are one derivation for every rewrite (`DeriveOutputPackID`). The `indexed` read (`ReadIndexed`, M6-D12) lists the active and archived repositories searched by index, by name over `code_repos_search_idx`, the engine's own read a git node's indexer polls, and `IndexHolders` (2) is how many git nodes keep each one's code index. The `expunge_due` read lists the active and archived repositories with an expunge open or a request waiting, by id, each with its live objects, which places it by the publication's charge (the partial `code_repos_expunging_idx`, replicated 0064), then — asked with `ReadQuery.ExpungedBefore`, the maintainer's weekly schedule — every other active or archived repository of any kind, scratch ones too, last expunged at or before that instant or never, least recently expunged first (one seek of the partial `code_repos_expunge_due_idx`, the same migration, keyset on `(expunged_at, id)` under a `weekly:` cursor; the instant is refused on every other read), the `expunge_tips` read the objects one repository's expunge keeps beside its refs — its ref drops since an instant, then whatever the instant what rows hold in flight (M6-D2): every revision head of a draft or open change, every live queue entry's head, every candidate's tip and every green row, each phase a seek of a named index in its order (`code_changes_open_idx`, then each live change's revisions by `code_revisions`' key, two seeks and never a join, since the store's planner sorts a join by an inner table's column; `code_queue_live_idx`, `code_candidates_train_idx`, `code_green`'s key) under a phase-tagged cursor (`ExpungeTipsQuery`, refusing an asker, a point read of the repository paged by a keyset over each phase's index, each object once a page) — what an expunge keeps beside the refs for its grace — and `ExpungeProposal.ValidateUnstored` holds a begin or a commit laid out and not yet stored to every rule `Validate` holds it to, and `PackKind`/`PackState` are the closed sets a pack row's kind and state are written from — M5's merge and candidate among the kinds, which no record of this build writes yet. A pack a record RETIRES moves to `code_packs_retired` — the FIRST `Retired` table (ADR-0051, `RetiredPackReferences`), whose rows keep their objects from the collector and do nothing else — for `RetireGrace` (24 h, over the 23 h 21 min of the largest bundle's longest read — `objstore.ReadBudget(MaxPackBytes)`, 21 h 21 min — plus the two hours the broker member that stamped a retirement and the node sweeping it may disagree by while no node raises `clock_skew`, twice `objstore.AssumedClockSkew`), and the same pass SWEEPS them before it purges anything: a `retire_sweep` record on the repository's subject (`Store.RetireSweep`; base format under the stated condition that no build carrying the code domain has run without the op, with the `versionedFields` row it would take named at `OpRetireSweep`) removes, oldest first, the rows whose broker instant the duty's clock puts past the grace — the walk stopping at the first row inside it, so a retirement behind one still inside the grace waits for the next pass whatever instant a leader change stamped on it, at most `RetireSweepRows` a record and never part of one retirement — and raises the repository's `retired_through` — past which a delta is no longer complete, so a snapshot from below it is answered in full; the apply reads no clock, and a purge takes a repository's retired rows with the rest, whatever their grace, since no read begins on a deleted repository. A CHANGE (`web!42`) IS ROWS ON ITS REPOSITORY'S SUBJECT, never a subject of its own (replicated 0058, `code_changes` created again by 0066): `code_changes`, its `code_change_bodies`, its `code_change_authors` and its `code_revisions`, so its lifecycle and the pushes that move its branches meet at one committer and one arbitration, and its number — the repository's `code_repos.next_change`, read and advanced by the apply that opens it — follows the log's order and never repeats, which no constraint asks of the rows; what is said ABOUT a change goes on a subject of its own into tables of their own, one subject a table. A CHANGE'S CONVERSATION IS ON ITS OWN SUBJECT (`KindChange`, `change.<id>`, appended last to `ObjectKinds` so the suite's first three never move; replicated 0072): `code_conversations` (its counters, what its comments weigh, `text_bytes`, and its version and position), `code_reviewers`, `code_participants` and `code_comments`, written by records on the change's subject and by none on the repository's — an ask for review, a comment and a thread's resolution, each a `ConversationWrite` the estate's `code.conversation` asks of the repository's COMMITTING data node, keyed by the repository like its pushes, whose `ChangeWriter` — the committer's sibling, sharing none of its queue, budget or batches, its options classified in the engine's epoch-seam gate — decides it in ONE snapshot under `PatternArbitrated` at the asker's `Seen` as a record of its own op (`ConversationOps`, base format under the domain's stated condition), ONE CHANGE'S WRITES AT A TIME (a single flight a change, waiting its turn only while the asker's attempt has a `CommitBudget` and `ReplyMargin` left, busy otherwise — and busy AT ONCE past `MaxChangeWritesWaiting` of its change, `ChangeWriterNodeWrites` across the node or `ChangeWriterNodeBytes` of them, the committer's admission rule; the checks writer shares the same single flights, `flight.go`, with bounds of its own) and its publish under the writer's own lifetime; its rules (`decide_conversation.go`, rule D21): an ask by an author, a principal at review or above, or a person, each reviewer judged on the deciding node's chart whoever asks (a seat at review or above and no author of the change, a person always; `no_reviewer`, `too_many_reviewers` past `MaxReviewersPerChange`, 32), a comment by any reader of the repository, a reply only to a thread's ROOT on the same change (`thread_gone`), an anchored comment's revision one the change has reached (`unknown_revision`), a thread resolved by its starter, an author, a maintainer or a person; a write asking for what the rows already say answered UNCHANGED; a comment's id DERIVED from the operation id (`CommentID`); a write the chart judges (`ConversationWrite.JudgesChart`: a seat's, and every ask of reviewers) decided by no node without a chart or behind its configuration, which the estate declares `judgesGrant` by; every answer read from the conversation's row (`ChangeWriter.applied`); `crewlet.code.conversation.writes` by op and outcome. Its record's scope names the repository's path and the change's (`ChangePath`, `c/c/<id>`), since its decide reads the repository's rows; the deletion gate reads its repository off the record's scope (`gatedRepo`, `scopedRepo`: the envelope framework decoded, never the payload, which the apply holds to it), so a purged repository's change takes no record; the apply holds a record to its change's repository (an error otherwise, a record no writer forms) and is pure over it, a reviewer's kind riding it. A purge takes a change's conversation with the change — its reviewers and participants among the narrow rows a cut takes first, its comments last and by BYTES as well as rows, weighed by `text_bytes` and taken off it as they go — and a change whose conversation's text would take a part past `PurgePartBytes` is CUT by its comments (`apply_purge_changes.go`), since taken whole it held the lane for every mebibyte past the bound and never cut it stopped every part. A CONVERSATION IS READ WITH ITS CHANGE (`conversationread.go`): a listing naming a `reviewer`, or `needs=review` (the asker's own where it names none, a person's by their human seat), walks `code_reviewers_reviewer_idx` (reviewer, state, change_id) — the WAITING asks, newest change first, the changes they name read by their keys beside them — an ask waits only while its review is owed: the reviewer's review answers it (`reviewed`), and a change's close or merge, a record on its repository's subject that writes nothing of its conversation, has its waiting asks ended (`ended`) by an `asks` record its home asks of the change's writer once the due listing finds it (`code_conversations.asked`, `.ended`, and the partial `code_conversations_asked_idx`), and asked again once reopened, so a listing reads what waits now, never a reviewer's whole history, `needs=review` keeping the live ones while an ended change awaits its settling — and a `change` read names the PART it pages (`ReadQuery.Part`): its revisions, with the detail and the conversation's counters on their first page, its reviewers by handle, or its comments in thread order (`code_comments_thread_idx`); the decide's read of the reviewers a write names NAMES the key (`reviewersKey`), which beside the reviewer's index was planned as a seek of the reviewer alone.
