THE BOX (`sandbox.Manager.AcquireBox`: no coding agent, no login, none of `providers.sandbox.setup`) gets EXACTLY the check's named `code.checks.env` secrets (resolved; one the company does not declare `errored defs_invalid`, one resolving to nothing `setup_failed`), `CREWLET_CHECK_{REPO,SHA,BASE,NAME,ATTEMPT,API}` (API the `/box/code` sibling of `CREWLET_BOX_GIT_URL`, which `New` requires to end in `/box/git`), and `sandbox.BoxGit` with user `check`, the check's token (`Tokens`, the engine's adapter of `host.CheckTokens`, valid timeout + `code.CheckBoxGrace`) and NO identity; its setup `GitVersionStep` and `CloneRecipe` single-sha with the cone, or nothing for `checkout: none`, made within `code.CheckBoxGrace` of the claim (box failure `box_failed`, a setup step's `setup_failed`, both requeued). The command runs detached (`StartBackground`), output and exit status to files under the box's home, and every `CheckPoll` (15 s, the coding waiter's) the runner reads the attempt's row — an ENDED row (cancelled, swept) closes the box and records nothing — asks the job (`LostPolls` 4 unanswered polls in a row `box_failed`), refreshes the TTL to the check's end plus the grace, and past the timeout (the file's held to `code.checks.max_timeout_seconds`, from the command's start) closes the box and ends it FAILED `timed_out`, a measurement; an exit is passed for 0, failed with the last `code.CheckTailBytes` of output otherwise. A RUN'S RESULTS: the command's wrapper copies the reports its definition names out of the checkout once it ends, BEFORE it writes its status, so a poll finding the status finds every report; `junit.go` STREAMS a JUnit report token by token (encoding/xml, which expands no declared entity) and `sarif.go` decodes only the fields a SARIF 2.1.0 log's results are read for (levels error and warning, SARIF's default, are failures; a location's `file://` and checkout directory taken off), both into one list held to an ending's bounds FIRST COME FIRST KEPT (`report.go`: at most `code.MaxCheckFailures` within `code.MaxCheckFailureBytes`, each message cut to `code.CheckFailureMessageBytes` at a rune, every field valid UTF-8, the rest counted `FailuresOmitted`; `FuzzJUnit`), a report at most `sandbox.MaxFileBytes`. THE SUMMARY is the file the command wrote to `CREWLET_CHECK_SUMMARY`, else the reports' counts, else its exit and duration, with every report named and not read said after it. A FAILED run (an exit or `timed_out`) keeps its log: the output's last `code.MaxCheckLogBytes` STREAMED from the box (`OpenFile`, the earlier bytes read and dropped) into one object through `Uploader` (the fleet's `objstore.Store`), which its ending's `CheckLog` and so its `code_check_logs` row names; a full store (`objstore.ErrFull`) or a failing one ends it without, its summary saying so. A passed run keeps its summary alone. The run's files are at `sandbox.DefaultHome`'s `.crewlet-check/`, since the summary's path is in the box's environment before the box exists and both cells a check runs in keep their home there. The ending is asked again for `FinishPolls` (2) polls through a busy writer, and the box closed on every path under `WithoutCancel`. A CHECK SPENDS NO TOKENS: the package imports no LLM or spend seam, and an ending's cost is its duration. Never imports `code/query`, `code/compact`, `code/host` or `code/home`. |

A blob past the limit, or a chunk refused for a limit the source is past (`repo_size`, `repo_refs`, `ref_conflict`, `pack_too_large`), ENDS the import with its `Error`; any other failure is tried again after `RetryAfter` (1 min doubling to `code.MirrorInterval`), from the rows. Faults: `codefault.ImportPath()`, `MirrorPath()`. The fetch and the cut are cache writers in the attempt's scratch alone (internal/code/cache's writers gate). `code/query`, `code/compact`, `code/host`, `code/home`, `code/checkrun` and this package never import each other (`TestTheHomeImportsNoQueryCompactHostCheckRunnerOrMirrors`). |
| `code/query` | THE SERVER-SIDE READS OF A REPOSITORY BY OBJECT ID — a tree's listing, a file's lines, a diff's stat or one file's patch, how two commits compare, whether a commit LANDED in a branch and which push landed it, a path's HISTORY and a file's BLAME, and a change's REVISIONS: a revision's facts, where anchored lines are in another revision, and what a change changed between two — real git over a PINNED incarnation of the node's cache, through its dropping view (`cache.Synced.Dropping`: an object an expunge is dropping is still served by id until its commit), in the READ lane, every answer one page of at most `code.MaxGitAnswerBytes` of JSON, measured in encoding/json's own terms (a table built from the encoder at init: `<` is six bytes, not one), its cursor a position in an answer that is a function of object ids alone, so sound on any node. EVERY REVISION IS AN OBJECT ID, and an abbreviation is expanded by `rev-parse --disambiguate` from the objects alone — NEVER by cat-file's own reading of a short name, which tries it as a REF first: measured, a tag named like an abbreviation answered for another commit. A file's PATCH is git's own, one `diff-pairs -p` of its one raw record — an added or deleted file against /dev/null, a change of type as git's deletion and creation, a submodule's commits, a binary pair's one line — never a hunk written here or a blob streamed twice. And within ONE BUDGET of git run time, `gitproc.ReadDeadline`, every process of the kind given what the ones before it left (a comparison's two walks and its stat share it, as a patch's lookup and its diff do — the lookup charged though it ends its own run once its file's record is read — and as a landed-in search's ancestry checks do, so no `gitproc.HistoryWalk` term: a git read's asker waits seventeen seconds, on the node that asked as on every other, and that bound was one no comparison could reach), its waits for read-lane slots charged nothing: only a read whose own git runs the whole budget is `code.ErrSliceTooLarge`. A LANDED-IN read is one ancestry check against the tip the asker resolved, then a SEGMENTED BINARY SEARCH of the branch's moves in the index (segments at every forced move, deletion and the index's gap), never a walk of the log or the history, at most `MaxLandedProbes` (1 024 by M3-O22's rule, the largest power of two whose checks fit the read's budget beside the tip's: one check, a git process, measured 6.7 ms at the 99th percentile, so 1 025 are 6.9 s and 2 049 would be 13.7 s) and within the read's one budget of git, WHICHEVER ENDS IT answered with how far it reached — the oldest push known to keep the commit — never the read refused as too large, which would throw that away (only the check against the tip, before the search, runs the budget out as a read too large) — NEVER A WRONG PUSH: a move is the landing only where the commit was not reachable from its old value, which is asked at the view's first move and the first above the gap, so a landing the index does not hold is answered with no push and the range it lies in. A REVISION'S FACTS (`facts.go`) are six processes in turn over a change's head and its target's tip: the MERGE BASE (none for histories sharing nothing, whose change is measured from the empty tree; the head itself is HEAD IN TARGET, with nothing to measure), the TOUCHED paths (`diff-tree -r --raw`, whose modes, blob ids and status name the paths a lexer CLASSES — a `.go` file changed in place, a regular file in one mode on both sides, never one added, deleted, re-moded, a link or a submodule — stopped past `code.MaxTouchedPaths`, past `code.MaxTouchedBytes` of JSON, at a path that is no UTF-8 or at one no read by oid could name back (`code.GitPath`), which then TOUCHES ALL with no list — the safe side for whatever matches against it, and a list the revise recording it takes whole), and `git patch-id --VERBATIM` of the cumulative patch — never --stable, which strips whitespace Python, YAML and a Makefile mean; `--full-index`, since patch-id hashes a binary pair by the ids its index line names, abbreviated to however many digits the copy needs — written by diff-tree into a cache SPOOL (no name, gone when closed) and read back by patch-id, the next process of the read lane, never two slots piped — at most `MaxPatchSpools` (2, `code/home`'s `Concurrency`, held equal by `engine.TestEveryFactsReadOfAHomeHasASpool`) spooled on a node at once, a read finding none free waiting `code.GitShareWait` and then answered unready (share), so the volume holds `PatchSpoolAllowance` (512 MiB) of them at most, which the engine takes out of the cache's room beside the host's request spools. Past `MaxPatchIDDiffBytes` (256 MiB: `BenchmarkRevisionFacts` took a 249 MiB patch's facts in 2.3 s on two E-cores, a quarter of the read's budget), the id is OMITTED PAST ITS BOUND (`code.OmittedPastBound`) — an id equal to nothing — and the read answered, final, as classes cut by `MaxClassedBytes` are; one the read's budget left out — the patch, its id or the classes — is answered OUT OF TIME (`code.OmittedOutOfTime`), apart from the bound's, since it measures the node at that moment rather than the commits: such facts are not `Final` and nobody records them (the home asks again), and a lexable blob the copy lacks is the node's failure (unready, `node_failed`), while a merge base or a walk that runs the budget out is too large, as any read's git is: so the facts need no deadline of their own. Between the walk and the patch ONE `cat-file --batch-check` (`read.check`, charged to the budget once the read's own git has begun) names the TREES of the base and the head — equal pairs are the same code, which an approval carries across (`code.ChangeClassNoCodeChange`) — and the sizes of each classed path's two blobs; and LAST, after the id a rebase's approval rides on, ONE `cat-file --batch --buffer` writes those blobs within `code.MaxClassedBlobBytes` a version and `code.MaxClassedBytes` in all, each pair lexed as it arrives (`lexer`, `code.ClassifyGo`) and dropped, so a classification holds two versions of one file; classes the bound or the budget cut short are answered INCOMPLETE, the paths lexed keeping theirs, and never fail the read. The facts — and a commit's CHECKS FILE (`checksfile.go`, `code.GitChecksFile`: `.crewlet/checks.yaml` at a commit, its object's id and type and, within `code.MaxChecksFileBytes`, its bytes WHOLE and exact, one answer read by one `cat-file blob` into a buffer refusing a byte past the size the batch reported, a file the parse refuses by its size answered by its size alone, never memoized — the home keeps what it parsed) — are THE ENGINE'S OWN READ (`code.GitReadKind.EnginesOwn`): the repository's home asks them — the facts to record, the file to queue a change's checks from — naming no asker — one naming any is refused — so they are held to no grant and recorded as nobody's read of a `visibility: unit` repository. So is a read MADE FOR A CHECK (`code.GitQuery.Check`: a check's box's tree, file or diff of its commit through the API's `/box/code/`, admitted by its attempt's row): the server's grant and its record both ask `code.GitQuery.EnginesOwn`, the kind's rule or the check's, never the kind's alone, and `Validate` has already held such a read to the attempt's commit. A RE-ANCHORING (`anchor.go`) places up to `code.MaxReanchors` ranges read in one revision's blobs — a side-new range at the head, a side-old one at the base — by `code.Placing` — `code.Reanchor`'s rule fed one hunk at a time — over ONE `diff-pairs -p -U0` fed each distinct pair of blobs NUMBERED rather than named, so no path reaches git and each hunk names its pair, every hunk read where it streams and KEPT NOWHERE, a pair whose anchors are settled read no further (O(anchors) of memory, `BenchmarkReanchor`): GONE where the path holds no file now, OUTDATED where its own lines changed or a side is binary. An INTERDIFF (`interdiff.go`) is a diff read of two revisions' heads whose stat and patch are KEPT to the exact SET of either revision's touched paths (`pathFilter` — `a*` a file's name, never a pathspec, which a diff would take on its command line alone, with no --pathspec-from-file), WALKING NEITHER HEAD: each touched path is LOOKED UP at both heads by ONE `cat-file --batch-check=%(objectmode) %(objectname) %(objecttype) -Z` fed `<head>:<path>` lines (`touchedRecords`), and the raw record diff-tree would write is made here for each path whose entries differ (a tree is no entry, a change of type `T`), in git's order of full paths, for diff-pairs to count or patch — so its git is O(touched), at most 2 × `code.MaxTouchedPaths` lookups, never the target's churn between the two bases, which a walk of the heads read whole — and by none where either touches all or the two share a base, since their heads then differ only in files one touches — answered and memoized as a diff is (Kind interdiff). EVERY PAGE A RUN CUTS IS MEMOIZED in the copy's memo (`memo.go`, the cache's `Memo`), keyed by the format, the page bound, the kind, every whole id, the normalised arguments and the cursor: a read naming whole ids asks it as soon as the grant is judged and BEFORE ANY GIT RUNS — the presence check would leave a hit no faster than two processes, and the memo is the copy's, so an entry exists only where this copy found its objects — and one naming an abbreviation once it is expanded; a hit holds no share unit and is stamped (position, pushes, coverage, `Memo`) as a cut page is. git cannot seek inside an object or a listing, so the run that misses cuts EVERY PAGE from the asked one to its answer's end (a listing, a file's range, a stat's files — each page's counts written into its staged entry once the stat has counted past it, `pageCounts`, so a run holds one page's counts and never every file's — and a patch), each the page a read from its cursor cuts — `blobPages` and `patchPages` chain the one-page cutters at the next cursor — so a file read page by page streams once, inside the read's one budget of git; a comparison is one page, a landed-in read, a revision's facts (asked once a revision and a target by whoever records them, and an id omitted for time is no page to keep) and a re-anchoring (keyed by a list of anchors each caller makes anew) never memoized. A miss of a page another read of the node is cutting is a SINGLE FLIGHT (`read.await`, keyed by the repository and the page's memo key): it waits for that read to answer — holding no share unit and no read-lane slot, its own answer's deadline bounding the wait — and is answered from the memo it filled, so N seats asking one page at once run one git; one whose memo still misses runs its own, unled. A page carries its BUDGET, the most its stamps may cost and it still fit, so one whose stamps grew is cut again; pages are encoded BINARY (varints, strings sliced from one copy), since encoding/json would decode a 400-entry page at a few tens of MB/s, and `memoFormat` is pinned to a fingerprint of the answer types (`TestAnAnswersShapeIsItsMemoFormat`). A read asked UNSTAMPED (`code.GitQuery.Unstamped`, a page's kinds only) reads no index of pushes and is stamped with nothing — no repository, position, memo, push or coverage (`read.stamp`, the one place they are set, which the envelope a page is cut against and a memoized page's budget read too) — so its page is cut against a room no stamp moves and is a function of the object ids it names, its arguments and the build alone: what a question answers immutable; it is memoized under a key of its own, since the stamped cut of the same read is another page. Asked of the repository's HOME git node first by the estate's walk, and built by the engine beside the code host over the same cache and pool (`native.gitReads`). Holds no goroutine, and is STOPPED by whoever stops the code host, before its cache (`Server.Stop`): every read after it is answered unready, `node_failed`, starting nothing, and every read in flight is CUT — the server's own lifetime ends every read's context — answered the same while its asker listens, and waited for, so once the stop returns no read of the node is being answered or recorded; the host never counted these reads among its requests, so a read in flight outlived its stop. A read of a `visibility: unit` repository that answered, a memo hit included, is recorded (`Options.Fetched`, required: one read via `tool`, with its kind, the asker and the answer's encoded size, filed by the engine in the node's reads ledger, `code/readaudit`, so neither the read nor one waiting on the page it cuts waits on the broker for its record). A SEAT'S SECOND LOOK AT THE LOG'S HEAD IS ITS TURN'S TO PAY (`Options.Resyncs`, `code.NewResyncs`, ONE a node with the code host's boxes and engine writes): its turn (`code.GitQuery.Turn`, stamped by the builtins' one `gitRead`) on its seat, 16 refilled at the census's 125 a seat-day, and past it the refusal is answered from the copy at the seat's floor, saying `(not re-judged at linearizable: …)`; a read made for a check (`/box/code/`) pays its attempt's bucket on its repository's checks (`code.CheckKey.Resyncs`) — the one its git pays at the node's code host, so an attempt has one burst a node, counted `crewlet.code.box.resyncs` with its git's; a person's and the engine's own reads are never charged. A SEARCH (`search.go`, `code.GitSearch`, M6-D12/D13) names a REF, not an id: it resolves its commit (`rev-parse --verify`, looked for once more at the log's head) before the memo is asked, then reads it ONE OF TWO WAYS that answer the same lines, RE2's, in path and line order — THROUGH THE CODE INDEX where the copy's (`cache.Cache.IndexTip`, `CodeIndex`) answers for that commit (the plan's candidates in every segment, kept where the tip map names them at a path the query's filter admits, read through one `cat-file --batch` in path order, confirmed line by line), and otherwise WITH `git grep -I -n -z -E` over `codeindex.Plan.Grep`'s ONE alternation of escaped strings (one pattern, never one a string: git's look-ahead rescans the rest of a file for each pattern after every hit), every line it answers confirmed the same way and every file past `code.MaxIndexedFileBytes` dropped by its `ls-tree -l` size as the index skips it — the query's paths filtered HERE over git's answer, never handed to git as a pathspec. The search's git runs under ONE unit of the share within the read's budget of git, and the whole within `code.SearchBudget` (5 s, the resolve budget): one that runs it out answers what it found, `Truncated`, and is memoized NOWHERE, its page no function of its arguments; a complete one is memoized under the commit, the ref and every part of the query, whichever path cut it. At most `code.MaxSearchHits` (500) lines of `code.MaxSearchLineBytes` (256) each. The FIRST of an indexed repository's two index holders, asked as its walk's home, whose index does not answer for the default branch's tip yet, answers `index_building` (no fill passed) so the second holder answers; no other node refuses so (`cache.IndexedTip.PassesOn`), so a walk always ends at an answer. |
| `code/readaudit` | WHO READ A UNIT'S REPOSITORY, AS THE NODE THAT SERVED THE READS KEEPS THEM BETWEEN FLUSHES: every clone and fetch the code host served and every server-side read the git reads answered of a `visibility: unit` repository, FILED as it is answered (`Ledger.Add`, a fold under one lock) and published every 15 s as ONE `code_fetched` record per key — company day, repository, principal kind and principal, way, a server-side read's kind, and the seat it acted as — counting its reads and summing their bytes, named as its newest read named the repository and stamped with that read's instant, so a record never straddles a company day and a CI token fetching every few seconds is a record a flush, not one a fetch. A `coalesce` ledger, so OFF THE READ'S PATH: a read used to be published as it was answered, and while the event stream had no leader each held its fetch's response, or its slot on the git lane, up to the client's five seconds — sixty-four of them the whole lane, every repository's reads queued behind them — and a refused publish lost its record for good; now a refused record waits sealed for the next flush, up to `coalesce.MaxPending`. Built ONCE on the engine (`codeReads`), not with the code host, since the host is rebuilt at an apply; its last flush is the teardown's, at once with the auxiliary spend's, after the code host stops and before the usage publisher's own |
| `codeindex` | THE CODE HOST'S TRIGRAM INDEX AS PURE FUNCTIONS OVER BYTES — which three-byte runs each blob holds, the two files a repository's index is kept in (a SEGMENT, `CRCX`: a blob table with an escape and a skipped flag, a trigram table, uvarint postings; a TIP MAP, `CRCT`: a tip's every path and the blob at it; each under a SHA-256 trailer, the cache's run-trailer check), the PLAN of a regular expression over them, and the QUERY a person or a seat writes — so a code search reads only the few files that can match. It reads and writes no file and runs no git: the cache owns the files, `code/query` the git. Postings are keyed by BLOB, never by path or commit, so a new tip indexes only the blobs it introduced, a compaction or a re-clone changes nothing here, and which postings count is the tip map's to say; a merge (`MergeSegments`) keeps only what its keep keeps, writing the very bytes a build of those blobs writes, and merges each trigram's lists IN PAIRS between two buffers kept from trigram to trigram — as a plan unions a clause's literals —, so its work is L·⌈log₂ k⌉ and it allocates nothing a posting, where folding the lists in one at a time cost k·L and allocated 69 bytes a posting over 32 segments. Trigrams are taken over the blob's UNICODE LOWERING (`bytes.ToLower`), the comparison `regexlit`'s prefilter makes for a folded literal — an ASCII-only lowering is cheaper and misses `(?i)` across É and é — and a blob holding an escape (`regexlit.HasEscape`, ſ) carries a flag every clause holding a folded literal admits, since no lowering sees through it. Both are taken with the Unicode tables the build carries, which move with the Go toolchain and give letters a case they lacked, while a segment outlives its build (kept across restarts, hard-linked across incarnations, never re-read for a blob it holds) — so a segment is STAMPED with `unicode.Version` and a build carrying other tables refuses it as `ErrMalformed`, which rebuilds the index rather than ruling out a blob a newer lowering looks up by other bytes; a tip map holds nothing the tables decide and is not stamped. The plan is `regexlit.Required`'s clauses, looked up where every literal of a clause is three bytes or more lowered; a plan is looked up reading each posting list it names ONCE, whichever literals of whichever clauses hold the trigram (`lookup.go`) — the blobs holding what every literal of a clause holds read once for all of them, each literal admitting only blobs among those; a list more than one place reads, of one clause or of several, decoded once for them all, as far as the furthest blob one needs; a list one place reads, read there alone, into nothing, as far as what it keeps reaches — and a clause another implies is not looked up at all (a part a pattern repeats is a clause each time to `regexlit.Required`: `(foo.*){100}` was a hundred lookups of foo, each holding its every blob) — where looking a literal up at a time read a list once for every literal holding it and decoded it into a buffer of its own each time (a clause of 64 words sharing lists over 2 048 blobs: 289 280 bytes of postings read and 1 457 152 allocated, against 12 288 and 100 288); a pattern with no such clause is REFUSED (`ErrNoLiteral`) rather than answered by reading every file, as is one that can match across a line (`ErrNotLineLocal`); candidates are CONFIRMED by Go's regexp line by line, so the index and the grep path both answer RE2, never a second dialect. What a confirm may cost is BOUNDED where the plan is made, because Go's regexp may step every instruction of its program at every byte and a run of it cannot be stopped part way: a pattern whose program is past `MaxProgram` (1 024 instructions) is REFUSED (`ErrTooComplex`) — 400 bytes of counted repetitions compile to a hundred thousand, 1.5–2 ms a byte of a line —, `Confirm` runs the expression on a line only where its bytes times the program's instructions are within `MaxLineWork` (2^25: 0.22 s at the measured 6.7 ns an instruction-byte, and a short program still reads a whole minified line), counting a longer line that could match rather than running it or calling it a miss, and checks its caller's context between lines, so a search's budget holds to within one line's run. A blob past `MaxFileBytes` (1 MiB) or binary by git's own test (`Binary`: a NUL in the first 8 000 bytes, the rule `git grep -I` falls back to — the ONE copy of it, which `code/query`'s file reads call too) is SKIPPED: recorded so no later tip reads it again, and never a candidate. `ParseQuery` reads leading `repo:`, `path:` and `ref:` filters, then the text as written, at most `MaxQueryBytes` (400: knowledge search's bound, held to it by a test, since the knowledge seam is not pure); every mode (literal, regex, word) is an RE2 expression under `(?m)`; a path filter reads as a `.gitignore` line without negation, escapes or classes. Its suite writes patterns over non-ASCII case orbits and the long s, and blobs matching them from each pattern's own syntax tree (`regexlit/regexlittest`), and holds that no blob Go's regexp matches is ever ruled out. `Plan.Grep` is the GREP PATH'S HALF of the same plan: the strings of the most selective clause git reads EXACTLY, as ONE `git grep -E` alternation with every byte of its syntax escaped — never a string a `-F` pattern, whose look-ahead rescans the rest of a file for each pattern after every hit, nor the query's own expression, a second dialect; never a clause with a string holding a NUL or a newline; one with no folded literal outside ASCII, since git's `-i` folds ASCII alone and lowers no line — and, where it folds, every rune outside ASCII that RE2's `(?i)` folds onto an ASCII letter or whose lowering is one, since a folded literal is held as its lowering (the long s, the Kelvin sign, İ, derived from the Unicode tables), so the lines git answers are a superset of RE2's and a repository read with git grep answers what the index would; a plan with no such clause has git answer every line |
| `codesearch` | A CODE SEARCH OF EVERY REPOSITORY AN ASKER MAY READ, on the git nodes that hold them (M6-D14). ONE repository is a git read of it (`code.GitSearch`) through the keyed walk, whose answer or refusal is the search's. MORE are ONE SCATTER on `topics.CodeSearch`, never a walk a repository — an unfiltered search of a company would be thousands of requests: every git node serves it, and the request carries an ASSIGNMENT TABLE, each repository named under the first node of its git order (`estate.Router.GitOrders`, one reading for the whole table: its home, and for an indexed repository the first index holder), because every server of a subject hears every request and a scatter divides work by what it names; a repository its node did not answer is asked ONCE more of the next node of its order (the second index holder) and never a third time, so a search's wait is two rounds of `RoundBudget` at most (9 s: the share wait, the search's own 5 s and a reply margin each way — a sum of whole budgets — and a round ends once every node asked has answered). A serving node searches each repository through `code/query` AS THE ASKER THE REQUEST NAMES, its grant judged from that node's own chart — so the request is an INSTRUCTION and the scatter a `signedask.Channel` (`code.search`): unsigned, any broker client could read any repository as anybody; a request the fleet did not sign, a replay and one past its deadline answer nothing, one under a key the node does not hold answers `Unverified`. A node reads at most `ServeReads` (64, `queue.ConcurrentAnswers`) of its repositories at once — the git under its seat share either way, so the bound is on syncs and goroutines. THE MERGE IS EXACT: every repository answers its first lines by path bytes and line, a node keeps the first `Limit` of its repositories' lines by (name, path, line), the asker the first `Limit` of every node's — no line a node left out can precede one another kept, and a round assigns a repository to one node, so the answer is the same whichever node answers first. EVERY ANSWER SAYS WHAT IT DID NOT SEARCH (`Coverage`): each repository named is in exactly one of — by what the LAST node asked for it answered, so a silent runner-up is `NotAnswering` — searched, `Building` (the index holder building, its order naming no runner-up), `Stale` (a node behind the log or the configuration), `NotAnswering` (by node, with why), `NotPermitted` (a chart that moved since the listing) and `NoRef`, beside `GrepNotNamed` (the grep repositories an unfiltered search leaves out — grep runs only on those `repo:` names, at most `MaxGrepRepos`, 8, a monorepo's grep being a CPU-second or two) and `NotIndexed` (indexed ones read with grep), each a count and its first `MaxCoverageNames` names. The text is bounded as knowledge search's (`codeindex.MaxQueryBytes`, 400, refused and never cut) before anything is listed. Counted as `crewlet.code.search.queries{via}`, `.duration{via}` and `.coverage{reason}`. A refusal over ONE repository — a `repo:` naming one nobody declared or the asker may not read, or the one repository a search reads refusing it — is a `RepoRefusal` naming it, so a surface answering it (`code_search`'s `not_declared`) says which |
| `flock` | The ONE exclusive cross-process file lock: the node database's (`store`), the embedded broker's store directory (`queue/jetstream`), a coding CLI's shared login (`hostbox`) and a git node's cache directory (`code/cache`, whose boot empties directories only the process that filled them may be using), which each carried a copy of the syscall or would have (ADR-0008). flock, never fcntl (an fcntl lock is dropped when ANY descriptor for the file closes in the process); the file never unlinked (a peer mid-open would lock a dead inode while a third locked a fresh one); unlock before close (a duplicate of the descriptor — a forked child not yet exec'd — would otherwise keep the lock past its release); the holder stamped only once the lock is held, and not synced, since a reader sees the page cache and a crashed holder has released the lock with it. PER DESCRIPTOR, so two takers in one process exclude each other as two processes do; a caller that wants its handles to SHARE a claim (`store`) counts its sharers on top of one lock. Non-blocking: a caller that waits (`hostbox`) polls with its own bound and its own words for the holder. A release is safe twice and from two goroutines at once, so every caller defers it. |
| `learning` | What a seat remembers. Everything here is BEST EFFORT by design: a failed write is logged, a failed read answers empty. `memsync` is the exception that makes the rest true across a fleet: a seat's memory is written to the NODE's store, and placement moves seats — so every memory row rides a COMPACTED CHANGELOG (one subject per row, one message retained per subject) and a node hydrates a seat's rows before its mailbox attaches — READING TO WHERE THE CHANGELOG'S LEADER SAYS THEY END (`jsapi.Leader` over the seat's wildcard, a `STREAM.MSG.GET` whatever the stream's allow_direct says, asked again while nobody answers), because the replay is a consumer the server places on any replica, and one hosted on a copy behind an acknowledged write stopped short of the seat's newest rows and reported success. Deletes deliberately do not travel: the lifecycle re-converges, and a tombstone protocol would be a second thing to keep correct forever. It DECLARES the two `statelog` contracts it rests on rather than implementing their shapes privately: `ReplayCompacted`, which every rule in the hydration follows from, and STREAM IDENTITY — because a recreated changelog is EMPTY, so hydrating from one reports success and gives every seat on the node a blank diary, and a compacted stream has no checkpoint and no generation to answer a recreation with, so its only honest move is to refuse the seat. A SEAT'S MEMORY IS KEYED ON ITS HANDLE, which is immutable (ADR-0013): the diary and onboarding markers on the agent id derived from it, and episodes, both skill tables, counterparty profiles (a colleague subject included) and the conversation ledger on the handle itself, in the `agent_handle`/`observer_handle`/`subject_handle` columns memsync carries rows between peers by. `memread` is the other half: every node that ever ran a seat keeps a copy and only the HOLDER's is current, so a screen's read of a seat's memory or conversation ledger follows the seat's LEASE — answered here, asked of the holding incarnation on an ephemeral scatter every node serves (a node serving no API may be the holder), or empty and naming nobody for a seat no node holds — and a silent or still-hydrating holder is `unavailable`, never an empty memory. A read names its seat both ways (`memread.Seat`): the agent id its lease, diary and onboarding marker are keyed by, and the handle the rest of its memory is keyed by. Its reads RAISE, unlike the turn's: "remembers nothing" and "the store did not answer" are opposite facts on a screen. And the WRITE side follows the seat for the same reason: post-turn reflection is fed by a `reflection_due` wake on the SEAT'S OWN subject, attached by the holder after hydration and let go before the release's last flush — on one fleet-wide group, all but one in N of a seat's reflections wrote memory where nothing carried or read it |
| `schedule` | Role- and unit-scoped cron work, with at-most-once delivery, missed-tick catchup and a wall-clock cap |

## The edges

| Package | What it is for |
|---|---|
| `api` | REST + the dashboard. Served INSIDE the engine's process and nowhere else: `crewlet run` builds it over the engine's own store, broker and coordination plane, and `New` refuses a missing one by name, because a narrower answer built around a nil (an omitted health field, a 503, an absent route) reads as deliberate and hides the wiring mistake. `NodeRuntime` is the seam it asks the engine through. EVERY REQUEST LEAVES THE GUARD CARRYING A PRINCIPAL — resolved, anonymous, or unknown — and the EXEMPTION LIST is the whole of what is not guarded (probes, webhooks on their own HMAC, the per-run token paths, the dashboard shell, the sign-in routes, and the sign-out of THIS session — which clears the cookie whatever the node can read, where guarded a node that could not read its identity estate answered it 503 before it ran, and which verifies every bearer it ends itself). There is no list of what IS: guarded is what a route is, so the always-guarded prefix list is gone and each surface states its authority as a VERB where it is enforced — which is why every surface `New` mounts, `/config`, `/secrets` and `/setup` included, is a `guardedMounter` whose routes go through `authz.Router`, and why the deployment's own controls (`/backup`, every `/work/retention*` route and the fleet broker's removals) are mounted there on `admin`. None of them decided anything before: the guard answers only whether somebody resolved, and a surface that relied on that opened itself to every signed-in reader the day sessions arrived. The query registry is where both transports meet that: one question, one declared POSTURE (`queries.Posture` — `Everybody()`, `Requires(admin|app)` or `DecidedBy(verb)`, whose answer decides record by record), and a REST route and a socket frame decided by the same check, so no question is reachable by choosing a channel — and a registration with a zero posture, a `Requires` of anything but admin or an app, or a `DecidedBy` of a verb the table does not know PANICS at wiring time, because a question nobody decided about is an ungated surface that ships looking deliberate; a principal whose stage may not act is refused before any answer runs. THE OVERVIEW IS EVERYBODY'S (ADR-0033): the org chart, the agents' and coding runs' STATUS, spend, budgets, schedules, tools, health and `viewer` answer any resolved principal, while a call's or a run's BODY — prompts, responses, tool arguments, narration, `llm_history` payloads, a coding run's task, question, reply and bridged calls — goes only to admin, to the seat's lead (`audit.seat.read`) and, for a run's question, to the person it is put to, through ONE mask per surface (`livestate.MaskCall`, `SandboxEntry.For`, the sandbox-run key walk) that zeroes the body's versions and sets `body_withheld`, held by walks that fail on a field nobody classified; and a cross-app answer filters its rows through `authz.Shows`. Its refusal is three-valued for `iam.From`'s reason: an identity estate this node cannot read is `ErrUnavailable` with a Retry-After, a context no resolver touched is a plain FAULT (a route nobody wired through the guard never clears by waiting), and only a credential positively checked and refused is unauthorized. THE VIEWER IS THE CALLER: the principal travels in the context on both transports and no operator id rides beside it, and `viewer=` is gone from `work_items` and `work_views` — it had exactly one honest value, and any other was a free choice of whose pins, personal views and queue to read. A question that names somebody else's record resolves the name first — `iam.OwnerOf` through `Sources.Holders`, which `New` REQUIRES, so a login is its holder's record and never read literally, and only after the question's own verb has been decided on the unresolved login (`Sources.mayLook`), so a caller who leads nobody is refused before the directory is asked — and is decided by the authority table on what it resolved to, under the QUESTION'S OWN VERB — a person's queue by the owner-or-lead class through `Sources.Chart`, whose UNKNOWN answers 503 rather than a refusal, and a seat's trail (`conversations`, `agent_memory`) by `audit.seat.read`, its lead or admin — and a refusal carries the rule's `reason` and the `grants` that would have admitted the caller rather than a sentence written beside the rule — and which `New` REQUIRES, because the production wiring once left it out and every lead's read of a report's record was a 503 no retry could clear; it used to be "any operator credential", which made every Tier A token a reader of every seat's inbox. A token acts AS A SEAT only when the identity directory holds an ACTIVE MACHINE row under its login bound to one, and that seat is resolved against the running org through the SAME `session.ResolveSeat` a signed-in person's is (`auth.SeatBindings`) — `403 seat_unavailable` once the running company no longer holds it as a human seat, `503` on a node that runs no company yet — because the lookup it replaced returned the row's raw handle, so a token went on acting as a removed seat; an UNBOUND answer is served even by a stalled node, which is what keeps break-glass reachable — and a seat's contact block names no credential at all, because it says how to reach a person and not which credential they hold. A CROSS-SITE WRITE is refused on its `Origin` before the handler runs, landing BEFORE any cookie-minting route exists rather than after, because the other order leaves a window in which every write on this surface is forgeable — and it judges the SIGN-IN routes the guard exempts (only the server-to-server edges, `/webhooks/`, `/otlp/`, `/mcp/`, are not judged), because exempting them from both left login CSRF open: a form on another site could post an attacker's password or redeem an attacker's invitation and leave the victim signed in as them. THREE CREDENTIAL SHAPES meet at that one frame — a Tier A bearer composed from the configuration alone, a SESSION COOKIE resolved through `internal/iam/session`'s two tables, and a MACHINE TOKEN (`auth.Tokens`, `Options.Tokens`) resolved from one read of the directory to its OWNER, carrying what it was minted with that the owner still holds, and naming itself on the principal's `Via` so every record and audit event it causes carries `pat:<id>` beside the owner — a configuration revision and a stored secret included, which keep the author, its kind and the credential in three columns (`auth.AttributionOf`) where they kept one, and it was the credential; the identity trail included too, a removal and a company-wide invalidation among it — which is also where `auth.PresentedToken` reads it, one carrier rather than a context value beside the principal — the two bearers told apart by the value's SHAPE, which picks the check and never admits anything; `crewlet run` builds the token arm on EVERY node that serves the API, and every one of them holds the estate (`ingress` requires `data`) and applies the directory from boot, company or none, so a token minted on a peer resolves there too — and a node that has not applied the mint yet answers 503 rather than a 401 teaching a pipeline its credential is broken — and the HEADER WINS when both arrive, because a browser sends its cookie whether or not the caller meant to and an `Authorization` header is only ever there because somebody put it there, so a request presenting a WRONG one stays anonymous rather than being upgraded by whatever was in the jar. The node's `api.auth.admin` posture clamps a person exactly as it clamps a token: on a node set to `refuse`, admin is withheld from every credential the guard resolves and every app it includes is kept. A PRESENTED BEARER MEETS NO CURVE, and that is decided rather than missing (`bearers.go`): a bearer names nobody until it is compared, so the only curve that could stand in front of the comparison is one keyed on the ADDRESS, and one was tried — a stranger refusing one guessed value every twenty-five seconds held every valid bearer at the address, break-glass included, at `429` on every guarded route, and a client with a dozen VALID requests in flight met the same `429` because an unresolved attempt counted as a refusal. A curve on `POST /auth/token` alone slows nobody, since every guarded route answers the same guess the same way. What protects a bearer is its VALUE — 32 bytes of `crypto/rand`, a keyring HMAC, a Tier A value config refuses under 26 characters — and what a guess costs is a count on the audit trail's per-minute failure row. And AN UNGUARDED ROUTE NEVER COMPARES ONE (`Guard.resolveUnguarded`): it resolves a session cookie, which the sign-out reads, and only MARKS a bearer, because nothing unguarded acts on whom a bearer names and comparing it there timed a right value against a wrong one — a matching Tier A token reads the directory for its seat binding before it answers — at line rate on `/health`, `/static` and the webhooks, where no refusal is counted. And TWO answers are neither a resolution nor a refusal the routes make — an `auth.Refusal` the guard writes ITSELF about somebody it has RESOLVED (this node knows exactly who they are), each carrying the routes it leaves (`Refusal.Applies`), because the two leave different things: a person — or a bound token — whose SEAT the chart no longer holds is `403 seat_unavailable` naming the seat — 401 would loop a browser through the sign-in page for ever — everywhere but `/auth/`, whose subject is a person's own credential rather than the seat, or an offboarded person would hold a live cookie with no way to sign out; and a session that may only ENROL A SECOND FACTOR is `403 second_factor_enrolment_required` everywhere but the three guarded routes `auth.EnrolmentAdmits` names (reading who it is, the enrolment, a password step-up) and the one sign-out no guard stands in front of, because the rest of `/auth/` regenerates recovery codes and signs a person out everywhere. Where both meet on one person the enrolment refusal holds, being the narrower. The guard also marks every answer under `/auth/` `Cache-Control: no-store` before it resolves anything, ends a session cookie it refuses together with the browser's cache (`session.End`) and carries one it re-issues on with that cache kept (`session.Continue`), since an answer kept by object id is keyed on no cookie. THE HUMAN SURFACES ARE BUILT HERE, by `NewHumanSurfaces` — `/auth`, `/iam`, the human write surface, and the session and machine-token arms — and `HumanSurfaces.Mount` is the one hand-over into `Options`, so `crewlet run` and `internal/e2e` build them through one constructor rather than two wirings that drift; a sign-in surface handed over as a typed nil had mounted `/auth` over nothing on a node with no active company, and `Mount` hands over only one that exists. `Options.Inbox` is REQUIRED: it is the engine's `SetOnInboxMoved`, which the app turns into `inbox_changed` frames naming each movement's `source`, and its `SetOnInboxGap`, which sends every watched seat one naming the source that switched and counting nothing — and every node that serves the API applies every domain's log, so a node that heard nothing would be a wiring mistake rather than a narrower node. ONE SOURCE OF EACH DOMAIN AT A TIME: a node hears its own applier of a domain while its copy of it serves and a sound peer's, relayed over the broker (`engine/inboxrelay.go`), while it is out of service and the node stays in rotation, and every switch is a gap, because what moved during it is not replayed. `Options.Credentials` is REQUIRED for the same reason: it is the engine's `SetOnIdentityMoved`, which the app turns into `stream.Service.CredentialsMoved`, and every node that serves the API applies the identity log, so a node that heard nothing would leave every revoked session's open tab streaming the company. The stream's `SeatOf` is the published company's roster read in memory (`seatOf`, by `org.Organization.Role`), which an open socket acting as a seat is decided by when a company is published. An UNBOUND viewer — `/viewer` and the inbox and My-work callouts that read it — is a Tier A token's session or a service account, never a person (ADR-0032), and its remedy names a HUMAN seat, since a bind to any other is refused. `/health` says whether the company has its FIRST PERSON (`identity`: `ready`, `unclaimed`, or `unknown` where the identity estate cannot be read — never folded into `unclaimed`), through the optional `Identity` seam — because it is the one surface an install with nobody in it can reach, and the first person is invited or created under a Tier A token like everybody after them — onto a human seat the company declares, so on a node with no company the token's session creates the company first — and a created person makes it `ready` as their create lands, before they have spent their first password link: it says SOMEBODY EXISTS, not that somebody can sign in yet; `crewlet run` wires it on every node that serves the API, company or none, over the engine's `AnyPerson`, which PROVES a "nobody" against the identity log's end, because a node that has just joined a fleet holds empty rows until its applier catches up and read bare they told an operator to invite a founder into a company that had one; and it never moves `status`. WHICH OF THE DASHBOARD'S OWN SURFACES EXIST is the org projection's `services`, every reader's: the tracker and the knowledge base the company runs, as the engine resolves them, and whether it hosts its code in the engine (`code`, `native` or `none` — `api.CodeHosts`, the projection's own closed set, since the document says it with `code.native`'s boolean), each held to the dashboard's copy and nothing else of those guarded blocks published. THE NATIVE HALVES ARE READ PER REQUEST (`native.go`: `NativeSources`, `NativeOperatorHalves`; `workapi.Options.Halves`): the human write surface, the operator surface (`/operator/mcp`, `/operator/act`) and the work, page and search questions are mounted on every node and answer `503 no_active_revision` until the node's first company brings the tracker and the knowledge base up, and a company that keeps either on a vendor answers `404 no_route` from then on — they were taken ONCE when the API was wired, so a node handed its first company by apply served none of them until it was restarted. What a request cannot carry is a LISTENING assistant's change of tools, since it makes no request: `OperatorListens` (`operatorlisten.go`) compares what a call would be served after every company the node publishes and every catalogue change a shared tool server makes (`App.OperatorToolsMoved`), tells every `subscriptions/listen` stream on the node through the ONE `operator.Listeners` where it moved, and announces the move to the fleet (`operator_tools_changed`, a nudge kept out of the event store), on which every OTHER node tells its own streams — because nodes apply on their own clocks and a listing reaches whichever node a load balancer picks, so a node telling only its own left an assistant holding a lagging peer's old tools; `App.EndListens` ends every stream as api.port's listener shuts down, and `App.Stop` leaves the fleet's announcements before the engine closes the queue they ride. `/config`'s ORG CHART routes — `PUT`/`PATCH /config`, a revert, and the seat and unit entity routes — are `config.org.write` and `config.org.read` rather than `admin` alone: a unit's LEAD writes and reads what is inside the units they lead, and every write path (dry runs included) is ADMITTED on what it changes between the revision it replaces and the one it proposes, after the document is built and before it is validated or the identity directory is asked who holds a seat it removes (`configapi/admission.go`, over `config.DiffOrg` and `authz/orgchart`), refusing a lead's write whole with a `refused` list naming every part — and a read or a write of ONE seat or unit (`/config/roles/{id}`, `/config/units/{id}`) is decided where it sits FIRST, before the id is looked up or the body read, at the root for an id the revision does not hold, so one outside the caller's subtree and one that does not exist answer the same 403 and only admin learns an id is missing; the engine's own writers (`Apply`, `ApplyEntity`, `Reload`) carry no principal and are not asked. And while Tier A names the document's writers (`api.auth.company_writers`, ADR-0030) every write that CHANGES it is refused `403 config_managed` on the CREDENTIAL — admitted only through a listed Tier A token, its login in `operator_id`, or as the engine itself — after the route's verb and before the body is read, so a person's session, a machine token acting as its owner and a lead's write of their own team are each refused, while a reload and the secret store stay open to whoever the table admits: the managed check composes with admin and the subtree rather than replacing either. ONE route table, and an optional SECOND LISTENER (Tier A `api.public`, whose own `external_url` is the address vendors and sandboxes are given) that serves only the routes outside parties call — `/webhooks/`, `/otlp/` and `/mcp/`, `auth.Public`, the same set the cross-site gate leaves unjudged, so no sign-in route is ever on it — and takes them off `api.port`: both sockets serve the same chain behind one partition that answers the other side's routes 404 BEFORE the guard, so the published socket requires no operator token and a route it does not serve is absent there, as the mux's own 404, whatever credential is sent. The table is a `routeTable` that keeps every pattern mounted, because the partition is a predicate over paths and only the table says which paths exist — the listener cases walk it whole and hold the published set to a reviewed list. A node that sets `api.port` WITHOUT the `ingress` role serves the PROBES alone, still behind the guard — `/health` answers, the dashboard is absent and every guarded route is 401 — so a port opened for a load balancer's health check needs no `api.auth` block and serves nobody the company. The engine's GIT HOST AND THE CODE READS are asked PER REQUEST for the native halves' reason (`CodeSurface`, built by `EngineCode` and required): the git routes and the cache's verify are mounted only where the node's Tier A roles name `git` — a fact of the NODE, known at `New`, never the company's — and the code host's ROWS are QUESTIONS on every API (`code_repos`, `code_repo`, `code_pushes` and a change's `code_changes`, `code_change`, `code_change_routing` and `code_reviews` in `api/queries`, `queries.CodeSource` asked per question; a change's parts read by object id — `files`, `diff`, `findings` placed in a revision — assembled from its rows by the code domain's one rule, `code.RevisionDiff`, `code.PlaceFindings` and `code.ObjectFloor`, which the code tools read by too, its first row read at the caller's freshness and every later one at `session` floored at that read's position; and a repository's CONTENT — `code_refs`, `code_tree`, `code_blob`, `code_log`, `code_blame`, `code_diff`, `code_compare` with its merge preview of the home (`code.merge_preview`) and `code_commit` with the push that introduced it and the changes it belongs to (the reader's `commit`) — every ref a request names resolved in ONE `code.resolve_ref` at the caller's freshness and the home git node asked at the floor of each ref's last move (`code.RefsFloor`, the code tools' rule too), and a question naming WHOLE object ids alone answered `queries.Immutable` from a page its git node cut UNSTAMPED (`code.GitQuery.Unstamped`), so the page is a function of what it names, while one naming a ref or an abbreviation is answered bare with what its read stamped) — and its merge queues and its checks (`codechecks.go`): `code_queue`, `code_checks` (a repository's, a change's, a commit's or the window across repositories, narrowed by a check's name and a state on the rows a page READ beside the grant, never in place of it), `code_check`, one attempt named beside the branch it was checked for (`target`, which a path segment cannot carry), and `code_check_log`, a page of a failed run's log read through `objstore.Store.ReadAt` and cut on a character, `queries.Immutable` only where the request names the log's OBJECT, never by the attempt, whose ending — and log — a later one can replace — served on the `/code/` paths their routes had through the named routes and on no second path, so the REST read, the socket and a screen reading its own code write back at a `session` floor are one implementation — and a SEARCH of their lines, `code_search` (`GET /code/search`), the one scatter over the git nodes (`codesearch`) asked as the caller through `CodeSurface.CodeSearcher` (the engine's own `CodeSearch`), `q` in `codeindex`'s grammar with its mode, case and one filter of each kind beside it, a refusal over one repository naming it (`codesearch.RepoRefusal`), and DELIBERATELY NO `session` FLOOR — `read_level=session` and `min_position` refused, the copies searched as they stand and the caller's level the listing's alone — because it answers at the tip it names (`tips`), so the dashboard's SESSION_QUERIES leaves it out — where they were routes of the code host's own with a parameter reading and a refusal mapping beside the registry's, refusing `session` for want of a caller's position on the code log; a route or a question that finds nothing answers WHY, as the engine decides it from what the FLEET ACTIVATED (`engine.CodeUnserved`, off its reconciler's reading of the activation and its own apply of it) and never from this node's published epoch, which an apply moves only once the code host it needs is built: `404` for a company hosting no code, and a `503` naming `applying` (with the health tick's `Retry-After`), `config_apply_failed` or `native_backend_needs_restart` (with none, since waiting clears neither and another node serves it), the question's in the read surface's vocabulary — `queries.NotFound` (`not_hosted`) and `stream.Unserved` under the 503's own code, the ONE type both transports render a surface this node serves none of now by (the native halves' `stream.ErrNoCompany` is one), so the socket's `unavailable` frame names the code, the words and the wait the REST 503 does — never a sentence contradicting the document a person just applied, nor one stating what it holds where the node never read it (`engine.ErrCodeUnknown`, an apply that failed before the decode: it cannot say whether the company hosts code, and says that); and a VERIFY is an operation, the check running on the host's lifetime and its request held `host.VerifyHold` (a quarter of a second) for it — `200` with a small copy's report, else `202` and its outcome read at `GET /code/repos/{name}/verify/{id}`, by the `deadline` the node states for the pass running — everything decidable without reading the repository refused first, and a failure answered WITH its sentence beside what the check found, since the route is guarded and its reader the operator who asked. The engine's own git host's two prefixes, `/git/` and `/code/`, are guarded like every route, and neither is on `api.public` (ADR-0055: git is one more way a person acts, a person's own token its credential); whether a git request is a FETCH or a PUSH is `auth.GitAccess`'s one answer — read by the guard, the drain gate (a fetch already begun served, a new one refused at its advertisement — `auth.GitAdvertisement`, since a fetch is an upload-pack process tree and a sync from the object store, not a read that starts nothing — and every request of a push refused) and the code host, every doubt a push, and `TestGitAccessIsTheOneClassifier` refuses a second reading of a request's git service anywhere in the module but the host, which reads it only to pick the process — because a fetch is what a drain serves and a credential bound to no seat may do, so two readings that drifted apart would admit a push; and the shutdown FINISHES every git request the drain gate let through (`App.DrainGit`, after the turns' drain and before the listener closes, then `gitExchangeGap`'s five seconds of quiet, git's own keepalive, because git's smart HTTP keeps no state between an exchange's requests; the gate COUNTS a git request before it judges it and takes back one it refuses, so the wait can never look between a push's admission and its count), with no bound of its own for the turns' reason, since a clone cut part way is begun again from nothing on a peer and the listener's five-second grace is a REST read's; Basic is accepted on `/git/` ONLY, its password as the token, because a git client presents a token no other way while a browser replays a Basic credential to every route of the origin; and a git route's refusal is `auth.GitText`'s `text/plain`, the one form git prints, in ONE LINE (git prints each line as a `remote:` line of its own, so a sentence of two panics in a test binary and is folded onto one in production), the guard's 401 a Basic challenge logged at debug when the request presented nothing (the protocol's first request never does). A REST question's parameters take a repeated key's FIRST value, except the keys the grammar reads whole (`tracker.RepeatedKeys`, `min_position`), which `queries.FromQuery` keeps every value of — one floor per log, as a socket frame's array names them — and a question read from more than one log is SESSION_QUERIES-listed under each (`company_feed`: the tracker's and the knowledge base's), holds each half to its own log's floor and refuses a floor on any other, and reports the weakest level a half was read at. Who read a unit's repository is a code question too, `code_repo_readers` on `GET /code/repos/{name}/readers`, for the rows' reason — one implementation on every transport and every node, where a route of the code host's own answered REST alone — decided inside its answer by its own verb (admin's) before anything is read, and answered through the operator tool's one answer (`builtin.ReadersOf`), the usage domain's readers asked per question of `CodeSurface.RepoReaders` (`queries.CodeSource`) on `CodeReads`' terms. `/box/git/` is the PUBLISHED FOURTH PREFIX (`auth.BoxGitPrefix`), exempt from the guard and admitted by the run: a coding box's three routes are mounted on the route table itself, as `/mcp/` and `/otlp/` are, never through the `authz.Router` the person's git routes take — a router decides on the request's `iam` principal, which a box's request never has, so it would refuse every box before the code host's admission ran (`TestABoxRouteIsAdmittedByItsRunNotTheGuard`) — mounted where git is served, classified by the one git classifier and drained by its rules unchanged; Basic is read by the guard on a PERSON'S git route only, so a run's token is never a credential the guard looks at. And a box route on a node serving none of the code host now tells a box WHY only once its request presents a run token this fleet signed (`App.boxGitRoute`; `CodeSurface.VerifyBox`, the engine's own `BoxTokens` over the keyring every node holds, so it verifies with no code host built), answering any other request the code host's own challenge — the box admission's credential step, `host.ChallengeBox`, in the same bytes whatever the reason — because the prefix is public and the reason (no code hosted, an apply in flight, failed or unread, a restart owed) is the deployment's state (`TestAnUnservedBoxRouteAnswersOnlyASignedToken`); a person's routes keep their answers, the guard in front of them. A CHECK'S BOX presents its check's token on the same box routes as `check`, and `CodeSurface.VerifyBox` takes the user name beside the token (`host.VerifyBox`), so each user name verifies its own kind alone (`TestAnUnservedBoxRouteAnswersACheckToken`). `/box/code/` is the PUBLISHED FIFTH PREFIX (`auth.BoxCodePrefix`, `boxcode.go`): a check's box's reads of the ONE commit its check measures — `tree`, `blob`, `diff` — mounted on the route table where git is served, admitted by the code host's check admission (`GitRoutes.AdmitCheck`, whose refusals are its git's one line) and asked of the code domain's git reads (`CodeSurface.GitReads`) as the engine's own read made for the check (`code.GitQuery.Check`) at the check's session; NO READ TAKES A REVISION — the attempt's commit, a diff from its base — and a parameter none takes is refused `bad_params`, never ignored (`TestTheCheckAPIReadsOnlyItsSha`); the answer is the page with the repository, the commit, a diff's base and the next cursor and none of a seat's push provenance, a read's refusal the read surface's envelope; a node serving no code host answers it as a box's git route. The question `inbox` (`GET /inbox`, queries' inbox.go) is the COMPOSED inbox (`inbox.Compose` over the tracker's and the code host's halves): whose by its own verb `inbox.read` with no app, each half read under its own permission and at the floor on its own log. |
| `api/webhooks` | The seven inbound vendor sources — GitHub, GitLab, Jira, Confluence, Slack, Datadog and Atlassian's Forge relay — plus Slack's OAuth landing and the GitHub App return. Six verify a signature over the body; Datadog and Confluence Cloud cannot, because their providers sign nothing, so a shared token IS the authentication there and it has a length floor for that reason (`secrets.CheckSharedToken`). A route whose secret is unset has nothing to verify with and answers 503 — it does not accept the delivery |
| `runtoken` | The signed, self-describing credential a PER-RUN endpoint carries — in its own URL path for the OTLP receiver and the MCP bridge, as git's Basic password for the code host's `/box/git/`. Minting and verifying happen on DIFFERENT NODES of a fleet, so a token has to carry its own subject and expiry rather than be a key into a map. One implementation because it was about to be two, and the two would have had to agree about five decisions nothing compares; the DOMAIN in the key derivation is what stops one endpoint's token validating at the other. A keyring that cannot sign for the fleet is REFUSED (`ErrNoKeyring`) rather than given a per-process key, which validated only where it was minted — that fallback was the posture of a node with no keyring, and there is no such node any more. The version segment is the LITERAL `crewlet_rt1`, on every token it mints (the OTLP receiver's, the MCP bridge's, a coding box's git credential, an artifact view ticket's), which `internal/redact` keys on as it keys on a vendor's prefix, because no node that collects a run holds the tokens its launch minted, so what a box prints is redacted of them by their shape or not at all — the bare `v2` it was is a word prose holds, which no rule could key on; the two packages spell the literal apart, since `redact` imports nothing of the engine, and `TestAMintedTokenIsRedacted` holds them to one spelling. THREE PER-RUN ENDPOINTS mint them: the OTLP receiver's, the tool bridge's and the code host's — a coding box's git credential (`code/host`'s `BoxTokens`), which rides as git's Basic password since git takes no other. Its per-key derivation (`DeriveKey`, `KeyTag`) also yields the embedded broker's route, leaf and operator credentials (`queue/jetstream`'s `FleetAuthFrom`, ADR-0064), so one rotation runbook governs the tokens and the broker |
| `signedask` | THE ONE RULE FOR A REQUEST ONE NODE ASKS ANOTHER TO CARRY OUT over the queue's ephemeral verbs, and its reply (ADR-0031). The broker's admission vouches for a CONNECTION, never a message — an embedded fleet's route credential crosses its network in clear (ADR-0064), and an external cluster admits whom its operator chooses — so a message on a subject a node SERVES is whatever anything the broker admitted wrote — a forged question where the node only answers, an INSTRUCTION where it acts — and four channels act: the estate service (a data node writes as the actor the request names), the fleet broker's membership (a member removes a voter through its own system account), a note to a running turn (the running node offers it as the person it names) and a code search of several repositories (every git node reads the repositories it is assigned as the asker the request names, judging that asker's grant: `codesearch`). Each is a `Channel` over the state log's own frame (`statelog.Signer`), in two key domains of its own (`<name>.request`, `<name>.reply`) so nothing verifies across channels or as a log record; a request carries an id and a deadline INSIDE its signed body and `SealRequest` refuses one without, a serving node remembers the ids it took until their deadlines in a `Guard` of its own (one per node and channel, never shared, because a scatter hands one request to every node), and an asker believes only a reply that verifies AND answers the id it sent — `OpenReply` refuses a reply read against no question, since a refusal bound to no request is exactly what an unbound read would believe. What arrives is one of FIVE dispositions (`Admit`), each handled once by every channel: admitted; tampered (nothing believed, not even the id, logged at error); key unknown (a fact about this node — "did not run", bound to the id read UNTRUSTED from the body); undecodable; stale (unbound, past its deadline, or already taken). It exists because the estate's copy was about to become three: the broker channel and the steer wire shipped unsigned beside a signed estate, and ADR-0031 and SECURITY.md said every instruction was signed. The scatters that only READ (history, search slices, held memory, a sandbox tail) are not channels here. ONE frame that is no instruction is sealed here all the same, ONE WAY (`OneWay`): a presence broadcast, which no node answers and every node displays as a person in a room, carries its expiry inside the signed body in place of an id and a deadline, in a key domain of its own (`artifacts.presence`), and a frame the fleet did not seal shows nobody |
| `api/mcpbridge` | One running seat's tool surface, served to a coding agent over MCP. The dispatch is [tools.Surface] itself — never a second idea of what is granted, what a guard refuses, or what gets recorded. What it adds is the six things that ARE new: a credential a box may hold, a durable call log (a bridged run outlives its turn and its process, so the reviewer's evidence cannot live in memory), a session lifetime, so a box that outlived its run keeps no working key, and an IDENTITY — the per-run token IS the credential, so every call acts as the run's SEAT, set by the bridge per call OVER whatever the request carried: `/mcp/` is exempt from the guard, which therefore resolves every box as anonymous (or, on a laptop, as the `-dev-principal`), the SDK keeps the opening request's context for the whole session, and the turn's own principal defers to any answer already there — so every gated builtin refused the seat's own call as needing a credential. It is overridden HERE and nowhere else, and a session whose surface is bound to no seat is refused at `Open`. And THE MODEL a call was made on: a vendor CLI's own loop made it, in rounds the engine never sees, so every bridged call carries the model the run's launch pointed the CLI at (`Session.Model`, set by `engine.LaunchExecutor` from the executor's run, which it resolves before it opens the session) as `toolloop.CallModel`, set here and nowhere else on this path — a seat's code write over the bridge names on its push the model its run's other writes name. | `api/setupapi` | `/setup`: connecting an integration from the dashboard rather than a shell. It performs no write of its own — a credential goes through the store `/secrets` serves and a pointer through the merge, validation and compare-and-set `PATCH /config` performs — so there is one write path onto the company document and one onto the sealed store. Every route is `admin`'s, reads included (`setup.read`), because the list of which credentials a company has NOT configured is a map of what to attack; CONNECTING is `setup.connect` and asks a recent proof, and wherever a credential is sealed — a submission carrying one (which only the body can say), a provisioning pass, a GitHub App — it asks `secrets.write` too, through the same guard, so a credential is written here on exactly the terms `/secrets` writes one and a step-up the store's own row asks for cannot be skipped by choosing this door. A GitHub App's return carries no credential of ours, so the state the begin route mints is SEALED under the fleet keyring and names who began the creation — the key, the webhook secret and the seat's revision are written as that person, where they used to name `setup`, which is nobody — and sealed rather than signed because it travels through GitHub, a browser history and every ingress log. A write that changes the company document asks the MANAGED document's rule too (`Authorize`, ADR-0030) — at the begin, at a disconnect, and in the App's completion BEFORE the code is exchanged — so a credential the document does not name is refused before anything is sealed, queued or created at the vendor |
| `api/operator` | The company's own tracker and knowledge base — and, where it hosts code, its repositories' read tools (`Halves.Code`, filled per call) — as ONE OPERATOR CATALOGUE, served to the people who run it — because the premise of running this engine is that an AI manages your company, and the person doing that is usually working through one too. The catalogue is BUILT FOR EVERY CALL (`catalogue.go`, `Options.Halves`) from what this node serves at that moment — `503 no_active_revision` before its first company brings the halves up, the route's absence for a company that keeps them on a vendor — because one built when the API was wired served the halves the node had then for the life of the process: none, on the node a company was bootstrapped on. THREE TRANSPORTS reach a tool through its ONE DISPATCH (`Server.Dispatch`) and nothing else — MCP at `/operator/mcp` (`mcp.go`) for an operator's own assistant, the ACT route at `POST /operator/act/{tool}` (`act.go`) for a person at the dashboard, and the human write surface's tool-backed routes (`internal/api/workapi`) for a script or the CLI — because a transport that assembled its own tool set would have a second chance to wire it differently, and a verb one surface offers and another does not looks complete from inside each. None of them decides a schema, a hint, a refusal's wording, its status (`Fail`, `refusal.go` — the ONE mapping from a result's CAUSE, never its sentence, to a status and the engine's envelope) or what is recorded about the call. The tools are `builtin`'s, not a second implementation: ONE field on the deps decides who a write is attributed to (`actor.go`), so a schema, a default and the wording of a refusal are written once. Every tool is decided by the SAME authorizer a seat's registry is built with (`Options.Authorize`, which `New` REQUIRES), for EVERY principal the guard resolved: nobody is refused for being unbound to a seat (ADR-0024), because the table already says what a credential acting as itself may do, and a second rule keyed on the binding would be a second reading of the directory that could disagree with the first — and who CAN be unbound is ADR-0032's: a Tier A token or a service account, never a person. A write carries the name `builtin.PrincipalActor` makes of the request's principal — a person, who always holds a seat, or a token the identity directory binds to one, writes AS the seat, kind `human`, which is what lets the tracker leave them out of the wake their own change sends; a credential nobody is bound through writes under its WHOLE login, colon and all, kind `operator`; and the credential the call came through rides beside either as the operator id (`iam.ActorFor`) — and there is deliberately no way for the caller to name a seat to act as, because a tracker whose author field is chosen by the writer is not an audit trail. The principal is resolved ONCE, by the guard, so the dispatch, the tool's actor and the audit record read one answer, and a rebind or a rename landing mid-call changes only the next request. A person has no turn but does RETRY: the act route and the human write surface take the operation from the `Idempotency-Key` header (`internal/api/opkey`), SCOPED BY THE PRINCIPAL'S ID — never a login, a token label or a session lineage, which a rename or a step-up moves — and every write the call makes derives its id from it; the act route REQUIRES one, answering an absent or malformed key `400 op_id_invalid` naming the header, and refuses an `op_id` argument beside it as a second answer to which operation this is, while an MCP call carries no header, so a tracker write there answers the `op_id` it was made under and takes it back as an argument on a retry. EVERY CALL THAT IS NOT A PROVEN READ IS AUDITED in that one dispatch (`audit.go`): one persisted `operator_acted` event — the caller exactly as every record the call wrote names it (`actor`, `actor_kind`, `operator_id`), the transport, the tool, the operation and the outcome the caller was answered with, never the arguments — on EVERY transport, so what is recorded about a person cannot depend on which surface they used; a verb with no tool publishes the same record (`AuditGesture`), `POST /backup` leaves a `backup_requested` through the same `Audit`, and `New` refuses a surface with no audit publisher. Its own always-guarded prefix rather than `/mcp/`, which is exempt WHOLESALE so a sandbox box holding no API token can reach its seat's tools; a writable company surface there would have sat behind no credential at all. MCP is SERVED STATELESS, so every call is decided by the credential of the request CARRYING it: the SDK's stateful session serves each later request through the context of the request that OPENED it, so a withdrawn grant kept working for as long as the assistant stayed connected and any other credential echoing the `Mcp-Session-Id` acted as the opener — a session id is a handle, not a proof. What a client CAN hold open is a 2026-07-28 `subscriptions/listen` stream — one POST, on the MCP server built for the call that answered it — so every call's server keeps its streams in the node's ONE registry (`Listeners`, `Options.Listeners`), each told through the sending handler of the server it is on, its subscription named as the SDK names it (the SDK announces only a change to a server's own registry, and a call's catalogue never changes once built): told ONCE AS IT OPENS, because a client's first listing races its own stream and may predate a change announced before the stream subscribed; then whenever what a call would be served MOVES, on this node or a peer (`api.OperatorListens`); and ENDED as the node's listener shuts down (`Listeners.End`: each listen answers its result and the POST completes, rather than holding the shutdown for its whole grace and being cut with `api_shutdown_failed`). A client of an earlier protocol has no stream, and sees the change at its next listing. One MCP request's body is bounded at 4 MiB (`MCPMaxBody`, the SDK's own default stated as a promise this surface makes), because the transport reads a body whole before it dispatches it: every call fits far below it but `write_project_file`'s whole file, and a larger file goes up through `PUT /work/files/{project}/{path...}`, which streams its body to the object store. `Halves.Readers` stands beside `Halves.Code`, filled per call on its terms, and serves `code_repo_readers` with the code reads. `Halves.Health` serves the health tools (`fleet_health`, `code_status`) on the same terms, its code half naming the caller as the code reads do; the authority table admits them to an administrator, so a narrower credential is refused at the call. A refusal of one of the CODE HOST's writes names the host's own word beside its class (`refusal`, read off `builtin.HostRefusal` in `Fail`), because several share one class — a head that moved and a train whose base moved are both `stale_version` — and a surface answering each in its own words (the change page's merge controls) must read the word, never the sentence written for a model. |
| `api/secretsapi` | `/secrets`: the company's credentials, written through a running node because the coordination broker is inside its process and listens on no socket (a `crewlet` command run with the engine STOPPED reaches the same store itself, through `engine.OpenFleetStore`). EVERY ROUTE IS `admin`'s, through `authz.Router` and THREE VERBS the one permission admits — the listing and a row's metadata `secrets.list`, every write and the rekey `secrets.write` (a recent proof), and the ONE route that returns a value, which needs an explicit `?reveal=true` and asks `secrets.reveal` on top BEFORE the store is read (a recent proof, and a PERSON present: a machine token is refused it on every arm), and logs the access — three verbs rather than one because what differs between them is the proof and the presence each row asks, and the table decides those rather than a check here that could drift from it. The routes used to decide nothing: the guard answers only whether somebody resolved, which meant "the operator" while a Tier A token was the only credential and meant "anybody" once a person could sign in holding a read-only permission alone. And THE ENGINE'S OWN KEYS ARE UNREACHABLE HERE: every route that takes a name answers a reserved one `403 reserved_name` before anything else, whatever the caller holds — and every refusal here is the engine's envelope, its codes in `httpjson`'s vocabulary with a sentence each, where the surface wrote bare `{"error": …}` maps no screen had words for — and the listing COUNTS them per keyring key (`engine_keys`) without naming any — the blind-index key was once an ordinary name here, which made a DELETE a way to orphan every address in the directory.
