And `POST /secrets/rekey` is the WHOLE rotation: the keyring that seals the bucket also seals every person's name, address and second factor in the identity estate, whose rows only a record can change, so it moves the store's rows at the version it read and the identity estate's through the node's own writer (the `Identity` seam), and the listing counts both (`identity_values`), because the answer an operator retires the old key on is "nothing is under it" |
| `api/stream` | The `/ws/stream` socket: pushes, plus a request/response query channel that is a thin adapter over the SAME function each REST route calls. WHO IS ASKING IS THE GUARD'S ANSWER, read from the context: the handler used to re-read the credential through the Tier A arm alone, so a browser signed in with a session cookie was refused here while every REST route beside it served them — and no identity travels beside the query as an argument, because two identities on one call are two chances to answer about different people. The in-flight query budget is PER SOCKET (`MaxInFlightQueries`, four): a tab's share of the reader floor `internal/store` derives as "two full dashboards at four queries each", so the two are one decision stated twice, and a person's tabs each have their own. A burst past the pool waits in the pool's own queue, and the one read that must never wait behind it — resolving who is acting — runs on the store's reserved connection; that reserve is why the per-PRINCIPAL budget it replaced, refcounted across a person's sockets behind a `sync.OnceFunc` release, was machinery that bought nothing. The HANDSHAKE'S `Origin` is judged by the writes' own rule (`auth.CSRF.RefuseCrossSite`), because a WebSocket handshake is a GET the write gate passes as a read while the socket it opens streams the company's state, and the library's own check compared `Origin` with the request's `Host` — refusing a second hostname in `api.auth.allowed_origins` and every browser behind a proxy that rewrites `Host`. A socket is AUTHENTICATED AT ITS HANDSHAKE AND ENDED BY WHAT ENDS ITS CREDENTIAL (`lifetime.go`), because a handshake decision alone let a revoked session's tab go on receiving the company's state for as long as it stayed open — and never on a timer of its own: the 60-second re-check it replaced read the directory per tab per minute and still let a revocation stand for that minute. Each event is handled the cheapest correct way. An identity move that NAMES the credential — a session's lineage, the person's id, or the login a Tier A token's binding row holds, `token:<id>` (`CredentialsMoved`, every node serving the API hearing its own applier) — decides exactly the sockets it names by the guard (`auth.Guard.ResolveOpen`, a session's idle deadline set aside — a socket can never be re-issued a cookie, and an open live view is activity), each on its own goroutine with a direct read. A move that NAMES NOBODY — the fleet session generation, a record the node retained, a replaced estate, the node passing the stall grace (`identityvouch.go`) — closes EVERY socket 1013 (try again later) WITHOUT READING ANYTHING, and the reconnect's handshake decides: a read per socket there was one identity read per open tab queued at once on the store's one reserved identity connection, and the per-credential decision queue built to soften it (`decisions.go`) is gone with it. A published company is decided IN MEMORY against the roster just published (`Options.SeatOf`): a seat that company no longer holds as a human seat closes 4403, a seat that answers to another handle 1013, and the watch is decided again, with no identity read. The credential's own end is a timer per socket (`auth.Lifetime`), re-armed while the guard still serves it, and every socket is decided once as it starts listening, so a record that landed between the handshake and that moment is not missed. A credential that names nobody closes 4401, a person whose seat is gone 4403, and one this node cannot decide 1013, so the reconnect's handshake answers 503 before any snapshot — there is no `identity` frame and no degraded hold. A decision that resolves SOMEBODY ELSE — another login, another seat or none, other grants — closes 1013 too, so the reconnect's handshake builds the snapshot for the new grants and the tab asks who it is (`viewer`) again: kept open, the socket answered the new principal while the tab went on naming the old one, and a snapshot re-sent in place for a changed audience moved the pushes and left that answer behind. WHAT a socket receives is decided per KIND: every push kind declares the NEED its question takes beside its route (`stream.Need`: `event` is an `events` row, so `admin`'s; `inbox_changed` is the SEAT ROOM's (`NeedInbox`), which names no grant: the watch that put a socket in the room was decided as a read of that person's inbox, and the frame names the domain whose half of it moved, so an app in front of the kind would be one domain's permission before every domain's news; every other company kind is the OVERVIEW, everybody's — ADR-0033), a client carries the AUDIENCE its principal's permissions describe, and what of the overview it receives is decided PER PAYLOAD (`stream.Shaped`) — a seat's live call masked to its status for a reader that seat's trail is not open to, by the rule `audit.seat.read` decides, and the snapshot — and its four REST mirrors, `/agents`, `/org`, `/tools` and `/stream/snapshot` — is built for that audience. It used to decide only that the caller was somebody, which was harmless while every credential was an operator token and a firehose the day a session cookie resolved: a person who could only write work items received every phase's prompt and response. Targeted kinds are ROOM-ROUTED through ONE keyed index (`RoomKey`, `seat:<handle>` or `artifact:<id>`), and a room frame with no room is dropped, never broadcast. `inbox_changed` is the seat room's kind, pushed by `InboxChanged` to the sockets whose `watch` of that seat was ALLOWED — the watch's name RESOLVED first through `iam.OwnerOf` and the same directory `work_inbox` reads, so a login is installed on the record its holder's notices are pushed to rather than on the login as sent (where no frame ever arrives, and on which a lead was refused as leading nobody), and the directory asked only once the watch is decided as far as it can be without it; then decided with `authz.ActionPersonRead`, the rule `work_inbox` takes about the same seat (its holder or whoever leads it, holding the tracker, or `admin`), and three-valued: a refusal is an error frame on a socket that stays open, never a 4403, which the dashboard reads as the whole channel withdrawn; an undecidable answer installs nothing; and every decision of the socket's credential, and every published company, re-decides the watch, withdrawing it only on a refusal. AN ARTIFACT'S ROOM (`rooms.go`) is joined by `artifact_join`, decided like reading the artifact (`get_artifact`, three-valued, through `Options.Rooms.Access`; refused is `not_found`, undecidable `unavailable` with its hint, and neither closes the socket), left by `artifact_leave`, told what the socket is doing by `presence` frames (held to ten a second per socket on the read loop, then handed with the joins to ONE room goroutine per socket so a leave never overtakes its join), and pushed `artifact_changed`, `artifact_data` and `artifact_presence`, each from this node's own appliers and presence hub, never forwarded. A join is DECIDED AGAIN on every decision of the credential and on every share, trash, transfer, purge or reset of the artifact: the movement SUSPENDS the room's datastore pushes on the applier's goroutine and a goroutine of the service's decides each member, withdrawing a refused one with an error frame naming the artifact, keeping an undecidable one, and telling a kept one that missed a push to re-read. A datastore push is SHAPED PER READER (`artifactdata.Readable` over the role each join decided and the reader's record, one encode per set of rows a reader may see, a reader who may see none sent nothing) and names its documents instead of carrying them past `PushFrameMaxBytes`. A node whose artifact copies are out of service withholds its appliers' pushes and tells every room to re-read as it leaves and returns. At most `MaxRoomsPerSocket` rooms per socket. Two walks hold the kinds: every room-routed kind needs a `PushSeat` or `PushArtifact` producer, and the dashboard's `PushKind` union equals the declared kinds in both directions. A refusal on AUTHORITY — a query's or a watch's — carries the rule's `reason` and the `grants` that would have admitted the caller on its `unauthorized` error frame, under the REST envelope's own keys: the app used to reduce it to the question's name on the way here, so the dashboard's live data channel was the one transport that could not say what would change the answer. AN UNSERVED SURFACE IS ONE TYPE (`Unserved`: its own code, its words, and when to ask THIS node again — zero where waiting clears nothing), read by `UnavailableOf` for both transports: a node with no company yet (`ErrNoCompany`, one value of it) was a sentinel with an arm of its own in each transport's mapping, and a code host a node serves none of now was answered over REST alone, by routes of its own — so a REST 503 and the socket's `unavailable` frame could say different things about when to come back A `watch` is decided as the composed `inbox` question about the seat is (`authz.ActionInbox`, own or lead, no app): its frames announce every native domain's notices. |
| `observe` | The observability edge, and the two routes are deliberately different: the STORE is written by a publish listener inline on the publishing node (no consumer group, so no two nodes can write one row); the PROJECTION is fed by an ephemeral per-caller broadcast. A node WITHOUT `data` writes no rows of its own — its store is deleted at every boot — so `Custody` publishes its events in batches onto a durable custody topic instead, buffered so a publish never waits and loud about what a full buffer drops, and every data node's `Keeper` takes them from ONE fleet-wide group. A group delivers a batch whose acknowledgement was lost again, to anybody, so a keeper writes a batch FIRST and claims it create-only in coordination SECOND: the winner keeps it, any other writer deletes its copy, and a crash between the two is settled from the node's `custody_unsettled` rows at its next pass — because a batch in two logs is a stateless node's spend the usage domain bills twice, and claimed first, a claimant that died before writing would hold a batch nobody wrote. A REDELIVERY OF A BATCH THIS NODE ALREADY SETTLED WRITES NOTHING (`custody_settled`, swept with the event log): written again it was in flight again, and its kept rows left every usage count until the claim answered a second time. And a keeper's stop LEAVES the group — stops taking batches, gives one in hand a second to settle, detaches — before the usage publisher's last flush, since every settle as kept makes rows count on this node's days |
| `eventfan` | The fleet's TURN-LEVEL HISTORY, read from every node at query time (ADR-0021). An event is written once, inline, to the store of the node that published it, so no one store is the company's history: `events`, `event`, `event_series`, `trace`, `turn`, `turns`, `phases`, `notification_outcomes`, a seat's history and the live projection's boot seed (its feed, its spend window through `phase_tokens`, each seat's last turn) are asked of every live node over the queue's EPHEMERAL verbs on `crewlet.observe.read` — the asker reads its own store directly and in parallel, and the scatter lasts until every node the roster NAMES has answered, never a count of replies, because every node serves, one the roster does not hold among them (a draining node gives its presence back first and serves until it stops) — and merged, with ONE `coverage{nodes:[{id,answered,error}],complete}` on every answer naming a node that did not answer inside the two-second `FleetReadBudget` (which the `history_partial` alarm borrows). The merges are PURE and each is exact for disjoint stores for a reason it states: a keyset page stops at the newest point any FULL part stopped at (a reply cut to fit the transport is full and short), a histogram's window is pinned to the asker's clock before bars are summed, and `turns` is TWO scatters — every node's page, then every node's share of exactly the turns listed — because the node a turn is selected on is not always the only node holding it. The interface `internal/api/queries` reads it through returns a coverage from every method, so a bare `*store.EventLog` does not compile there. A seat's history is asked by its AGENT ID and never by a role name, and every question the reply is drawn against carries the ONE instant it is answered at (`q.At`), so a node never labels its part by its own clock. A departed node's detail is gone, by design; the aggregates survive it in `usage`. `eventfantest` certifies it on the twin and on a three-member cluster |
| `tokens` | Rolls LLM spend into the breakdown a dashboard renders — by phase, model, provider entry, worker and seat, plus a per-turn tail on the LIVE window only — and its time axis. ONE IMPLEMENTATION is the whole point: this aggregation had three copies once, and a refresh routinely disagreed with the page it replaced. TWO INPUTS, one shape: the live projection's phase records (`Aggregate`) and every NAMED window's company days from the replicated `usage` domain (`FoldDaily`, `BucketDaily`), which is why a named window has no turn and no hour — a day's row holds neither, and inventing them from this node's event log is the one-node answer ADR-0020 replaced. A phase chart draws FOUR bands folded once in `PhaseBand` (a coding run is Execute), a window is at most `MaxSpendRangeDays` (90) and one reaching past the 181-day horizon is REFUSED rather than drawn short. `calls` is PROVIDER CALLS on every bucket (`PhaseCalls` is the one rule both producers count a phase by), and a TURN'S COST is defined here once (`Record.InTurn`): its phases and its in-turn auxiliary spend, never the reflection after it. A leaf in everything but the calendar (`period`), because both the live projection and the query surface need it. A seat's band is keyed on its AGENT ID and a unit's on its KEY, each carrying a `label` drawn from the company as the caller holds it now, because a name is prose two seats may share — keyed on it, two namesakes pooled their spend into one row — and a PERSON's auxiliary spend is a row of its own, keyed on the HUMAN SEAT'S HANDLE, since it was charged to no agent |
| `coalesce` | HOW A NODE PUBLISHES A RECORD IT MAKES MANY TIMES A SECOND, once for every such record (ADR-0008): each occurrence FOLDED into one bucket per key under one lock, every bucket SEALED into one event a flush — its id and instant fixed, so a retry is the same event the store's (time, id) key collapses — and published OFF THE CALLER'S PATH, since no publish ever runs under the lock: a broker electing a leader or short of its quorum holds up no request, where a publish inline waits for a replicated, fsynced acknowledgement, up to the client's five-second default. Flushed every `FlushInterval` (15 s, the usage domain's cadence, held equal by its suite, since every record a ledger carries is counted by a usage day), on an owner's own moment (`FlushWhere`) and at the stop. The first refusal ends a flush, and what it held back is kept, MERGED BY SEAL ORDER — never put in front, which once made the bound drop a turn's fresh records as the oldest — up to `MaxPending` (4 096, under three megabytes), past which the oldest is dropped and said with what it held. Two owners, `auxspend` (auxiliary spend) and `code/readaudit` (who read a unit's repository), each saying only what its records are (`Records`: the key, the seal, what a lost one costs) |
| `auxspend` | What the seats' AUXILIARY model spends, recorded — the `Use` every caller of the auxiliary seam states (stage, purpose, turn; the zero value REFUSED), the per-turn `Tally` a turn's work item is charged from (ADR-0022), the turn's own `Budget` meter a turn-stage call is charged THROUGH and asks before it is made — because a post-charge beside the meter left the window it filled unseen, and the turn's next round was sent, billed and refused — and the node's `Ledger`, which coalesces each call into one `auxiliary_spend` record per (stage, seat or person, turn, purpose, model, provider entry, company day) per flush. RECORDED INDEPENDENTLY OF THE COUNTER at the one seam that charges it, because the counter exists only where a coordination store and a budget scope do, and a figure a person reads must depend on neither. The ledger is a `coalesce` ledger, so its fold, flush, sealed backlog and stop are that package's: flushed every 15 s, for a turn before its end is published and before its reflection pass's sentinel, and at the stop — ordered after every producer of auxiliary calls and before the custody flush and the stream close — and a refused record kept sealed up to `coalesce.MaxPending`, so a process that dies during a broker outage loses that backlog as well as its open buckets |
| `tracing` | The OpenTelemetry wiring: ONE TracerProvider, built from the standard `OTEL_*` environment (never a Tier A block), and the conversion between OTel's `context.Context` carrier and the `trace_id`/`span_id`/`parent_span_id` the event envelope has always had. The provider is installed UNCONDITIONALLY and only the exporter is optional, so ids reach the event store whether or not a collector exists — and its own `init` installs a working one, because OTel's built-in default is a no-op that passes the parent's span context through and would silently reinstate the bug this package removed |
| `notify` | The backend-neutral notification spine — conversation keys, digest coalescing, party resolution, the rate valve, and BOTH DIRECTIONS across a chat surface: the working indicator writes, and `ThreadReader`/`ThreadReaders` read a thread back for the turn-start block. Each direction is a per-backend seam with a fan-out that routes on the trigger's own `transport`, because a company runs two chat surfaces and a caller holding one backend's reader silently reads none. `ThreadOf` is deliberately NOT `ConversationOf`: one answers where a reply lands (a top-level message anchors on its own id), the other what was already said (a message with no thread has no answer). Built before any vendor sat on it, because a spine built after its first vendor has that vendor welded into it. A human seat's contact identities are registered only while the person the IDENTITY DIRECTORY binds to it may be reached: a `Standing` is ONE reading of the directory, applied whole to the registry being built, and its zero value is CHART-ONLY, which no running node builds from: a node that HOLDS THE ESTATE reads its OWN identity rows, from boot, company or none, and a node WITHOUT `data` reads the same reading through the estate router (`SeatHolders`, floored on the identity log, so a data node behind answers unknown rather than an old binding) — because routing by the chart attributes a suspended person's word to their seat for every delivery a node wins, and a node that has NEVER read a standing withholds every human seat until it does. The zero value is what an engine with no directory at all builds from, which is `crewlet validate` and a test. A binding names its seat by its handle (ADR-0013), so a reading finds the seat exactly as the request path finds the same binding's seat. What AGENTS are shown follows the same reading: a turn pins the live registry's withholding beside its org (`turnctx.Turn.Withheld`), so a lead's roster and `lookup_colleague` leave out exactly the people an inbound message would not be attributed to, and neither says why. `active`, `invited` and `enrolling` route; `suspended`, `retired`, a removal and a stage this build cannot name withhold, and an unreadable directory is an error rather than a reading, so a caller never hands every suspended person's seat back to the chart over an outage. The CHAT PROMPT reads the same addressed rule the indicator and the delivery check do (`addressOf`), and on an addressed message states that verdict IN PLACE OF the triage: the triage tells an unaddressed seat that silence beats noise, which on an addressed message is the one answer the decoder refuses. A FIRST-PARTY notice STATES who wrote its change (`Inbound.Writer` onto the wake and each coalesced constituent, `FirstPartyWriter`): the tracker and the knowledge base have no namespace an id resolves in, and the KIND their record holds says whether the actor is a seat's handle, a credential's login or a node's name — never the spelling, which is the same for all three |
| `backup` | A restorable copy of everything a node holds, taken from INSIDE the engine because that is the only place both estates are reachable: the store file is locked to this process and the driver refuses a second opener, and the embedded broker binds no socket for any outside tool to dial. The store copy is `VACUUM INTO` (the backup API is stubbed and plain `VACUUM` is behind an experimental flag) taken by `store.DB.Backup` as ONE MOMENT holding every commit that returned before it began — which the trim hold leans on, since it pins the log at the position the node stood at just before the copies, and a copy older than that would leave the records between them pinned by nothing — and is VERIFIED before it counts. A copy holds its file's write-ahead log for its whole run, so before it pins or copies anything a backup checks each database's own volume has room for the log its copy will hold back there (`store.DB.CopyHoldsBack`, the last copy's measure), and refuses otherwise: the node commits to that volume and would stop once the log filled it; the copies themselves land in the operator's directory and are not counted there, since nothing tells two volumes of one pool apart and counting them on every live volume would refuse every backup to a disk of its own. The streams — mailboxes and every coordination bucket alike, since a bucket IS a stream — go over the JetStream wire API, because the vendored client has no snapshot call at all. The MANIFEST IS THE CLAIM: written last, and a directory without one is the debris of a run that did not finish rather than a partial backup. The objects the copy's Required rows name are WALKED (a Retired or Derived row's object is never copied, counted or asked after — on `nats` its bytes ride the bucket's snapshot uncounted, ADR-0051 — and each walk is built for `objstore.PassBackup`, so one handed such a table refuses it), never gathered — `objstore.MergeWalks` over the copy, a page of each table held, a key two rows name de-duplicated against the one before — and carried thirty-two at a time on `s3` (`backupFetchConcurrency`: a first copy is bound by each GET's first byte rather than the link, so the depth is Little's law over S3's 100–200 ms, measured at its lower end — `BenchmarkCarry` measured the large model's first copy at about five hours sixteen wide and three thirty-two wide, a 1 Gbit/s link's worth, and at the upper end it is about four (arithmetic) — under a twentieth of the prefix's GET rate and at the bucket's own transport's warm connections — s3obj's clone of httpx's, HTTP/1.1, `httpx.MaxIdleConnsPerHost` of them) or asked after thirty-two at a time on `nats` (`streamCheckConcurrency`, the audit's depth), each a closed loop that the first object the store still does not answer for after `fetchAttempts` (four, within one read's budget, in the same slot) ends. THE OBJECTS FOLLOW THE LOG (ADR-0052): a restore brings the copy back at P and replays each declaring log to the last sequence L its snapshot captured — the snapshot is point-in-time at the state its answer states — so the backup also reads (P, L] of each log, and the records the copy's applier deferred, through each declaration's `Records`, and carries every object they name; it never waits for an applier or reads the live rows, whose read would land past L. A record it cannot read that may write a declared table refuses it (`objstore.ErrRecordUnreadable`, `409 record_unreadable`), judged from what every build reads of the record, never from what this build's applier does with its kind; every record is read through the verified log, so one signed under a key this node does not hold refuses it too (`statelog.ErrRecordUnverified`, `409 record_unverified`) and one no key of the fleet opens is a `500`; a log trimmed past the copy or a hole between P and L (`ErrLogGap`, `503 log_gap`), or a copy keyed to an earlier instance of the log (`409 log_recreated`), refuses it too, each answered with a hint saying what to do; the object stream is snapshotted after every log; and an object it could not carry is LOST only where the restored rows name it. ONE AT A TIME PER NODE (`ErrBackupRunning`, `409 backup_running`): the trim hold is keyed on the node, so two at once would share one pin and the first to finish would release the other's. `backuptest` restores a backup's stream snapshots into a bare broker — the runbook's `nats stream restore`, for the suite that proves a restored node serves what it backed up — handing each stream back the configuration its snapshot recorded, verbatim, because the stamp in it is the stream's identity (`statelog.CreatedAtKey`) |
| `maintenance` | The retention sweep for the tables that answer "recently" rather than "ever" — fire claims, channel membership, counterparty profiles, the conversation ledger, each state-log domain's OPERATION LEDGER, and the coordination store's REMOVAL MARKERS (a bucket no clock ages keeps every delete marker, and every listing that meets one re-reads it from the leader) and the PARTS a detached run keeps beside its record once that record is gone (`SandboxRunPartJobs`: a write that raced the run's end leaves one in a bucket no clock ages, a conversation's megabytes among them, at the markers' horizon and for their reason). Every one of their migrations says they are swept and ships the index a range delete needs — a table with a documented retention and no sweep grows for the life of the deployment, which is what the `<domain>_ops` tables did until `StatelogJobs` existed. A ledger horizon is the DOMAIN's, and a domain may declare a SHORTER one per subject KIND whose writers never re-ask late (`OpsHorizon.Kinds`, a job of its own) — the identity estate's sessions go in an hour where everything else keeps the month, a range over the stored subject on an index only that domain ships. Every horizon is a FUNCTION read at each sweep (`Horizon`, `Fixed` for a constant), so a retention a revision changes is the next sweep's rather than the next boot's. A fleet singleton, because N nodes scanning the same rows is waste rather than corruption — except where the rows are a table each node owns its own copy of, which is what a job's `Scope` of `NodeLocal` is for: swept under the singleton, such a table is tidied on one node and grows for ever on every other, and that looks exactly like a sweep that works to whoever checks the node it ran on |
| `mattermost`, `slack`, `gitlab`, `github`, `jira`, `confluence`, `datadog`, `atlassian` | The vendors. Each contributes only what is genuinely its own: a client, a parser, a transport, a prompt, a provisioning reconcile — and no more, which is why Jira has no transport (an agent writes through its own MCP tools) and why its reconcile and GitHub's report rather than mint (neither vendor issues a credential on a provisioner's behalf). Datadog is the ALERT one and the shape shows it: a monitor is addressed to nobody, so it routes on the monitor's own TAGS with a company-wide fallback seat, and its deliveries cannot be signed at all. Slack is the one with no reconciler — its apps are created from the command line — so it is registered teardown-only and carries no reconcile status. A vendor's PROMPT states only what the engine knows and the model cannot — why the seat is reading this, the item by the reference a person would paste, the vendor facts (mention markup, subscription mechanics, what a merge or a close means) and how a seat declines what it HOLDS on that surface (reassign the item assigned to it, hand a review request on) — never offered to a seat asked about somebody else's item, which is not its to move — while "stay silent" is shown only to a seat the prompt's own `Addressed` reads as NOT asked. How to review a diff or work an alert is the model's own, and that an ask may not end in silence is the executor's contract and the decoder's refusal, so neither is restated per vendor |
| `integration` | The app-neutral reconcile spine, and the reason there are eight vendor packages rather than eight loops. It owns the vocabulary a pass reports in — `Finding`, its severity ordering, and the `Phase`/`Actor` a `Report` classifies to — the schedule that follows WHO HAS TO ACT rather than what failed, the fleet-singleton `Worker` that runs every registered surface, and the disconnect intent a teardown is driven by — WHO ASKED included (`State.RequestedBy`), because the loop carries the teardown out minutes later on whichever node holds its duty, and the revision that drops the block was recorded as the loop's own while a forced disconnect recorded the person. It also decides the TOOL ROLL-UP (`Rollup`) — one state per tool the `integrations` answer carries — because a screen that re-derived it kept a second copy of the phase order and drifted. `integrationtest` is its certification suite: one set of cases every reconciler passes, in the `queuetest`/`coordtest` tradition, because a promise about what a pass does to somebody's Slack workspace written down and checked by nobody is the worst shape a rule can be in |
| `fleetsecrets` | The company's credential store: this package owns the KEY, coordination owns the BYTES. THE ONLY STORE: there is no node-local half for the engine to carry here at boot — a `crewlet` command run with the engine stopped writes this store too, through the node's own broker (`engine.OpenFleetStore`), because a command that read a table of the node's own with the engine stopped read every value the fleet held as unset, and a provisioner mints over unset. TWO VIEWS OVER ONE BUCKET: `Store` is the OPERATOR's, and it neither lists, snapshots, reads, writes nor deletes a name in the engine's own namespace (`secrets.Reserved` — `iam/…`, the identity directory's blind-index key), answering `secrets.ErrReservedName`; `Estate` is the ENGINE's, and it addresses those names and nothing else. They shared the environment-variable grammar once, which made the engine's key listable, revealable, deletable and decrypted into each node's `${VAR}` snapshot on every apply. A REKEY is the one gesture that crosses — the keyring is one keyring — and it COUNTS the engine's rows rather than naming them, and leaves every row's provenance as it found it: it re-seals a value it did not choose, and stamping its own caller over each row made every credential in the company read as set by whoever rotated the keyring. The engine's view writes its key CONDITIONALLY, `Estate.Create` only where none is, so two minters keep one key; and a REKEY re-seals every row at the version it read, because a plain put of what it read reverted an operator's rotation that landed in between |
| `compact` | Making text fit a budget by having a model REWRITE it, never by cutting it. A cut keeps whatever came first and drops the rest unread, which for text a model acts on is a different message: a comment cut at its cap reads as a comment that ended there, a runbook cut at four kilobytes as a runbook with no step four, an error page cut at two hundred runes as its HTTP preamble without the line naming the missing scope. So where a bound is genuine — a block re-sent every round, a document bigger than the model answering from it can hold — the over-budget text goes to the seat's AUXILIARY chain (`llm_auxiliary`, falling back to `llm`) through the same metered seam the learning workers use, and comes back rewritten to fit with identifiers verbatim. WHETHER a bound is needed is the caller's question, and most of what used to be cut needed none: something upstream already bounded it, or a reader could page through it whole. WHAT TO DO WHEN NO REWRITE CAN BE HAD is the caller's too — drop whole entries and say how many, name a payload by size and digest (`Omitted`), carry a draft whole — and the one fallback no caller may take is the cut this replaced, which is why `Fit` returns an error rather than a degraded string. Text that already fits costs no call; a rewrite is cached by its input in the engine's one `Cache`, so a block re-rendered every round pays once; an input past `MaxChunks` × `ChunkBytes` is refused (`ErrTooLarge`) rather than summarised at a cost nobody priced |
| `textcut` | Shortening a string without breaking it. It replaced FOUR helpers — three named `truncate`, in `agent/extension`, `learning` and `providers/llm/cliagent`, plus `learning`'s own `preview` — which were four copies of one rule already disagreeing on the easy half: two appended `…` and two appended `...`. A fifth, the sandbox runner's private rune-counted tail, became `Tail` — the one cut that keeps the END, for a process's account of itself. The hard half is that a plain `s[:n]` is invalid UTF-8 whenever a multi-byte rune straddles the cut, which a JSON encoder substitutes, a model reads as a replacement character and a vendor rejects. BYTES ONLY, because cutting is the last resort rather than the first: content a turn reasons over is passed whole, and a value with a vendor limit is REFUSED naming the field rather than silently cut to fit. Text a model or a person acts on is never cut here: where a bound is genuine it goes to `compact` and is rewritten to fit, is refused naming the limit, or is described by its size. What is left is a diagnostic or a process's tail past a transport ceiling, a MARKED preview of something the reader can open (a search snippet, an inbox excerpt), an identifier with a length limit, which keeps a digest of the whole beside the cut, and EMBEDDING INPUT, which no reader sees — bounded by the model and cut, unmarked, by `providers/embeddings`' rule |
| `jsoncarry` | How a record ROUND-TRIPS through a build that only half understands it: a decode keeps the fields the struct has no home for, and the next encode folds them back in. ONE CLAUSE is the whole package and it is the one a second implementation gets backwards — A CARRIED FIELD LOSES TO A KNOWN ONE, because with the precedence the other way round a stale carried copy silently reverts the write that set the field, on exactly the path an upgrade is in progress on. It was written TWICE, in `pages` and the org chart's domain (since removed), and the copies had already drifted — one decoder carried a fourth parameter the other did not have and neither read — which is `textcut`'s, `whsec`'s and `jsprovision`'s story arriving for a fourth time; a fifth state-log domain would have made three copies of a precedence rule nothing compares. THE KNOWN NAMES ARE READ OFF THE STRUCT'S TAGS (`Names`), by encoding/json's own rules, and never off a marshalled zero value: a zero value does not marshal an omitempty field, so every domain had to list those names by hand, four had been missed (a field on a person, on an invitation, on a unit and on a seat), and each missed field was decoded AND carried, so clearing it to its zero value re-encoded the stale carried copy straight back. A LEAF over `encoding/json` and `reflect` |
| `httpx` | The ONE outbound HTTP transport. Leaving `http.Client.Transport` nil is not "the defaults" — it is `http.DefaultTransport`, whose `MaxIdleConnsPerHost` is **2, process-wide**, which seven vendor clients were silently sharing. One transport rather than one per client: a per-client clone is strictly worse than the default, since each keeps a private pool and reuses nothing between them. EVERY outbound client is built here, the operator CLI's included — a bare `&http.Client{}` outside this package is the bug, and a round tripper that wraps another takes `httpx.Transport()` as its base, which is the shape that slips through because it names no client at all. `Customized` is for a client whose TLS or protocol genuinely differs — the bucket's, over HTTP/1.1 alone. It also holds the ONE PUBLIC-ADDRESS RULE, which a dialer reaches through one of two doors. `NewRule(resolver)` returns the `Rule` on the network of the resolver a host is resolved with (the zero `Rule`, and a nil one, refuse every address), whose `Public(ctx, addr)` a dialer that makes its own connections asks of the very address each connection is made to — the CDN fetch's in its `net.Dialer`'s `ControlContext`, before the connect, so `net.Dialer` still resolves the name, races its address families and splits its bound across them, and a refused address is sent nothing. `ResolvePublic(ctx, resolver, host)`, for a dialer that hands an address to another program to connect to, returns a host's addresses only when every one is public, and `ErrNotPublic` otherwise. An IPv4 address is public unless it is in a block IANA's special-purpose registry reserves (private, loopback, link-local with the cloud metadata address, carrier-grade NAT, documentation, benchmarking, the deprecated 6to4 relay anycast) or multicast, and one written in IPv6 form is judged as itself. An IPv6 address that carries a zone (`fe80::1%eth0`) is refused before any block is asked: no host on the internet is named with one, and netip's own predicates judge a zoned address as if it had none, so a rule judging its bits alone would step around every block. An IPv6 address in a NAT64 prefix is judged by the IPv4 address it carries and by nothing else: the well-known `64:ff9b::/96` always, and the network-specific prefixes RFC 7050 discovery reads from the same resolver's answer for `ipv4only.arpa`. That lookup is made at most once a dial or a resolve, never kept, and ONLY for an address whose verdict its answer can change: an IPv6 one in global unicast or in `64:ff9b:1::/48`, where a named prefix counts (below). No record means none; any other failure refuses each address that needed the answer, with the resolver's error, so the rule is never applied on a guess. An IPv4 address, one in the well-known prefix and one the allowlist refuses elsewhere are judged without it, so an IPv4-only host costs no lookup beyond its own and is never refused for a lookup it did not need; `ResolvePublic` refuses a host one of whose addresses is not public with `ErrNotPublic` even when another could not be judged, whatever order the resolver answers in. A prefix the resolver names counts only where its word cannot widen the rule into a network that is not on the internet: inside the allowlist below, where judging an address by what it carries can only refuse more, or inside `64:ff9b:1::/48`, the block RFC 8215 reserves for a network's own translation prefix. Named anywhere else (unique-local, link-local, documentation, `::/96`) it is ignored and its addresses stay refused, so a DNS64 network translates through `64:ff9b::/96`, a prefix in `64:ff9b:1::/48` or one in its own global unicast space, or its dialer reaches the host through a proxy its caller trusts: through a translator on a unique-local prefix the rule reaches no host that has only an IPv4 address. Any other IPv6 address is public ONLY in global unicast (`2000::/3`) outside `2001::/23`, the two documentation blocks and 6to4: an ALLOWLIST, because a list of reserved IPv6 blocks cannot be completed (IANA keeps reserving more) and missed every form that carries an IPv4 address (NAT64, 6to4, Teredo, IPv4-compatible). Every dialer that refuses an address asks it rather than keeping a list of its own, which would drift from the others unnoticed; the dialer stays with its caller |
| `api/httpjson` | How every JSON surface answers. Four byte-identical `writeJSON` copies had already drifted into three spellings of one 413, and five sites handed `http.Error` a JSON literal — which sets `text/plain` **and** `nosniff`, the one combination that stops a strict client parsing it. EVERY CODE IS ON ITS TABLE WITH A SENTENCE, and every 503 goes through `Unavailable` / `UnavailableWith`, which write the `Retry-After` and the envelope together — ZERO meaning not retryable, because a node missing its keyring has none after any wait and a header saying "come back" teaches a client to hammer it. A state-log refusal gets its seconds from `statelog.RetryAfter` through `RetrySeconds` (rounded UP) on every surface — zero for one waiting cannot clear (an evicted node, a record this node cannot decode, a full log), the refusal's own derived hint where it has one — because two surfaces each turned those into `Retry-After: 2` and a client polled a node that would answer nothing until somebody readmitted or upgraded it. `/config`, `/secrets` and `/setup` had built their own `{"error": code}` bodies with no sentence, `/setup` had minted a dozen codes of its own (two of them second spellings of codes the table held), and a dozen 503s said "come back" without saying when; a source walk in `internal/api` now holds those surfaces, `/work` and `/webhooks` to all three rules — the webhook edge had answered `{"error": "invalid signature"}`, a code with a space in it, and a `{"status": "unavailable", "reason": …}` envelope of its own. EVERY 429 goes through `Throttled`, which carries the wait as a `Retry-After` ROUNDED UP and never below a second — the sign-in surface answered `throttled` with no header, so a client retried at once into the same refusal and a person read a lockout where there was a wait of seconds — and a walk over every file under `internal/api` refuses the status or the code spelled anywhere else. AN ANSWER THAT NEVER CHANGES (`Immutable`, a question's answer whose BYTES what its request named determine — whole object ids and its arguments, never what the read that answered it stamps: a copy's position, a memo, an index's pushers and coverage, or a page cut to the room they leave, each a new tag on every read and a year of whatever it said once; `queries.Immutable` states it, and a question answering one carries the test that asks it twice and wants the same bytes) is sent with a STRONG entity tag of its own encoded bytes — never a name for the request, so another build's other field is a new tag and a `304` never confirms a body the caller was not sent — `ImmutableCacheControl` (`private`, since it was decided for the credential that asked, a year, `immutable`, so a reload does not revalidate it) and `Vary: Authorization` — the bearer a client sets, NEVER `Cookie`: a served session's cookie is re-issued every five minutes of use, so a key holding its bytes missed every kept answer minutes after keeping it, and who a browser is signed in as is kept apart instead by `session.Begin` and `session.End` emptying its cache (`Clear-Site-Data: "cache"`) — and a request whose `If-None-Match` names the tag (the weak comparison, a list or `*`, PARSED rather than split on commas, which an entity tag may hold) is answered `304` with no body |
| `api/opkey` | The HTTP half of an OPERATION ID: the one header a caller sends a key in (`Header`, `Idempotency-Key`), the rule a key it sends is held to (`Key` — the caller's, held to `statelog.CheckCallerOpID`, or one minted through `statelog.NewOpID`; refused `400 op_id_invalid` naming the header by `Refuse`), and the DIGEST of the request — its path, its query and its body re-encoded from the decoded value — that `/iam` steps a key by, so a key sent with ANOTHER request is another operation rather than the first one answered back from the ledger with nothing of the second written. Two write surfaces, the human write surface and the CLI each declared the header, the two each carried the digest byte for byte and all of them the key rule and its refusal, each commenting that it matched the others, which nothing compared — a retry is one script whichever surface it wrote to. What a write is published under beyond the binding stays each surface's, because what a write is ABOUT is the surface's vocabulary. A walk refuses the header's literal in any other non-test file |
| `api/paced` | THE PER-MEBIBYTE PACE a client's connection is held to, both ways, and ONE PACKAGE for every surface that streams to a client (a project's files in `api/workapi`, an artifact's uploads and downloads in `api/artifactsapi`, the code host's git transfers), so no two surfaces hold one client to two paces; the server's own read and write timeouts are deliberately unset, which is why a route that streams has to bound itself — `Body` for a request, `Writer` for a response written as it is produced and FLUSHED AT EVERY WRITE (a git process's keepalives must reach the client when they are written, or a balancer's idle timeout cuts a transfer that is merely working), and `Send` for a stored file, which is a `Writer` fed pooled 64 KiB pieces (a piece is what every download in flight holds, a slow one for hours, so its size is chosen on memory against a measured CPU cost, never on the pace). `objstore.MiBPace` a mebibyte, charged only for the time spent inside the connection's own reads, writes and flushes — never the server's work between them — each given what is left of its mebibyte's budget as its deadline; a write that would cross a mebibyte's end is written as two pieces, each under its own mebibyte's budget, because a write returns only once all of it is taken where a read returns what has arrived; and a response is ENDED through its Writer (`End`, deferred by the handler), because net/http writes a chunked response's terminator after the handler returns, under the deadline in force, and the last write's would charge the handler's own work to the client. Lifted from the file routes when the code host needed the same rule; `TestOnePacingImplementation` refuses a deadline set on a CLIENT'S CONNECTION anywhere else under `internal/api` and `internal/code/host`, judged by what it is set on and never by the method's name — an `*http.ResponseController`, an `http.ResponseWriter` asserted to a deadline interface, or a hijacked connection followed inside the function that took it (one that leaves is reported, and so is a receiver the walk cannot type), each typed by `sourcetree/decltype` from `net/http`'s and `net`'s own declarations rather than from a list of names this gate keeps — so the hook relay's handshake bound on a socket it accepted is no pace. Its exemptions are FUNCTIONS, never files — this package's `Read`, `Write` and `End`, and `httpjson.ReadBody`'s one bound over a small body, which is not a pace — so a second loop written beside either is a finding, and each exempt function must still set one |
| `api/pagepolicy` | What a BROWSER is told about every response: the Content-Security-Policy, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, and HSTS on https. It matters here because the engine also serves two UNAUTHENTICATED pages rendering what an arriving browser supplies — the GitHub App and Slack OAuth landings, on the dashboard's own origin while one listener serves everything and on the public listener's once `api.public` is set — and a policy is per response, so one on the shell alone left exactly those as the way in. `Apply` sets the API policy (`default-src 'none'`) on EVERY response BEFORE routing, so the mux's own 404 and 405 and the redirect from `/` are covered by construction and a page handler REPLACES it; `Set` runs before anything writes a status, because a header set after `WriteHeader` is silently dropped — which is how a 304 from the asset handler went out with none of the headers its 200 carried. A templated page is allowed its inline blocks by their sha256 hashes taken from the template RENDERED (`ForTemplate`), never from its source, because html/template strips comments inside a block and a hash of the source refuses the page's own script. HSTS follows the address the LISTENER is published at — `api.external_url` for api.port, `api.public.external_url` for the public listener and the bridge-only one — and never the bind address or `r.TLS`, since the engine sits behind a TLS-terminating proxy; one flag for both sockets sent none on the landings a deployment had published over https beside a private http admin side: a YEAR, with no `includeSubDomains` and no `preload`, because this engine is one host and has no business committing its neighbours under the same domain to https. The ARTIFACT ORIGIN's policies live here too, so every policy the engine writes reads side by side: `ArtifactDocument` (a nonce fresh to the response with `'strict-dynamic'`, published handlers by digest under `'unsafe-hashes'`, the artifact's own origin as the only place it connects to or loads images, media, fonts and styles from (scripts are trusted by nonce, and a script the page's own script creates loads from any host, so the policy bounds what a viewer's row can run and not what the page's author can send elsewhere; the package comment states that residual), object, base, form and frame sinks `'none'`, the frame's own sandbox, every device feature denied, no COEP ever, CORP by origin mode), `ArtifactSubresource` and `ArtifactPublic`; and `DashboardFor` is the dashboard's policy with a `frame-src` naming that origin. So is the ONE gzip negotiation (`AcceptsGzip`, `GzipTag`, `GzipSmaller`) the dashboard's assets and the artifact origin both answer by |
| `artifactupload` | How bytes become a version, and THE publish pipeline every surface calls. BYTES ARE RESERVED BEFORE THEY MOVE: an intent decides paths, types, limits, carries and quota first, mints one key per new file at the engine's clock and holds the bytes in the fleet's reservation ledger (`coord.UploadReservations`, one compare-and-set record, per principal and four times that per company) until the collector could have deleted them, so unnamed bytes never exceed the holds; the pipeline's own objects (a linted page, an edit's result, a copied CDN file, inline content, a copy of the adopted brand's logo or face) are reserved the same way; a brand kit's logos are stored sanitised (`brandformat.Sanitize`), and an ADOPTION copies every file the kit serves, re-sanitised, beside the stylesheet generated once, under one reservation, after refusing an unreadable text colour before a byte moves. FINALIZE READS BACK BEFORE IT DECIDES, on the engine-streamed leg (a put once under a claimed key) and on a presigned S3 PUT bound to exact length, type and digest alike, and holds every file to its type's leading bytes. Then edits against the base's source, the linter, vendoring, search text and the runtime contract, and the domain's decide. A REFUSED PUBLISH LEAVES NOTHING BEHIND (its own objects deleted, the upload open again) — a full object store among the refusals (`StoreFull`, reason `store_full`, its surfaces' 507), never a failure held for a retry, since the same publish meets the same full bucket until an operator makes room — yet judged a BAD `publish_success` event, the deployment's refusal rather than the client's, so a full bucket burns the objective; a landed one deletes what nothing names; an agent's turn is held to the D28 guardrails before a byte moves |
| `artifactupload/edits`, `artifactupload/lint`, `artifactupload/vendor` | Pure exactly-once `old_str`/`new_str` edits against the base version, overlapping matches counted; the publish-time policy linter (allowlisted script and stylesheet hosts at exact versions, the nonce placeholder written into every script element and the rewritten page verified by a second tokenize, inline handler digests, eval flags, remote module imports refused, every import map entry on another origin copied in or refused, warnings); and allowlisted CDN files fetched once per node over https through a dialer that connects only to an address the network's `httpx.Rule` calls public (its NAT64 prefixes asked of the resolver the dialer resolves the CDN's name with, at most once a dial and at the socket of the first address whose verdict they can change, so an IPv4 address waits on no lookup and a failed lookup refuses only the addresses that needed it), judging each at the socket `net.Dialer` opens for it (so a name's IPv6 and IPv4 addresses still race, an address that never answers costs a share of the dial's bound rather than all of it, and a name answering both ways is dialed at its public addresses alone), integrity-checked, then copied into the artifact's own version, so a view fetches nothing third-party and no object is shared across artifacts |
| `artifactserve` | The artifact ORIGIN, the `api.artifacts` listener, and the frame builder the API mints a viewer's frame through: five routes (a ticket's file, its artifact's asset, the embedded runtime and kit, and the adopted brand's files by hash, served only while an adoption names them) and nothing else, no guard, no session, no cookie read or written (a source walk holds the package to it), no API route, ever. A path-borne view ticket names one principal, one immutable version, the CREDENTIAL it was minted through and the artifact's access epoch; in either origin mode the request's host must be the one the ticket's artifact is served on, and a top-level open is refused. DOCUMENTS ARE DECIDED ON EVERY REQUEST and never read a remembered verdict: the principal re-composed from the identity rows by `api/auth`'s `Lineages` (the guard's own arms, over the credential's NAME), the artifact read through the estate router bounded by the stall grace, `get_artifact` asked again; a subresource rides its document's verdict at most thirty seconds, forgotten the moment this node's applier reports the artifact moved. Anything that cannot be vouched for (a stale or unreachable copy, an identity copy past the grace, a copy older than the ticket, an undecided chart) is a 503 with `Retry-After`, never a page. A real origin only on a site of its own (config's site rule); the version's pinned runtime placed before every script of each HTML page (past its doctype, a bounded scan and never a parse), and the brand stylesheet the version pinned linked after it unless the page's `<html>` opts out; a strict script policy by a fresh nonce substituted per response into the linter's placeholder; every byte read through the object store's checking reader, ranges by length; documents and small gzips kept per node by digest; every write at the object store's pace. `artifactserve/ticket` mints and opens the tickets |
| `presence` | Who is in each artifact's room and where. TOLD, NEVER STORED: rosters in the memory of the node holding the sockets, crossed between nodes on the queue's `Broadcast`/`Listen` (no row, no log record, no coordination key; the hub's broker seam has no durable verb at all), every frame sealed ONE WAY under the keyring (`signedask.OneWay`, key domain `artifacts.presence`, a 60-second expiry inside the seal) because it names a person, so a frame the fleet did not seal shows nobody. A node listens on a room only while one of its SOCKETS is in it (one reconciler goroutine listens and withdraws), fans each room out to its sockets at most ten times a second and tells the fleet its own members' moves at most twenty times a second (one short window on each side, so a cursor crosses nodes within 150 ms), re-presents at most ten members a frame (the change waiting longest first, so nobody starves and a socket's bytes stay bounded), rebroadcasts its members in full every 15 s (a peer replaces what it held from that node, so a lost leave costs one rebroadcast), expires a peer's member unheard for 45 s, asks its peers once when it gains a room's first socket and applies each answer as it arrives (`AskEach`), and tells the fleet its members left when it stops. Large rooms present the fifty most recently active and count the rest by kind, back to everybody only below forty. An agent's tool call TOUCHES the room (present as an agent, viewing or editing, for 45 s after its last call) and never makes its node listen. Who may be in a room is the socket's decision, never the hub's |
| `provenance` | The ONE fence around text a model reads that somebody else wrote (page bodies, work comments, project files, chat threads, artifact files and rows, the adopted brand's voice, a repository's files, patches and commit messages — `repo_file`, `repo_patch` and `repo_commit`, the subject a history or a blame names each commit by, read at `external` since a read by object id names no writer — and a change's description, reviews and comments — `change_description`, `change_review` and `change_comment`, each under the writer the change's rows record, at the trust that writer earns, a description `TrustForPartial` once an edit may have rewritten it — and what a check of a commit said, `check_log`, its summary, tail, failure messages and log pages at `external` naming no writer, what the commit's code printed or a person's CI sent): a per-turn random boundary (minted per turn segment and carried on `turnctx.Turn`), an author label and a trust level (`company`, for what an administrator adopted for everybody, is never inferred from authors and governs tone and wording only), the envelope a whole read answer carries, `Unfence` for a write that sends the fence back, and the one standing sentence the seat prompt carries. Content cannot close a fence: the boundary is removed from it and every marker-shaped `[` is escaped. `agent/builtin`'s read tools answer through it (`fence.go`), which also defuses every other string in the answer. A leaf |
| `trashreap` | The trash reaper duty: purges what has been trashed past its term, as the system, for every domain that keeps a trash (pages first, then artifacts), under derived operation ids so a moved duty never purges twice |
| `objectives` | Service objectives as named constants with their reasons (publish success and latency, served first byte, browser-reported ticket-to-ready, datastore round trip and resolution, change visibility, wake latency, best-effort presence), each an EVENT objective over thirty days whose good event is answered without fault within its bound (`Objective.Good`), so an alarm borrows its threshold rather than inventing one (ADR-0015). The `Meter` every recording site calls (the publish pipeline, the artifact origin, the datastore writer) classifies once and counts twice from that one call: `crewlet.objectives.events{objective, outcome}` into the engine's recorder, and a minute ledger over six hours the multi-window burn rates are read from (1 h and 5 m at 14.4, 6 h and 30 m at 6, at least two bad events), which `objective_burn` raises. The `Beacon` admits the dashboard's ready reports (`POST /artifacts/ready`): one per frame, never longer than the frame has existed, a sustained one a second per credential |
| `api/artifactsapi`, `api/artifactdataapi` | The HTTP surfaces for artifacts and for their datastores on the API listener, every route stating its authority where it is mounted. A caller with no relation is answered exactly as an unknown id. Writes carry an Idempotency-Key (datastore writes too, never socket frames); a publish names its base version and a concurrent one is refused with the live version to merge onto. THE API ORIGIN NEVER RENDERS AN ARTIFACT: raw bytes here are attachments, a doc's source is JSON, and a page is opened only as a frame on the artifact origin. ONE ADMISSION (`artifactsapi.Gate`) for the artifacts' and the datastore's routes and read questions alike: it reads the head, decides the verb on its owner and shares before anything else is read, stamps the exact role into the write, and records an elevated read before the admin path's read is answered, refusing it when the record cannot be written |
| `api/datasetsapi` | A `dashboard` artifact's datasets, answered AS THEIR VIEWER (`adr/0043`): an engine dataset is the very question the dashboard's own screens ask, through the registry, on the viewer's own request (the agents' status is `GET /agents`' own decision), projected into the catalogue's columns; a store dataset reads under the collection's read rule; a calculation runs over inputs read the same way. A source the viewer may not read is a refusal of that dataset naming the grants that open it. A binding may name only a question decided on grants alone, refused at wiring otherwise, because answers are cached 30 s under a key of everything about the principal such a question reads. Live reads are charged to `costlimit` per viewer and per artifact |
| `artifactspike` | Test-only: the artifacts capacity spikes (datastore rate and footprint, sandbox streaming, the linter and strict policy over a committed page corpus, edits, presence fan-out, the object store under a publish) as reproducible benchmarks over the shipped components, whose figures `docs/reference/artifact-capacity.md` records and the limits and objectives are checked against. Benchmarks gate nothing; the corpus records are tests. Forms clusters, so it runs alone |
| `setup` | What an integration NEEDS before it works, in one vocabulary every vendor and the dashboard share. A vendor declares a list of requirements — where each value lives in the config, what it is called at the vendor, whether it is a credential, whether the engine can mint it, and which reconcile FINDING satisfying it clears — and the screen renders a form from that without knowing any vendor. It also owns the write ORDER, which is the whole of it: the credential is sealed first, then the config gets a `${VAR}` pointing at it, then the epoch advances. Pointer-first leaves a route refusing every delivery; value-only reaches no running seat at all, because a `${VAR}` resolves from a snapshot taken at apply time. It also runs a vendor's PASS — the same Reconcile the loop runs, with the same sink and the same public base, because CONNECTING IS THE PERMISSION and a timer running what a person already asked for is not a second decision. What it adds is `Hold`: the ONE guard every writer at a surface takes, an in-process claim AND the surface's fleet lease, because either alone leaves the collision reachable — a lease claim by an owner that already holds it doubles as a renew, so two goroutines in one process are both told yes. It also holds a credential's CHECK before it is stored (`verify.go`): a vendor's `Probe` is one read-only call that answers a `Verdict` (`works`, `refused`, `unreachable` or `unchecked`, a named string type with `Valid`) and writes nothing, served at `POST /setup/integrations/{kind}/verify` within `VerifyBudget`, and a requirement says whether it is `Checkable` or, as a webhook secret a vendor only signs with, how the vendor proves it (`CheckNote`). Every probe runs ONE suite, `setup/setuptest`, in the `queuetest` idiom, with a meta-test that hands it vendors which lie, because a probe that answered `works` for a refused key is the one failure a person cannot see |
| `jsprovision` | The ONE place that decides how long a replicated JetStream create gets, how often a forming cluster is re-asked, and what "still forming" looks like on the wire. Both subsystems that provision replicated objects at boot are the same call underneath — `queue/jetstream` makes the streams and consumers, `coord/kv` makes the buckets, and a bucket IS a stream — and written twice the rule drifted exactly as `textcut`'s and `whsec`'s did: five decisions spelled separately, each doc comment asserting it matched the other with nothing enforcing it. The budget BRANCHES on clustered, which is what the flat thirty seconds could not: a solo create is local file-store setup and a clustered one is a raft round trip against peers that are themselves booting, and the flat number was measured failing a fleet's boot on a different object every time — which is what says the object was never the problem. It also bounds the SEQUENCE, because a bring-up is many creates in a row and a per-create budget bounds only the term: without a ceiling the real limit was the product, which nobody had declared and raising the term would have multiplied. And it is where a stalled create gets its one BREADCRUMB — the step was otherwise silent, so a node that hung emitted nothing between the line before it and the failure a budget later, and the log could not say which of fifteen objects it was on. Its `Settle` owns the READ-BACK window rather than taking one, which is the only arrangement where both halves are bounded — a window the caller built separately was still running while a lookup blocked past it — and it is handed the context that bounds the BOOT, never the per-create one: two of these read-backs exist precisely BECAUSE a create's deadline expired, so a loop gated on that deadline was dead code on the paths it was written for. Every duration it waits on is ONE `Timing` — `Clustered.Timing` is the production value, a test runs `SettleWith`, `Ask`, `ReadWith`, `ReadBoundedWith` and `Place` at a scale it can afford, and a field left zero is refused by name — and it splits a request by what the broker does with it: a READ (does this stream, consumer or bucket exist) is answered when a worker takes it from the server's API queue or never, so `ReadWith` presumes a request lost after `ReadTerm` (a second) and asks again after `ReAsk` (another) WITH EVERY REQUEST STILL OUTSTANDING, the first answer from any of them taken and none outliving the call — because a request behind a backed-up queue is answered late rather than never, and thrown away at its term no read of a leader slower than a second was ever taken, a cliff rather than a slowdown — and so does every read only a stream's, a consumer's or the metadata group's LEADER answers — a domain log's (`DomainLog.Stats`, `LastSeq`, `At`: a domain log allows no direct get, and `GroupAckFloor`), `jsapi.Leader`'s message reads and subject pages, `jsapi.Meta`'s listings, the account's limits, coordination's stream infos, the object store's stream info, the memory changelog's and the backup's log lookups — because a member that still believes in a leader the cluster just lost stays silent, and one request held for its caller's whole context cost a committer its thirty-second budget across an election; and NO READ IS ASKED PAST `Lookup` (thirty seconds) whatever its caller set, the longest silence the server keeps — a nine-second election, a lost leader stepping down ten seconds after, at a check every ten — past which a group in any state answers, because a caller with no deadline (the trim tick, on whose loop the node's alarms are evaluated) was otherwise asked for ever; a read that was ONE request until it was hedged — all of those but the domain log's three — keeps that request's bound for a caller that set none instead (`ReadBoundedWith`), the client's own five-second default, asked again inside it at two and four seconds, because every such caller — a boot's steps, a duty's tick, a heartbeat — was sized against those five seconds, and a pod's startup window counts each read at them — and such a read now costs the server what its re-asks do, three requests in those five seconds where it sent one and up to fifteen under the ceiling, each processed, so a leader's queue that answers slower than two seconds meets more of them the slower it answers; the applier's pending count asks the broker nothing at all (`DomainConsumer.Pending`): every record a pull delivers carries the consumer's count of what follows it, so the count the applier asks on every run is the newest delivery's plus what the pull holds untaken, where a consumer info was a request to the consumer's leader on the commit path; a state read opens a stream handle of its own for every request, since the vendored handle caches its info with no lock and a hedged read may have two requests out, while a WRITE's reply is held until the object has a leader, so `Ask` waits `AskTerm` (fifteen seconds clustered) before it asks again, the one request abandoned since a second create beside it is a second write. `Refused` names the answered refusals that skip the read-back, and every create site — `coord/kv`'s buckets and the object store's included — asks it |
| `jsinflight` | How a CONDITIONAL publish to a replicated stream is answered while the leader still has another write to that subject in flight. The leader gives a compare-and-set THREE answers: it lands; it is refused on a comparison (10071, naming the revision the subject IS at); or it is refused WITHOUT one (10164, "wrong last sequence" and no number), because the leader marks a subject in flight when it proposes a conditional write and clears the mark only after it has applied it AND acknowledged it — so the write ahead may be the caller's own, already confirmed. The client maps both refusals to one revision mismatch and calls them equivalent, and both conditional writers here believed it: under load a removal at the very revision its caller had just read back was refused, leaving a create race over that record with no winner, and a charge spent its sixteen compare-and-set rounds on refusals no other writer caused. `Decide` retries the identical publish while the answer is in flight (1 ms doubling to 64 ms, jittered), within the publish's own deadline or the context's DefaultTimeout, and one still undecided at the end is `Undecided`, which wraps the context error and NEVER the refusal, so no caller reads it as a race somebody else won. One package because the rule is the server's and two writers publish conditionally — `coord/kv` to the buckets, `queue/jetstream` to the state logs — and written twice it would drift as `jsprovision`'s did; `statelog`'s appender, which reaches the log through the second, reads 10071 alone as a refusal, so 10164 is a compare-and-set answer nowhere in the tree. A LEAF over the JetStream client and `backoff` |
| `jsapi` | WHICH JETSTREAM API A CONNECTION SPEAKS, decided once. A member of the embedded fleet runs JetStream itself; a LEAF — a node without the `data` role — runs a broker with JetStream switched off and reaches the members' across its link; an external cluster is somebody else's. A leaf's broker answers nothing on `$JS.API.>`, and what crosses a leaf link is a DOMAIN, so every embedded member serves one fixed domain and every client of the fleet addresses it — a member's own clients too, whose server answers the domain's subjects as it answers the plain ones, so no caller ever has to know which kind of node it is on. Five subsystems build a JetStream client (the queue, both coordination stores, the memory changelog, the backup), which is five places to disagree; the zero `API` is REFUSED rather than read as either, because each reading is a node that boots and times out on first use. It also holds the ONE LEADER READ of a single stream message (`jsapi.Leader` — the newest, the oldest or the next from a sequence on a subject or a filter, answering `ErrNoMessage` only on the leader's word), which coord's certified KV listings and `objstore/natsobj`'s metadata reads, `Stat`, `Delete` and the check of where a consumer's replica ends share, and the memory changelog's read of where a seat's rows end, because a direct get can be answered "not found" by a replica that is behind and the client's own last-message read picks a direct get from the stream's flag alone — and ASKED AS A METADATA READ IS (`jsprovision.ReadBoundedWith`), never once: only the leader answers it, and a member that still believes in a leader its group has just lost takes the request and returns without a word, so one request held for its caller's deadline, or for the client's five seconds where there was none, failed the object collector's first pass on a loaded fleet of three; injected elections (a stepdown, a killed leader) left the first read on every other member never answered, and the one asked again at two seconds was answered by the new leader. A caller with no deadline keeps those five seconds, asked again inside them, since each was sized against them; and a read whose answer is a whole large message is presumed lost only once a reply that size has had time to cross a link at the floor (`Leader.Carrying`), or each request asked again puts another on a link the first is still crossing — `Crossing` is that one rule, the message base64-encoded in its JSON reply, and a state log's read of a record by its position across a link takes it too (`queue/jetstream`'s `DomainLog.At`, where for a log of megabyte records it outlasts the ceiling and the read is one request; the write path's `LastSeq` keeps the read term). The PAGED LISTINGS are its too — a stream's subjects under a filter (`Leader.Subjects`), the stream names and a stream's consumers (`Meta`) — EACH PAGE ITS OWN hedged read, never a whole listing, which a slow listing restarted from its first page every two seconds; a subjects page is given what the page before it took, since the leader sorts every subject under the filter for each page, and every page after the first is asked from one entry before where the last ended and asked again from further back unless it overlaps, so an entry removed meanwhile cannot carry another past both pages; a consumer whose own leader did not answer the metadata leader fails the listing (`UnreadError`) rather than vanish from it. `jsapitest` makes a broker with no domain drop or hold one leader read's API subject (a message, a stream's info, a consumer's info, the stream names, a consumer list, the account's info) through an account subject mapping, so a caller's test reaches the hedge through the real path rather than a seam a reader could build its own around |
| `jwks` | THE ONE CACHED READER of somebody else's published key set, and the one place that decides what this engine trusts one to say. The Forge relay a webhook arrives over verifies Atlassian's signed token against the keys Atlassian publishes, and what it needs from a key set is SIX decisions none of which is about Forge and none of which is locally obvious: the staleness budget, the unknown-kid refetch floor, the document ceiling, whether one unusable entry discards the rest, whether a stale key still verifies when the source is down, and whether the fetch happens UNDER THE LOCK. Kept apart from the relay, each is stated and tested once, against a key set rather than through a webhook, and the relay's own copy had already got the last of those wrong before it was extracted: it held its mutex across a ten-second network fetch, so a CDN having a bad minute serialised every delivery in the process behind one hung request. THE FETCH IS OFF THE LOCK and the window that opens — every caller deciding to fetch at once — is closed by a SINGLEFLIGHT, so one key rotation is one request however many tokens arrive during it, and every caller waits on ITS OWN context rather than being held to the leader's deadline — while the flight runs on NOBODY's, bounded by the fetch timeout: it ran on the first caller's, so one webhook sender giving up failed every verification waiting beside it. A failed flight is CLEARED rather than latched, because one bad minute at a provider must not refuse every token until something restarts. An unknown `kid` refetches at most once a minute, because a kid nobody published is what a forged token carries and treating it as staleness makes verification an amplifier an unauthenticated caller aims at whoever hosts the keys — and that minute is counted from the last ATTEMPT, a failed one included, because counted from the last success an outage made every forged kid a fetch again once the set aged past it; inside it a STALE key the set names is served without asking a source that just failed, and only a COLD cache, which has nothing to answer from, asks again at once, one flight at a time. It reads RSA keys and nothing else, because the relay pins RS256 and a key nothing should verify with is a key nothing should hold, and skips every other type (`EC`, `OKP`, `oct`), an `oct` key above all, since a symmetric key read out of a published set is a signing secret anybody can download. And a key the issuer marked `use: enc` is skipped while one with no `use` at all is kept: the field is optional, so requiring it would discard every set that omits it, and honouring it is refusing to check a signature against a key its own publisher says cannot have made one |
| `logging`, `secrets`, `provision`, `envref`, `envfile`, `redact`, `workkey`, `backoff`, `cron`, `period` | The small shared grammars. Each imports nothing from the rest of the engine but others in this row — `secrets` reads `envref` and `redact`, and `provision` reads `envfile`, `envref` and `secrets` — which is what lets `config` itself depend on them — and what let the org model validate a schedule's cron with the parser the scheduler evaluates it with, where it had counted the fields because the parser sat in `schedule`, above it. `secrets` is also where WHICH FIELDS HOLD A CREDENTIAL is decided, once: a `secret:"true"` tag on the field — `secret:"content"` for a BODY, a setup step's file, read whole at launch through `secrets.ReadContent` — read through `secrets.Field` and shown by `secrets.Mask`, because a tag is maintained by declaring it where a list of paths is maintained by whoever remembers it exists. `period` is the company CALENDAR — the day, the ISO week and the month a moment falls in on the company's clock — and a window's identity is its LABEL (`2026-09-23`, `2026-W39`, `2026-09`) rather than an instant, because a label is what every node computes alike, what a counter rolls over by comparing, and what sorts as a string. A day begins at the FIRST instant of its date, which `time.Date` alone gets wrong in the zones that move their clocks across midnight (Santiago resolves to the evening before, Amman to the second of two midnights); the tracker's relative dates are cut by it too, so there is one calendar rather than a copy |
| `gitref` | THE GRAMMAR OF GIT NAMES the engine and its configuration share — repository names, refs, branch names, protection patterns and object ids, with their bounds, and the seats' branch namespace (`SeatsRoot`, and `ReservedSeatBranch`: `seats` and `seats/<one name>` as a branch, which no push creates and no repository takes as its default branch) — standard library only, because config validates a document with it, the code domain decides a push with it, and the domain imports config, so the grammar lives below both (ADR-0008). `ValidBranch` is the sandbox's grammar too: the branch a parked coding run records for its re-seed, and each one a run's report says it delivered, is judged by it rather than by a copy — so it is valid UTF-8 (the run's row is JSON, where any other byte arrives as another branch's name) and at most `MaxBranchBytes` (244, what a ref's 255 leaves after `refs/heads/`). Git's rules are the FLOOR and real git is the ORACLE: `ValidRef` is `git check-ref-format` in Go and `MatchPattern` is `git for-each-ref`'s glob over whole components — `*` one component, `**` zero or more, and one or more as a pattern's LAST component, which is how git's wildmatch reads a trailing `/**`, so `release/**` does not protect `release` — and every row of both test tables records git's verdict beside this package's, re-measured through `gittest` on every run, with a reason exactly where the two differ, so a rule transcribed wrong and a git that changed its mind both fail.
