A rule asked about many refs is PARSED ONCE (`ParsePattern`, `Pattern.Match`), because the decide asks every rule about every command on every compare-and-set round: the pattern's checking and cutting belong to the rule, and a ref allocates nothing. `PatternReachesSeats` says whether a pattern matches some ref of a seat's namespace (`refs/heads/seats/<handle>/…`), EXACTLY rather than by prefix — `refs/heads/**` and `refs/heads/*/*/*` reach it, `refs/heads/*` does not — because a push mirror's refs (the code domain's `MirrorTarget`, `code.repos` `mirror_to`) are refused there, and the rule is held to `MatchPattern` over every pattern and seat ref of up to four components (`TestAPatternReachesASeatsNamespaceExactlyWhereItMatchesARefThere`). Nothing can absorb a component before a pattern's first `**` or after its last, so those parts are compared ONCE each at the ref's two ends — a rule with at most one `**` costs a comparison a part however deep the ref (`**/hotfix` costs the deepest ref 37 ns) — and only a run of parts BETWEEN two `**`s is searched for along the ref. That search is the one cost a pattern can make large, so it is bounded BY THE GRAMMAR rather than by whatever a founder writes: a glob has at most `MaxPatternComponents` (8) components, which holds the costliest rule a ref can meet to 13 µs, where its bytes alone let one cost 85 µs; a literal is one comparison and bounded only as a ref is. The walk — written for speed — is held to its rule written out plainly on every shape a short pattern and ref can take. Where it is STRICTER than git it says so at the rule: a whole ref is at most 255 bytes (`MaxRefNameBytes`), valid UTF-8 (a ref crosses JSON, where invalid bytes are replaced and two refs arrive as one) and `refs/<namespace>/<name>`; a branch is never `@` (HEAD in every revision expression); and a LITERAL protection pattern matches only the identical ref, never for-each-ref's prefix up to a `/`, because a protection on `main` must not also protect `main/x`. A PROJECT repository's name is ONE host-name label (`ValidProjectName`, `MaxRepoNameBytes` 63, no hyphen at either end) and a SCRATCH repository's its seat's handle, a dot and one label (`ScratchName`, the whole at most 63 bytes, a DNS-1123 subdomain, the slug never `git`, which a client would strip as the URL's `.git` suffix), so whatever a deployment names after a repository takes the name unchanged; `ValidRepoName` takes either, and a name says its kind and its owner — the code domain's `RepoKind` follows the name. And the GLOB OVER A REPOSITORY'S TREE PATHS (`ParsePathGlob`, `PathGlob.Match`), which a path owner names its part of a tree by: `Pattern`'s rule over a path, read by the ONE reading of a glob a pattern takes (`readGlob`, the trailing `**` as one component or more), held to git's own `:(glob)` pathspec over a real tree, to the rule written out plainly and to `Pattern.Match` itself on every short shape — and stricter than git where a pattern is: a literal glob matches only its path, never everything below a directory of that name, and `*` is its one metacharacter, `?`, `[`, `\` and a leading `!` refused rather than read as themselves. A path, unlike a ref, has NO BOUND, so its walk is not a pattern's: it reads each component's end as it reaches it rather than cutting the middle onto the stack once, compares the parts before the first `**` and after the last once each as a pattern's does, and allocates nothing. A glob is at most `MaxPathGlobBytes` (128, past any glob a person writes — a bound on ONE glob: a repository's 2 048 at their longest JSON spelling would weigh 1.6 MB, so what a repository's path owners weigh is bounded as a whole, by config's `CodeMaxPathOwnersBytes`) and, holding a `*`, `MaxPathGlobComponents` (8) components — a pattern's bound, which holds the run between two `**`s to seven parts and so a glob's cost a byte of path, never a match's: a path has no bound, and the costliest glob found costs 3.2 ms on the 48 KiB a revision's touched paths may take (`BenchmarkPathGlobMatch`), so a home matching a repository's 2 048 globs, every one of that shape, pays about 6.5 s of a core a revision, and some 42 ms for as many directories' `**`. And GIT'S UTF-8 FOR A COMMIT'S TEXT (`CommitText`): any valid UTF-8 but Unicode's 66 noncharacters, which git's `has_invalid_utf8` refuses and `ensure_utf8` then rewrites as Latin-1 while exiting 0 — ONE reading below both of its readers, `code/gitproc`, which holds a commit-tree's identity to it before any process starts, and the code domain, which holds an engine write's message to it as it validates the write, so no text is refused in one place and rewritten in history by the other. And THE URL OF A REMOTE REPOSITORY (`ParseRemoteURL`), which an import or a mirror dials: https only, read one way and written back that way — a lowercase DNS name whose last label begins with a letter or an address in its one written form, the default port left out, and no user, query or fragment, nothing another parser reads as another host — ONE reading below its three readers: `code/gitproc`, which hands git only the URL it wrote back, and the code domain and the configuration, which hold a recorded mirror or import to it, so `crewlet validate` refuses what the dial would |
| `regexlit`, `regexlit/regexlittest` | WHAT A REGULAR EXPRESSION'S MATCHES CANNOT DO WITHOUT, read off the expression's own syntax tree, for every scan that skips a text the expression provably does not match. `Required` derives a `Prefilter` — CLAUSES, each a set of substrings every match contains one of — so a text failing a clause is skipped without running the expression, and the verdict is the same set of matches. Go's regexp jumps only to a literal PREFIX and a gate's pattern almost never has one (`\bADR-`, `(?m)^func`, `(?i)…`, an alternation), so the matcher stepped through every byte, at about a megabyte a second under the race detector, where a substring search is SIMD. `LineLocal` says when the expression may then run on the admitted lines alone. `Terms` hands each clause over with how each substring is compared, for a caller that looks them up in an index of what each text holds rather than in a text, and so makes `Admits`'s comparison itself. Folded literals are compared on the text's Unicode LOWERING, not an ASCII-only one, which misses `(?i)` across É and é; a rune the regexp folds onto an ASCII letter without lowering to it is an ESCAPE (`HasEscape`: DERIVED from the Unicode tables, U+017F alone today, while the Kelvin sign lowers to `k` and needs nothing) that admits a text outright whenever a clause compares folded; and a rune whose orbit lowers inconsistently (σ, ς, Σ) cuts a literal. The derivation may admit too much and never too little, which its suite certifies with matches GENERATED from each pattern's own syntax tree and by exhaustion over every rune. ONE derivation rather than a literal list beside each pattern, because a hand list drifts in the unsafe direction: the withdrawn-vocabulary gate's lowered a line with `strings.ToLower` and passed a withdrawn name spelled with a long s. A package of its own, moved out of `sourcetree`, because the code host's code search plans a pattern from the same substrings and production code does not import `sourcetree`, which imports `testing` and finds a source tree that does not exist where the engine runs — and a second derivation written for that caller is the copy ADR-0008 exists to prevent. `regexlittest` is the generator a suite certifying such a derivation draws on: expressions from the caller's own vocabulary, and strings an expression MATCHES, written from its own syntax tree — a member of a folded rune's case orbit, a class's rune or a byte that is not UTF-8, a branch, a count — because random text almost never matches a pattern, so a property checked over it holds vacuously. ONE generator, lifted out of this package's suite and shared with `codeindex`'s, because a copy that stopped writing the long s would leave its suite passing without meeting the case it exists for |
| `skipgate` | THE ENFORCEMENT BEHIND "a skip is not a pass", which was a convention held by memory and had already decayed twice. It reads `go test -json` on both test pipelines, renders it back so a CI log is unchanged, and fails on a skip nothing declared — plus it carries the run's own verdict, because in a pipeline `go test`'s exit status is gone by the time make looks. The allowlist is keyed on the test's own NAME with a reason per entry rather than being a count, because a count goes green when one skip is fixed and another appears. Two-sided where it can be: an `Always` entry that stopped firing is stale and fails, judged by its TEST and never by its package alone — its test reported a result that was not a skip, or a passing run of every test of its package never ran it (renamed or deleted; a package that failed may have died before it, or under it, which leaves a test that started and never reported, so a `run` record alone judges nothing) — because each half of the partition covers half the packages and a `-run` covers part of one, and judging by the package failed every targeted run of a package that declares an entry. Whether a run is narrowed is read from the command it runs (`-run`/`-skip`, and GOFLAGS): the stream names the tests that ran, never the ones a filter left out. `-timings FILE` writes how long each passing package took (the partition's weights) — read from the package-level records it already judges, and written whatever the verdict; a failed package's time is left out because it stops wherever the failure stopped it |
| `solo` | WHICH TEST PACKAGES NEED THE RUNNER TO THEMSELVES, as a declaration rather than a list. A package imports it from `TestMain` and `internal/solo/partition` derives both halves of the suite from that, so `make test` / `make test-solo` / ci.yml share ONE computed answer instead of three hand-maintained copies of a `grep -v`. Its roster guard is what makes the declaration self-maintaining, and it fails in BOTH directions — a package standing up a multi-member broker without declaring (the flake) and a package declaring without needing to (the flake-suppressant). The predicate is exact rather than approximate: the cluster harness's ENTIRE exported surface is four multi-member constructors, so importing it IS forming a quorum. The package doc is also where every alternative's measurement lives — a build tag hides files from `go vet` and golangci-lint and can drop a package from `go list` entirely, `-short` skips nothing on its own, a custom flag kills every test binary that does not define it, `-skip` leaves `[no tests to run]` and exit 0 — and every one of them fails SILENTLY, which is why this is an import and not a tag. `partition` also ORDERS the parallel half from measured seconds (`-weights`): longest first, because `go test` starts package runs strictly in command-line order and alphabetical order started the three longest packages last; the solo half runs at `-p 1`, where no order ends it sooner. Weights decide order and NEVER membership — an unmeasured package weighs its half's median |
| `e2e` | The gates only a whole running node can answer: a real engine on a real embedded stream, the real API in front of it, a real turn driven by a scripted model, and the dashboard's OWN modules consuming the frames that come out. Nothing is stubbed but the vendor endpoint, because what it exists to catch is a misunderstanding BETWEEN two components that each implement consistently with their own tests — an event shape the projection does not key on, a slice the client does not read — which no suite of one component can see. It has no exported surface, and it forms clusters, so it runs in `make test-solo`. It holds the one piece of code in `internal/` that is not Go: `replay.mjs`, the CLIENT HALF of `golden_test.go`'s wire-protocol gate, which pushes the frames a real company's socket sent, and one `/operator/act` answer, through the committed `static/dashboard/protocol.js` under plain `node`, and fails on anything the client cannot read. It sits beside the test that runs it because the two are one test that changes together: not under `dashboard/`, since it is no test of the dashboard (its Vitest suites are), and not in a top-level `tests/` tree, where it outlived the old dashboard's own suites it had been written beside |
| `docsgate` | The tree's prose held to the files it points at: every relative markdown link names a file that exists, every `#anchor` names a heading its target renders under GitHub's slug, and every page under `docs/` is in `docs/index.md` — the docs site's navigation. It reads this tree through `sourcetree.Walk`, so a nested checkout's stale copy of a page is never judged as this one. TEST-ONLY, and scoped to markdown on purpose: a dashboard address (`#/work/ENG-42`) is a ROUTE, which only the dashboard's resolver can evaluate, so `dashboard/src/app/links.test.ts` holds those — and every pointer at a `dashboard-design.md` heading, which lives mostly in code — rather than a second copy of the route table here. A link is never followed across the network: whether somebody else's page exists is not a property of this tree |
| `docslinks` | Test-only, like `docsgate`, and it certifies the links INTO the documentation site rather than between files: every address on the docs site that this repository carries — in the docs and the skill it publishes, the dashboard a person clicks through, the schemas an editor resolves, the code and the process files — names a page the site builds under its product and version segment, because the site forwards no path it used to serve and a link outside that shape is a 404 however it once resolved. It lives under `internal/` because the site copies `schema/` and `skills/` verbatim and serves them, so beside the skill it guarded it was itself published |
| `clientsource` | Finds a declaration the DASHBOARD makes, so a gate can hold it against the engine's own. Several lists exist twice by necessity — the dashboard is a separate build in a separate language and cannot import a Go identifier — and each copy has drifted at least once, silently: a chip for a category no event carries filters a log down to nothing and reads as a quiet engine. The check belongs on the engine side because the engine owns the value. KEYED ON THE DECLARATION, NEVER A PATH, so a screen moving is invisible and the only two failures it reports are the ones that matter — nothing declares it, or two declarations do, in one file or in two. KEYED ON THE SYNTAX, NEVER THE LAYOUT: the source is SCANNED — strings, comments, template literals, regular expressions and JSX prose each recognised for what they are — and `Literal`, `Scalar`, `Union`, `Interface`, `Calls` and `Property` (one row of a table keyed by name) read tokens, because every regular expression they replaced was a claim about how prettier lays a declaration out, and one that stops matching does not fail, it matches LESS. What is not a literal (a spread, a computed key, a union member that is not a string, an expression where one value was expected) is refused by name rather than skipped. ONE REGISTRY, BOTH WAYS: `Contract()` names every declaration a gate reads, its reader and its ONE owning gate; a reader refuses a name the table does not carry, and `contract_test.go` refuses a row that does not resolve to exactly one declaration or whose gate does not read it. ONE HOME: every row is declared in `dashboard/src/contract/` and every name that directory exports is a row, held from both sides — the contract test here, and the dashboard's own `contract.test.ts`, which keeps each module there PURE (siblings-only imports, no behaviour, every export read). `Tree(t)` is where the dashboard's source IS, anchored at the module root (`sourcetree.Root`), because a relative `../../dashboard/src` is right from exactly one depth — every gate one level deeper prepended another `..`, and three packages kept a copy of the path to avoid it — and its one walk is `sourcetree.Walk`, so a nested checkout's copy of a screen is not a second declaration. A normal package rather than a test file because the gates of every package that owns a declaration read it — engine packages under `internal/`, and `cmd/crewlet` for a declaration the CLI's own commands decide (the terminal steps a screen hands an operator, which only package main's tests can run) |
| `sourcetree` | What is and is not THIS REPOSITORY'S TREE, for everything that reads its own files: the module root (`Root`) and a walk (`Walk`) that never enters VCS metadata, a NESTED CHECKOUT or a `node_modules`. `Root` is where every path a test takes out of its own package starts — a docs page, the examples, the schemas, the dashboard build, the directory `go list ./...` runs in — never a counted `../..`, which is a depth every copy had to get right for itself and one did not. The middle one is why it exists: this repository is developed with `isolation: worktree`, which puts full checkouts of it under `.claude/worktrees/`, and of twenty-one walks, each with its own idea of the tree, the only one that stepped over them did so by skipping every dot-directory — a name, not a rule — so the rest read those copies as this tree's, so the withdrawn-vocabulary gate failed on a clean tree by finding its own list in a nested copy, and a presence check passes on a stale one. A nested checkout is recognised by STRUCTURE — any directory below the start holding a `.git` entry of its own, file or directory, whether or not it resolves to a repository — never by the name `.claude`, which would miss a clone dropped anywhere else. That is DELIBERATELY BROADER than git's embedded-repository rule, which walks into a dangling gitfile or an empty `.git` and would stage what it finds there: a pruned worktree is still a copy of this tree at another commit, and broader is the safe direction for a guard, because git never tracks a `.git` path, so a fresh checkout — CI's — holds none and the walk there reads every committed file. Every other skip (static/, dist/, schema/, testdata) stays the guard's own decision, and reaches only what lies BELOW the start: `Walk` never hands its start to the callback, because the start's name is wherever the checkout lives, and a checkout at `../dist` was skipped whole by every gate that steps over dist/ — and each of those asserting an absence without counting what it read passed having read nothing. A start that is not a directory is an error for the same reason, since `filepath.WalkDir` does not follow a symbolic link at its start, and `Root` resolves its links so a checkout reached through one is still read. `gofiles` is the same rule for the formatter: `gofmt -l .` enters every nested checkout, so `make fmt` rewrote another worktree's files. The disk rather than `git ls-files`, because the compiler reads untracked files and a guard over the index passes a violation until the commit that tracks it. It is also where a gate learns which Go source it may SKIP without parsing: `Identifiers` skips a file spelling none of the identifiers a syntax-tree matcher compares — exact because an identifier has no escapes, and refused for anything that is not one. What a regular expression lets a gate skip is `regexlit`'s, not this package's: it moved out so that production code can read the same derivation, and production code does not import this package, which imports `testing`. `ModuleRoot` is `Root` for a value several gates of one binary load once |
| `sourcetree/decltype` | THE ONE WALK THAT TYPES A VALUE FROM THE DECLARATIONS IT REACHES, for every static gate that judges a value by what it IS rather than by its name — `statelog`'s stream-identity gate, `api/paced`'s pacing gate, `code/cache`'s writers gate, `api/auth`'s one-classifier gate, `code/host`'s one-landing gate, `engine`'s alarm-reading producer gate and `code`'s read gate (a `*sql.Tx`'s, `*sql.DB`'s or `*sql.Conn`'s `ExecContext` takes a statement's text where a `*sql.Stmt`'s takes its arguments: one name, told apart by the declaration) — read from source with no type checker, because a type checker types every package the judged ones import (41 s cold for `cmd/crewlet`). It reads this module and any package a caller `Locate`s through `go list` (the NATS packages from the module cache; `net/http`, `net`, `net/url`, `sync/atomic` and `database/sql` from the toolchain), each the files this platform builds, and types locals, package values, fields and methods promoted through embedding at the SHALLOWEST depth as the language resolves a selector, method expressions (`T.M`, `(*T).M`, `pkg.T.M`) as the method value `T` declares wherever `T` is declared — another file of the package and another package included, since a type named where a value goes is that type — call results, conversions, composite literals, elements and ranges, following generics through instantiation, explicit arguments and inference. THREE ANSWERS, NEVER TWO: a `Type` is known, `Unread` (only a package it does not read could type it, and that package is named) or unknown, and a `Selection` is a field or a method with the declared type that holds it, `Beyond` (a part it does not read may hold it), `FromUnread` (the receiver is itself a value only a package it does not read could type), `Absent` or `Undetermined` — a member found DEEPER than a part it could not read included, since that part may shadow it — and what each answer MEANS is the gate's: the stream-identity gate reports an unread value, the pacing gate, which reads `net/http`, passes one. `ImportName` is the one rule for an unnamed import's name (a major version, a gopkg.in `.vN`, `.go`, `go-`), which the rendezvous and gitproc gates read too. Lifted when the second gate's private walker had already drifted from the first's — one could not name `nats.go`, the other `yaml.v3`, and only one followed generics (ADR-0008); a normal package rather than a test file because seven gates' tests read it |
| `conflictmarkers` | A test-only package — what it certifies is content rather than code, as `internal/docslinks` does for the docs' links — that FAILS THE BUILD on a file in this tree still carrying a merge conflict's markers. A Go file with one fails to compile, the one place a marker is loud; everywhere else it is silent — a docs page renders it as prose and the site publishes it, a YAML or SQL comment swallows it, the committed dashboard bundle ships it — and a long branch resolves the same page one commit at a time, each commit permanent history. It reads the DISK through `sourcetree.Walk` rather than the index, because the `.orig` copy a merge tool leaves beside a resolved file carries the conflict whole and is one `git add -A` from a commit, and it fails a walk that read under five hundred text files, since a root resolved to the wrong place passes with no offence found. The seven-`=` divider is refused inside a conflict and everywhere outside Markdown, and allowed in Markdown directly under a line of text, where it is a setext heading — a stated gap rather than a hidden one: a divider left behind with both of its markers deleted reads there exactly like a heading |
| `adr` | The gate behind `adr/`: every record carries its fields under a number no other record holds, names an Authority that exists and mentions it, and an Enforced-by gate that exists (or the literal `nothing`, saying why); every citation of a record names one that exists, and the index names every record. It runs inside `make check` because the register it replaced, `docs/reference/design-decisions.md`, was referred to by nothing and drifted until it described an engine that no longer existed |
| `version` | What the binary calls itself: the tag goreleaser stamps at link time (for the image of `main`, `main-` and twelve hex digits of the commit, stamped the same way), falling back to the module's own build info and then to `dev`, so a binary built outside the release path names itself honestly rather than claiming to be a release it is not. `IsDevelopment` is the one PREDICATE over that, and it is an ALLOWLIST of the two unversioned spellings (`dev`, `(devel)`) rather than a test for a release shape — its caller is the development principal, which authenticates every request with no credential, so a spelling this build does not recognise, `main-<commit>` among them, must refuse the bypass rather than enable it. Split as `IsDevelopmentFor` for `resolve`'s reason: the process's own version is a `sync.OnceValue` over link-time state, so no test can put a release in front of the predicate that decides whether a bypass exists — and that predicate is certified beside its caller, in `internal/api/auth`'s development-principal suite. The package's own tests cover the package and nothing else now: four cases over the stamp/build-info/`dev` fallback. They used to be ten times its code and to assert the RELEASE SURFACE instead — the ldflags stamp, the pure-Go build, the image's `${TARGETPLATFORM}` copy, the catch-all notes category, the pre-release flags, the single tag trigger and every Dependabot entry — because each of those fails silently and has no other symptom |
